PREFACE 

The continent of Asia has been the stage upon which many of the most dramatic events of the twentieth century have taken place. There the world's oldest existing civilization is yielding to the customs of the Occident. The torch of a culture which once illuminated Europe, and passed in turn to the New World, is completing its circuit of the globe. 

In the course of more than twenty centuries the age of Euclid has developed into the age of Edison. Machines and other modern inventions have gained a foothold in the Orient, and are gradually transforming it. 

The industrial changes which are taking place in Asia are no greater than those which are modifying its political life, while nowhere has the social advancement which is taking place in the East been better exemplified than in Australia and New Zealand. 

In this volume, the fundamental geographical facts needed to understand and appreciate the life of the people of Asia and Australia are so interpreted as to bring them within the comprehension of elementary school children. This is done not by a superficial treatment of the subject matter, nor by leaving out essential facts, but through the vivid and detailed descriptions in which children delight, through the consideration of geographical controls, and by clear and accurate explanations of their effects. 

As in the other books of this series, the social point of view is paramount. Inanimate nature as well as the flora and fauna of the earth have their influence upon the life and customs of the human race, and are therefore of keen interest to those who would understand themselves and their fellow men. These influences are varied. Every country cherishes

vi 

PREFACE 

the places associated with its history, literature, and art; beautiful and majestic scenery attracts travelers to the most remote regions; natural forces impose conditions upon man which affect his welfare and to which he must adjust himself. But, however potent these aspects of the earth may be, there is probably nothing in the world of greater interest than the people who inhabit it. A knowledge of their habits and customs, their homes and other buildings, their occupations, and their dealings with people of other lands is needed by all who would understand and enjoy the newspaper or magazine, the story book, biography, history, or volume of travel.

By an effective pedagogical device to which maps, text and exercises contribute, the pupils are enabled to comprehend the relation between geographical regions and political divisions. The interest of pupils is enlisted through a varied program of activities in which they may coöperate according to their capacity and inclination. These activities are designed to fix the habits of intelligent study. They will also insure the permanent visualization of the earth, coupled with accurate location of places referred to in gen eral and current literature.

ASIA AND AUSTRALIA THE EASTERN HEMISPHERE 

The Eastern Hemisphere is the half of the globe (hemi means half) which includes Europe, Asia, Africa, Australia, and part of Antarctica. It is separated from the other half of the earth, the Western Hemisphere, by two meridians. directly opposite each other, 20 degrees west longitude and 

160 degrees east longitude. These two meridians together form a circle which passes through the North and South poles. The meridian of 160 degrees east longitude crosses the northeastern corner of Asia, so that a very small part of 

Asia is in the Western Hemisphere. 

There is more than twice as much land in the Eastern Hemisphere as in the Western Hemisphere. Most of it is divided between Europe, Asia, Australia, and Africa.

The Pacific Islands between Asia and Australia are parts of one or the other of those continents. The islands of the 

Pacific Ocean do not, however, belong to any continent. 

Inclosed on three sides by Africa, Asia, and Australia, the Indian Ocean lies wholly within the Eastern Hemisphere. 

Each of the four other oceans lies partly in both hemispheres. 

Europe is merely an enormous peninsula attached to the west side of Asia. In the remote past, however, these two continents were separated, for the waters of the Arctic Ocean. joined those of the Mediterranean Sea. It has long been customary to regard Europe and Asia as separate continents, although they form one continuous land mass. Europe has played a commanding part in both ancient and modern history. From it as a center of culture, the New World 1

The science and study of geography had its origin in Europe. Asia, on the other hand, is even now but slowly reaching out to a knowledge of the other continents.

In this volume we shall study Asia, the largest continent, and Australia, the smallest, as well as many islands of the Pacific Ocean. If we were in an airplane, and had to make a forced landing in some unknown part of those lands, what food, shelter, and other necessities of life could we secure?

What people would we meet? The character of a region and its inhabitants depends largely upon such geographical controls as the character of the land, its surface, soil, and waters, its climate, its timber, and other resources, including its wild creatures in fins, furs, and feathers.

Let us make a mental exploration of the Eastern Hemisphere, except Africa and Antarctica, to see how the life of the people we would find there differs from our own, or resembles it, as the result of the geographical controls. We

ASIA 

shall visit the region of Siberia of the long winter cold, China, with its many ancient customs the reverse of our own, progressive Japan, India, home of a sun-bronzed white race, steamy Indo-China, and the East Indies, and the deserts from Palestine to Persia and beyond. We shall end this tour in picturesque New Zealand, and Australia, home of the kangaroo, where wild bushmen have all but given place to thrifty English pioneers.

1. How many degrees of west longitude are there in the 

Eastern Hemisphere? How many degrees of east longitude? 

2. Which continents are in the Eastern Hemisphere? 

3. In which hemisphere is Northeastern Asia? 

4. Which of the continents of the Eastern Hemisphere are wholly in the Northern Hemisphere? Which is partly in the 

Northern and partly in the Southern Hemisphere? 

5. Between which continents are the largest Pacific 

Islands? In which hemisphere are the most eastern islands of the Pacific Ocean?

Map Study 

Sketch a large outline map of the Eastern Hemisphere. 

Show the polar circles, the tropics, the Equator, and the prime meridian. Mark the names of the oceans and the continents. Locate the Pacific Islands. 

Name the continents of the Eastern Hemisphere in their order of size. Name the oceans. 

The Old World. In 1492 A.D., when Columbus sailed to the Western Hemisphere and discovered America, the history of the people of the Eastern Hemisphere already dated back for many centuries. In contrast to the New 

World which Columbus found, the countries of the Eastern Hemisphere are therefore commonly called the Old World.

THE OLD WORLD 

5 Some of the oldest cities of the world, Babylon among the number, are in Asia. On the two continents of Asia and 

Africa we find the earliest records of the human race. 

Thousands of years before the Christian era, some of the people of Asia were civilized. They built great cities with splendid palaces and temples. They made beautiful statues, jewelry, and other works of art. From the records 

The ruins of ancient Babylon. Notice the ornaments upon the brick walls. they left on stone, clay, and parchment, we know that they studied medicine, astronomy, and other branches of learning. 

It is probable that Europe was settled by wandering tribes who fled from Asia to escape from famine, or from those who had defeated them in war.

A Maker of World History. In 336 B.C., at a time when 

Greek civilization made much of its arts, science, literature, 'philosophy, and good government, a fine-looking young Greek twenty years of age became king of his country, and

ASIA 

started the career that gave him the title of Alexander the Great. Crossing the Hellespont and Asia Minor, that rugged peninsula between the Mediterranean, the Aegean, and the Black seas, he conquered Persia. It is recorded that he built a causeway into the sea that may still be seen in order to reach and conquer the ancient city of Tyre. 

At this time there was real danger that the civilization of the East would come to dominate that of the West; but 

Alexander took his army to Persia a second time, and was victorious in one of the greatest battles of history. Alexander next led his army beyond the Hindu Kush into western India, which he also conquered. He then explored to the southwest till he discovered the Indian Ocean. He was now the ruler of most of the civilized world of that day. In preserving Greek (western) culture, he prepared the way for the Christian teachings that were to follow. 

Two European explorers, Vasco da Gama and Marco Polo, discovered other routes to the Orient than the one followed by Alexander the Great. One of these discoverers was a mere boy who lived in Venice. Here is his story. 

Asia's First Geographer. At the time Marco Polo began his adventures in 1271 A.D., he was but seventeen years of age. At that time the empire of Kublai Khan, the great 

Khan or ruler of Cathay (China), was monarch of much of Asia and much of the eastern part of Europe. Marco started in the company of his father and uncle, who were jewelers. Marco and his kinsmen saw many wonders along the way, Mt. Ararat, and "a fountain from which oil springs in great abundance" in an area where petroleum wells have been discovered. Crossing Persia, and journeying to the north of the Himalaya Mountains, the three Venetians traversed an arid desert waste that the Chinese call the Great Sea of Sand, which we know as The Gobi. They saw choking sandstorms and weird mirages, or mirrorlike images seemingly miles nearer than they really were, and

MARCO POLO 

7 usually upside down. Later explorers verified every detail of his strange story. 

When, after four years of the toilsome and hazardous travel of those days, Marco reached the Khan's palace at 

Peiping (Peking), he found a monarch incredibly rich. The dome of the palace was plated with gold, there were 10,000 milk-white horses at the summer palace, and 5000 elephants laden with treasure marched in the Chinese New Year's parade.

Kublai Khan took such a liking to Marco Polo that he made it possible for the boy to explore other parts of Asia, and not till Marco was a bearded man of forty did he finally return to his home in Venice. Then he came with a fleet of sailing vessels, through seas infested with pirates. He concealed his jewels, which amounted to a fortune, by wearing them sewed in the seams of his clothing. 

Polo had blazed a trail across unknown Asia, and he had seen many of the strange things with which this book is to deal. But at the time no one believed the tales he told. 

His reminiscences, The Book of Marco Polo, were not published for several hundred years. They finally appeared about half a century before the discovery of America by Columbus.

The Orient. Asia and northern Africa are also called the 

Orient, from a Latin word meaning east. We in the Occident (west) are familiar with Oriental rugs and other, products of the Orient. Christopher Columbus was seeking a new route to the Orient when he found his way barred by America. 

The Orient is still a realm of mystery to us, because the people are so different from us in race, religion, and manners. 

Their leisurely life contrasts sharply with our businesslike ways. Their daily habits are the same today as centuries ago. Men as well as women wear bright-colored costumes, sometimes richly ornamented, and persons of wealth indulge in luxuries unknown to even the millionaires of the Occident.

ASIA 

The scenes of the Biblical narratives and many other stories are laid in the Orient. The rivers, mountains, and cities named add reality to these stories and share in their charm. Even such tales as the Arabian Nights seem more real, when one has located the city of Baghdad on the map. 

Making swords of Damascus steel in Damascus, Syria, a city of the Orient, the oldest inhabited city in the world. 

We obtain some misleading ideas, however, from stories, poems, pictures, and the theater. These sources of information are likely to veil Oriental life in a haze of enchantment which hides the poverty, ignorance, and lack of cleanliness that are only too common in most Eastern countries,

PART I. ASIA AS A WHOLE 

CHAPTER I THE LARGEST CONTINENT GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS The 

Location. We have seen that all of Asia west of the northeastern tip is located in the Eastern Hemisphere. mainland of Asia lies wholly north of the Equator. Some of the islands between the continents of Asia and Australia, however, are considered part of Asia. Europe and 

North America also lie wholly in the Northern Hemisphere, as does the larger half of Africa and the northern part of South America. But Asia extends farther north than either Europe or America.

As the globe will show you, Asia lies east of Europe, though in one land mass with it. That is why certain parts of Asia are known as the Near East and others as the Far East.

Yet Asia is west of the United States (including Alaska), north of Australia, northeast of Africa, and lies farther north than most of South America.

The continents of Asia and Europe together form one body of land called Eurasia. The boundaries between Eu rope and Asia consist of the Ural Mountains, the Ural 

River, the Caucasus Mountains, and the Caspian and Black seas. Turkey and Russia or, officially, the Union of Soviet

OCEAN BOUNDARIES 

11 Socialist Republics, are each partly in Europe and partly in 

Asia. At Bering Strait, Asia is only thirty-six miles from 

North America. Asia was once joined to Africa by a low neck of wind-blown sand, the Isthmus of Suez. The Suez

Canal was dug across the isthmus; thus Asia and Africa were cut apart. Southwestern Asia is further divided from 

Africa by the Red Sea and the Gulf of Aden. 

Ocean Boundaries. The waves of three oceans break on the vast shores of Asia. To the east stretches the Pacific

Ocean, the largest body of water upon the earth. Pacific means peaceful, and the ocean is well named, for it has fewer perilous storms than any other ocean. The Pacific Ocean covers more area than all the land of the world. Near the island of Guam, the Pacific is six miles deep. Near the 

Philippine Islands a warm current, like the Gulf Stream of the Atlantic Ocean, moves northeastward across the

Pacific. From the dark color which marks it off from the adjoining waters it is called the Black Stream. It is also called the Japan Current, because it crosses the ocean in the latitude of Japan.

The warm south shore of Asia is washed by the Indian Ocean, which extends from Asia across the south temperate zone to the Antarctic Circle. Along the low, swampy, icebound north coast lies the Arctic Ocean. The Mediterranean Sea reaches in between Europe and Africa to the

Levant, as the Mediterranean coast of Asia and the adjoining continents is called.

Thus Asia has water boundaries on all sides save that of Europe. Of these, the Indian Ocean, on the south, lies wholly within the Eastern Hemisphere, which has less water than the Western Hemisphere. The Pacific Ocean lies partly in each hemisphere, eastern and western, northern and southern. The Arctic Ocean lies rough with pack ice. for miles offshore, almost wholly in the North Frigid Zone, but divided between the Eastern and Western hemispheres.

ASIA 

None the less, Asia is so large that vast areas lie far inland, thousands of miles from any ocean.

Coastline. Like the other continents north of the Equator, Asia has an irregular coast, with many harbors. There are, indeed, so many indentations that the coastline is 20,000 miles in length. The Pacific coast slopes gradually into the sea and there forms a continental shelf, as the formation is called where the continental land mass extends for a number of miles beyond the coast, beneath a shallow sea. Near shore, this continental shelf lies not more than a few feet 

The Indian Ocean at Ceylon. 

beneath the water, but farther out it has sloped downward till the sea is several hundred feet deep. At the outer edge of this shelf, the continent ends abruptly, and the ocean becomes suddenly thousands of feet deep.

The chains of islands looped along the shore not far from the mainland are the tops of low mountains that rise from the continental shelf. The Japan Islands lie separated from the mainland by the Japan Sea. The Philippine Islands, directly south of Japan, will be easy to locate on your physical map. 

The East Indies come next, and include the large islands of Borneo, Java, and Sumatra. Ceylon is close off the tip of India.

SIZE AND REGIONS 

15 Two peninsulas jut into the Pacific Ocean from the eastern coast of Asia. They are Kamchatka and Chosen. Kamchatka helps to inclose the sea of Okhotsk, lying between it and Bering Sea. It belongs to Russia. Chosen (Korea) belongs to Japan, and separates the Japan Sea from the Yellow 

Sea. Both of these peninsulas are much smaller, as you can see, than the three large peninsulas of the southern coast. 

Size. Eurasia is the largest land mass on the globe. Asia by itself is the largest continent. It is larger than the New 

World. It contains nearly half the land of the Eastern 

Hemisphere, and much more than a quarter of all the land of the earth.

From the Arctic Ocean to the Indian Ocean, Asia measures about 6500 miles. The distance from the Ural Mountains to the Pacific Ocean is only 500 miles shorter. This vast continent thus occupies half the distance between the North and South poles, and from east to west it stretches just about a quarter of the way around the globe. 

Regions. Like the other smaller continents, Asia consists of a number of physiographic regions which differ in surface, climate, and other natural characteristics that affect the life of the inhabitants. Since Europe is continuous with the land mass of Asia, it is not surprising to find that the regions of Europe resemble those of Asia, of which they are, in fact, 

The principal high mountain region of Europe is the western continuation of a greater, longer, higher, mountain region which crosses Asia from east to west. In Europe this region of high mountains lies north of 40 degrees north latitude; in Asia it lies south of that parallel. The highest mountains of the world form the center of this region in Asia.

About half of Asia lies north of the region of high mountains. It is mainly a lowland; but, as in Europe, the southern part of it is broken by mountains, and includes

PAUSTRALIA

COUNTRIES 

17 upland as well as lowland. The northern lowland slopes toward the Arctic Ocean; and as in Europe, much of it is fertile and suited to farming. The northern part, bordering the Arctic Ocean, is tundra, frozen during most of the year, and swampy when the warmth of the short summer thaws the ice. South of the tundras there are vast forests. 

Southern Asia, like the Mediterranean region of Europe, has three peninsulas; but they are vastly larger than those of southern Europe; and since they extend almost to the 

Equator, they are much hotter. Asia, like Europe, has an island empire along its coast; for Japan, the greatest nation of Asia, corresponds in that way to the British Isles in Europe. 

A great depression, the bottom of what was once a vast inland sea, stretches across the middle of Asia in about 40 degrees north latitude. This inland basin is fenced by mountains. The principal ranges of the high mountain region separate it from the southern peninsulas, while other mountain ranges lie between it and the great lowland of northern Asia. 

What do you know thus far of the surface of Asia?

Countries. We have observed that on other continents the boundaries of countries do not always correspond with the boundaries of natural regions. True, natural boundaries such as mountain ranges, rivers, or lakes often do separate countries, but they are not usually those which also separate geographic regions. Hence several countries often lie wholly or in part within a single natural region. On the other hand, several physiographic regions, such as those into which Asia. is divided, may form parts of one country. 

Four great nations control more than two thirds of all the territory of Asia. Two of them are Asiatic nations, the small but powerful empire of Japan, and her enormous rival, China, with an area greater than the whole of Europe. The other two are European nations. The Asiatic possessions of the British Empire, of which India is the largest, rank next

ASIA 

Differing sections of Asia present remarkable contrasts. 

The natives of the sparsely populated polar region find the reindeer their leading source of food, clothing, and transportation. in size to Canada and Australia. Last and most vast of all are the possessions of Russia in Asia. These occupy a third of the continent. They are large enough to make a continent by themselves, one only a little smaller than the continent of South America. 

The remaining third of Asia is divided among several independent, but unimportant nations, numerous colonies, and a few countries under the control of European powers. 

Mandated Territories. After the World War, the defeated nations were deprived of much of their foreign territory, and in some cases, new little nations were formed. 

The change was so sudden, however, that the people of these new countries were not entirely ready for self-government. 

The League of Nations therefore issued mandates to various more powerful countries to direct affairs in these countries and in the colonies taken from the defeated nations, until the native people should be prepared for complete selfgovernment. In the case of certain islands of the Pacific 

Ocean, however, native populations are at present so uncivilized that it may be many years before they are fitted

SECTIONS 

19 The natives of the densely populated tropic region of Asia need little clothing, employ water transport to a large extent, and for food depend largely upon the growing of rice. for self-government. The little colonies and new, young countries thus placed under the direction of more experienced nations are called mandated territories.

Japan has a mandate over about 1000 islands of the Pacific Ocean, including those north of the Equator which belonged to Germany before the conclusion of the World War. These islands include the Marianas, with the exception of Guam, which belongs to the United States; the 

Caroline Islands, and the Marshall Islands. Of these, the northern group of the Mariana Islands is volcanic and uninhabited. The Carolines are largely coral islets, and the Marshall Islands, which consist of two chains of lagoon islands, export copra.

In southwestern Asia, Palestine and Trans-Jordan are mandates of Great Britain, while Syria is a mandate of 

France. 

Part of New Guinea is a mandated territory of Australia, and the little island of Nauru is a mandate of Great Britain. 

Sections. So vast is the continent of Asia that the natural regions, as well as some of the countries, are themselves

ASIA 

continental in size. For purposes of study, therefore, it is convenient and helpful to divide Asia into sections where the geographical controls operate upon a smaller scale, that their effect upon the people may be the better understood. These sections may be named from their relative locations as the 

Northern, Eastern, Southern, Southeastern, and Southwestern sections of Asia.

Northern Asia is occupied by Siberia. Russia has made several divisions of this region. Eastern Asia comprises 

China and Japan. Southern Asia includes India, the middle peninsula which juts into the Indian Ocean. Southeastern 

Asia includes the peninsula of Indo-China, whose slender extremity, the Malay peninsula, reaches southward to the East Indies, which also belong to this section. Southwestern Asia comprises the peninsulas of Arabia, Anatolia (Asia Minor), and the countries which lie between the 

Caspian Sea and the Persian Gulf.

Map Study 

With the hemisphere maps open before you, tell the location of Asia, its ocean boundaries, and its size. Then locate, by the aid of the List of Maps in the front of the book, the relief map and the physical map of Asia. Find the high mountain region, the northern lowlands, the inland basin, and the great peninsulas. Note the coastal lowlands.

Next, turn to the political map of Asia, and locate Japan, China, India, and Russia in Asia. Finally, glance at the maps of the islands of the Pacific Ocean. Note the location of Australia and New Zealand.

The Gobi. 

Am. Mus. of Nat. Hist. 

An expedition sent by the American Museum of Natural History to explore this desert found the remains of extinct animals. form a confused mass of peaks and ranges known as a mountain knot. Pamir is a name given to lofty mountain valleys in this part of the world. 

North of The Pamirs, the mountain ranges spread out to encircle the low central depression. Thus the great highland region descends from central Asia to the broad lowlands in the north and west, and slopes to the Arctic 

Ocean and the Caspian Sea. At several places along the eastern and southern coast, comparatively large lowlands lie between the highlands and the oceans.

The desert plateau of The Gobi is part of the depressed region of swamps, deserts, salt lakes, and marshes. Excavations there have revealed fossil remains of the life that existed in the earliest ages of the earth. 

The great highland of Asia is part of the world-embracing ridge of plateaus and mountains which borders the shores. of the Pacific and Indian oceans. In Asia, as in the other continents, this highland region is subject to earthquakes, and has many active volcanoes. 

Plateaus. Find the Plateau of Tibet, north of the

Himalayas. Find India, the southern part of which is the

HIMALAYAS 

25 Deccan Plateau. Then look to the westward of India and locate the plateaus of Iran, Arabia, and Anatolia. 

Peninsulas. The Peninsula of Indo-China, in one with the Malay Peninsula, is best located first on your physical map, which shows its elevation. 

India forms another important peninsula. Reaching southward, it divides the northern waters of the Indian 

Ocean into the Bay of Bengal and the Arabian Sea.

Arabia is the large peninsula to the west, with its desert plateau rising to mountains 5000 to 10,000 feet high. The relief map shows how it is separated, in part, from the Plateau of Iran by the Persian Gulf and a great river, and from Africa by the Red Sea and the Suez Canal.

Himalaya Mountains. Of all the mountains that radiate from The Pamirs, the mighty Himalayas are the highest. 

The name Himalayas means, in Sanskrit, the "Abode of Snow," and the higher slopes are indeed perpetually white. 

They consist of several ranges. Rising from the 10,000 foot plateau of Tibet, the Himalayas have hundreds of peaks over 20,000 feet in height, and several that rise five miles above the level of the sea hide their snowy summits in the clouds. 

Mt. Everest, the loftiest mountain in the world.

ASIA 

Overtopping all other crests is Mt. Everest, the highest and most famous mountain peak in the world. It towers between 29,000 and 30,000 feet above the level of the sea. It has recently been attempted by mountaineers, some of whom have lost their lives on its dangerous heights. You may see news in the papers of other expeditions organized to scale the world's loftiest peak; but no one has yet reached the summit of Mt. Everest. 

Drainage Basins. Most of the rivers of the northern lowland flow into the Arctic Ocean; most of those in the eastern and southern highlands flow into the Pacific and Indian oceans. The mountain ranges often confine each stream of the highlands to a separate course. In the lowlands, on the contrary, there are few elevations. Hence the rivers here are joined by many large tributaries, thus forming a few immense river systems.

Both highlands and lowlands have large basins with no outlet to the ocean. There the streams flow into inland lakes and seas, or are lost in vast swamps.

The continental divide of Asia consists of the ridges which separate the four chief drainage basins. The divide follows the mountain ranges which border the great plateaus of 

Mongolia, Tibet, Turkistan, and Iran, and sweeps around the Kirghiz Steppe and the Caspian Sea. It thus encircles the greatest inland drainage area in the world, which, in fact, extends into the central portion of the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics. In the west the continental divide extends to Asia Minor, where the streams drain into the Mediterranean Sea. In the northeast it extends to Bering 

Strait. 

The drainage basins of Asia, the streams of which discharge into the oceans, correspond to a considerable extent to the sections into which Asia is divided. We shall study the rivers of Asia each in connection with the section upon the life and development of which they exert a controlling influence.

Zones. The map shows that the Arctic Circle passes through extreme northern Asia, so that part of the broad northern end of the continent lies in the North Frigid Zone, as does northern European Russia. Northern Asia is bordered by the ice pack floating in the Arctic Ocean. 

See where the Tropic of Cancer passes through southern Asia, and note the broad expanse of continent that lies north of it, in the North Temperate Zone. Yet an important part of Asia lies south of this line, in the Torrid Zone. 

Climate. Asia is so large that the southern shores reach to within a hundred miles or so of the Equator; whereas the northern part is within the North Frigid Zone. This wide extent in latitude accounts, in part, for the extreme variations of climate found on the continent. Along the irreg-

Asia. Winds, ocean currents, and lines of equal temperature. ular coastline the climate is moderated by the ocean currents and by the moisture of the ocean winds, but vast interior areas are thousands of miles from the sea. 

There are, therefore, extremes of moisture and aridity. Again, some areas are below sea level, while others rise miles higher. 

Thus altitude is a factor in extreme variations of climate. 

The prevailing winds are westerlies, but all the moisture they gather in crossing the Atlantic Ocean falls as rain upon Europe. By the time they have crossed the Ural Mountains and the Caucasus, the westerlies are dry winds. Hence there is a belt of deserts across the middle of Asia, as you can see on your rainfall map. There are also monsoons, the local storms of certain areas, that combine with latitude,

MONSOONS 

29 altitude, winds, and distance from ocean currents to cause the various kinds of climate found in Asia. Because of wide differences in the latitude, the altitude, the winds, the distance from the sea, and other minor causes, every kind of climate may be found in the vast continent of Asia. 

The great northern lowland of Asia is as far north as Canada, and has severely cold winters. The temperature in places has been known to fall as low as 90 degrees below zero, and the average temperature is 60 degrees below zero. 

Within the Arctic Circle there are days in winter when the sun does not rise, and in summer days when it does not set. 

The summers, although short, are hot. On that account, and because the summer days are extremely long, grain and other crops are harvested as far north as the Arctic Circle. 

Many mountain tops in or near the Torrid Zone are as cold and barren as the polar regions. In the south, on the river plains and at moderate heights, frost seldom occurs. 

The summer heat may reach 110 degrees, but it is usually less. The snow fields and the rainy season moderate the heat.

The interior of Asia has a continental climate, such as is found in all great land masses far from the influence of large bodies of water. That is, the summers are hot and the winters cold. The air, however, is dry, and this makes both heat and cold more bearable.

The Monsoon. When it is summer in the Northern 

Hemisphere, the sun makes Asia and the air above it very hot. 

As the hot air is lighter than the cool air, such as moves over the ocean, a strong, steady, rain-bearing wind called a monsoon blows from the Indian Ocean and from the southern Pacific. The general direction of the monsoon is from the southwest. When the warm, vapor-laden monsoon blows against the mountains, the moisture falls as rain. 

Hence the foot of the south slope of the Himalayas is the rainiest part of the world. The rainfall there is more than

ASIA 

Inhabitants per square mile under 2 2 to 26 

26 to 128 128 to 256 256 to 512 

Over 512 ARCTIC CIRCLE  

500 1000

EQUATOR TROPIC OF CANCER 

Distribution of population in Asia. 

500 inches annually. The rainy season lasts from June to 

September. But as the sun moves southward, the oceans become warmer than the land, and a cool northeast breeze blows from the mountains. It is then the dry season. 

The monsoon does not reach the Plateaus of Iran and Arabia. These regions are in the belt of northeast trade winds. By the time the trades reach these parts of Asia, they have lost the moisture they brought from the Pacific Ocean. Hence Iran and Arabia are chiefly desert. These deserts must be distinguished from those which lie in the path of the westerlies, of which The Gobi is the largest. 

Both of these desert regions, however, are caused by the fact that Asia is so large, and the interior so far from the ocean that the winds, whether blowing from the Atlantic or

PEOPLE 

31 the Pacific Ocean, are dry when they reach the interior of the vast land mass of the continent. 

The south and southeastern coasts are visited by violent gales called typhoons. These are like the hurricanes of the 

West Indies, and cause much damage along the China Sea and the Bay of Bengal.

The westerlies are cyclonic winds; that is, they swirl around a center of low pressure, sweep out over the Arctic and Pacific oceans, and return as rainstorms. Hence most of northern and eastern Asia has at least twenty inches of rainfall a year, enough for the growth of crops. The islands, finally, have a climate that varies greatly. 

People. We have considered some of the geographical controls of Asia, the largest continent, including its location. in three zones and in the Eastern Hemisphere, its surface, which includes some of the world's highest mountains, greatest deserts, richest coastal lowlands, and coldest northern

ASIA 

plains; and its climate, which ranges from the driest to the wettest, and from the coldest to the hottest. There are other geographical controls which affect the lives of its people, as we shall learn in the study of the sections of Asia. 

Before we consider to what extent the geographical controls of location, surface, climate, and varied resources have determined the civilization of plain, mountain, plateau, and off-shore islands, and to what extent cultural history and race (yellow, brown, or white) have determined the present life of the varied peoples of Asia, let us look at them as our airplane flies above the geographical regions in which they make their homes. These regions vary from the frozen tundra of the 

Arctic to the steamy heat of the tropic lowlands, and from the icy peaks of the Himalaya Mountains to that sun-baked desert the Gobi. There is the fragrant evergreen forest of

Asiatic Russia, with its long white winters; there is the tangled hardwood jungle of India, hot, wet, mysterious with the calls of strange beasts and the more menacing silence of great serpents. There is the loveliness of verdant New 

Zealand, with its fjords and canyons.

At some time, perhaps thousands of years ago, other people have migrated, or else fled into the various parts of Asia and Australia with which this book is to deal. Let us see how they solved their problems of how to live in each of the varying regions into which the ancient continent is geographically divided.

In Asia lives half the population of the world. To what extent have the geographical controls of location, surface, climate, and varied resources determined the civilization of plain, mountain, plateau, and off-shore islands, and to what extent have cultural history and race (yellow, brown, or white) determined the present life of the people of Asia?

There are people as small and industrious as the Chinese rice farmers, and as large and easy going as the Siberian lumbermen. There are people whose civilization is as new

PEOPLE 

33 as that of the modern Japanese, and as old as that of the tribes of ancient Palestine. 

Several likenesses among these people are outstanding. 

In much of Asia there are nomadic peoples who follow their Newman 

An ivory carver of northern India. Note his Caucasian features. flocks from one patch of grassland to another; and in Asia as a whole (especially southwestern Asia, India and China) there is fully twice as much irrigation as in the rest of the world. What kind of people are these countless multitudes who have made Asia their home? 

Mongolian. Mongolians, called the yellow race by reason of the brownish-yellow color of their skin, occupy most of the continent. The Mongolians are short and have straight black hair, narrow, oblique eyes, high cheek bones, and flat noses, and the beards of the men are but scanty. The 

Chinese and Japanese are the Mongolians with whom we in America are most familiar. The upper classes are as well educated as any people in Europe, but on the Plateau of Tibet and other remote places the natives are warlike, barbaric, and hostile to strangers. Many of the tribes who get

ASIA 

their living out of the mountains and the deserts are Nomads, that is, they roam from place to place. 

White. The people of India, Persia, and Arabia belong to the white race, though their skins are browned by the sun and their eyes are dark. It is a scientific fact that dark eyes are better able than blue to withstand strong sunlight, as the heavier color pigment forms a better protection to the delicate structure behind the iris. A dark skin is also a better protection in hot sunshine than is the pale skin seen in a cool, cloudy land like Sweden.

The people of India, centuries ago, reached a high degree of civilization, and our kinship with them is shown by the term Indo-European sometimes employed for the Aryan race. There is one other white race in Asia, the Semitic, to which belong the Jewish and Arab people of the Arabian peninsula.

Brown. Asia is also inhabited by people of the Malay or Polynesian race, which is brown. The Polynesians are the fine-looking people of New Zealand and the Hawaiian 

Islands, with their wavy hair, dark eyes, splendid physiques, and features resembling both Mongol and Negro.. Most 

Malays are marvelous swimmers. This brown race inhabits the Malay Peninsula and many of the neighboring islands. 

The very word means " many islands."

Asia has several areas of extremely dense population, but near them are other areas that are almost uninhabited. 

Altogether, the vast continent of Asia is the home of half of the world's population.

PART II. - EASTERN ASIA 

Asia is particularly associated with the yellow-skinned people, the Mongolian race, of whom the Chinese and Japanese are the most numerous, the most highly civilized, and the most powerful. 

Eastern Asia lies east of The Pamirs, between the northern lowland and the peninsulas of India and Indo-China. If a continent as large as Europe were cut out of this part of Asia, there would be enough land remaining to make three countries the size of France, Italy, and Greece. 

Most of Eastern Asia belongs to China, one of the largest nations of the earth, larger than the United States, Canada, or Brazil, and more populous than all three of them together. 

The rest of Eastern Asia, though relatively little from the standpoint of area, belongs to Japan. Her territory lies chiefly upon the archipelago of off-shore islands strung along the coast from Kamchatka to the Tropic of Cancer. In 

Chosen and elsewhere, however, Japan has a foothold on the mainland.

This small nation, a dwarf beside her giant neighbor, is the mightiest of the Asiatic nations, one of the five or six great international powers. She has risen to this position. from one of comparative obscurity within the memories of men now living. Her growth was accelerated at the time. 

Japanese ships are seen in all Pacific of the World War.

GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS

Japanese cherry trees in blossom.

CHAPTER III JAPAN LAND OF THE RISING SUN GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS Location. The Japanese Empire includes the peninsula of Chosen (Korea) and a chain of islands fully 2400 miles in length which extends along the Pacific coast of Asia. 

There are about 4000 islands, all told, though many of them are mere rocks that rise above the sea. 

Size and Importance. The little island Empire of Japan is the foremost country in Asia. In area and population

JAPAN 

Japan is but little larger than the British Isles, and far smaller than China and Russia in Asia, her neighbors on the mainland. Yet the wisdom of her statesmen, supported by the courage, self-denial, and patriotism of her citizens, has enabled Japan to outrank all other Asiatic countries. 

Japan proper includes only the four large islands off the sea of Japan and the Strait of Korea. Of these, Honshu is the largest and most important. It measures 700 miles from end to end. Find the others on your map, Hokkaido,

Shikoku, and Kyushu. The lower half of the large island of Sakhalin, off Siberia, an area called Karafuto, also belongs to the Japanese Empire. Japan, also, exercises a mandate over certain small Pacific islands.

Japan lies almost straight across the Pacific Ocean from our own west coast. Trace the parallels of latitude across the Pacific and see how closely the southern boundary of Kyushu and the northern boundary of Hokkaido meet our own southern and northern boundaries.

The Chosen peninsula is attached to the mainland of 

Manchuria; Taiwan (Formosa) is separated from China proper only by a strait, and the four main islands of Japan may be reached from Chinese ports by a short voyage across the Yellow Sea or the East China Sea.

As to Japan's neighbors on the south, the Philippine Islands, a possession of the United States, begin not far beyond Taiwan, though the meridians of longitude show them to be a little farther west than Japan proper.

The Japanese call their land Nippon, and in olden times 

Europe spoke of it as Cipango.

Because the Japanese have always viewed with reverence the sun rising above the Pacific Ocean, Japan is also poetically termed "The Land of the Rising Sun." The naval flag of Japan bears this emblem. 

Scenic Beauty. The waters between the three islands of the main group are dotted with rocky islands covered

SURFACE 

41 with trees and temples. The scenery is enchanting in this 

Inland Sea, as it is called.

There is many a landscape in which snow-crowned peaks, glowing rose at sundown, lie mirrored in the still waters of the rice fields. Flowers are planted in profusion. Cherry trees which bear no fruit are grown for the sake of their pink blossoms. Other favorite flowers, often copied by Japanese artists in their decorative designs, are the wistaria, iris, peony, and the chrysanthemum, the national flower of Japan. 

Japanese gardeners are expert in arranging miniature landscapes, with dwarf trees, and realistic but diminutive rocks, lakes, and rivers. People have their tea and rice cakes in gardens gay with cherry blossoms or spicy with chrysanthemums. 

Japan is a country in which east meets west; and the capital city has shops, offices, and modes of transportation surprisingly like our own. All within less than a hundred years, Japan has adopted more western ways than ancient Asia ever dreamed of seeing.

Surface. The islands are volcanic. Perhaps 150 of the volcanoes are still active, and big eruptions have occurred from time to time. As another evidence that there is volcanic activity somewhere underground, hot springs steam in many places, as around Lake Biwa, on Honshu.

The volcanic nature of the islands, which are part of a great belt of volcanoes around the Pacific, makes slight earthquake shocks a daily commonplace. Occasionally a severe earthquake occurs. Then the cities may be destroyed by the shaking down of buildings, by the fire that breaks out among the ruins, or by a typhoon and a tidal wave that may accompany the "quake."

One characteristic that adds scenic value is the fact that the islands are rough and rocky, with a main mountain range that follows the curve of the islands, and cross ranges that run into the sea in wave-lashed promontories. Many

JAPAN 

Mt. Fujiyama, a volcanic cone over 12,000 feet high.

Newman small and rapid rivers descend the gorges. There are lowlands along some of the coast, and there are broad mountain valleys. Chosen is mountainous, though it descends to plains in the west, and Formosa has high mountains clothed with forests. 

Salt spray and the call of the sea birds, and the deep blue of the Japan Current, warmed by its passage through equatorial waters, shores green with rustling bamboo, mountains clothed with forests, and the symmetrical white dome of Mount Fujiyama rising over all that, in clear weather, is often the traveler's first impression of Japan. 

While Fujiyama, on Honshu, towering over 12,000 feet

HARBORS 

43 above the sea, is the highest peak of Japan proper, Mount 

Morrison (Niitaka), on Taiwan, rises to 14,500 feet, or practically to the height of Mount Whitney in California. 

The Sea of Japan is but the shallow covering of the continental shelf of Asia, but the shelf ends abruptly on the Pacific side of the islands, descending almost at once to the great depths of the ocean.

Harbors. As the coast of Japan has sunk, leaving many bays and islands, good harbors are numerous. Those of 

Tokyo and Yokohama, on Tokyo Bay, and Kobe, on the south coast of Honshu, are now the most important. 

Nagasaki, on the northwest coast of Kyushu, about opposite Shanghai, was more frequented several hundred years ago. 

Osaka, on Honshu, has recently developed a good deepwater harbor. Osaka is a city of canals lined by modern factory buildings. Countless small harbors give shelter to the fishing fleets. 

Waterpower. Many small rivers, the longest less than 

300 miles in length, tumble down the mountain valleys, singing along the rapids and roaring over the water-falls. 

This is especially true in spring, when they are fed by the melting snows of the mountains, and after the cyclonic storms of summer. This means an abundance of potential hydro-electric power, not all of which is used at present. 

Some of it is employed to safeguard the dangerous sea coast with miles of incandescent lights. 

Climate. Most of Japan lies in the north temperate zone, and the climate is stimulating. Though winds from continental Asia blow in winter, they are moderated by passing over the Japan Sea, and the mountains of Japan cut them off from her eastern and southern slopes. The surrounding waters are warmed, on the Pacific side, by the Japan Current. The Empire has a heavier rainfall than 

New York, as your map shows; but not enough rain occurs in the growing season, and crops have to be irrigated.

JAPAN 

There is fog much of the year, as on our own west coast.

The temperature varies considerably from north to south. Taiwan has hot summers and no snow in winter, while Karafuto has severe winters, with snow in the mountain canyons most of the year around. Midway of these extremes, in Japan proper, the climate is mild, save for a short time in midsummer, when it is hot and humid. 

The typhoons (hurricanes) that sometimes strike Japan are violent rains with whirling, twisting winds that often do much damage. Occasional cyclonic storms in winter also sweep the Pacific coast of Japan, bringing snow or rain.

Fisheries. The surrounding waters teem with fish. 

Nearly 400 varieties are found in the shallow Sea of Japan.

Herring and sardines and several with less familiar names like tai and bonito are the commonest. Cod and mackerel are also taken. The rivers and small lakes contain an abundance of fresh-water fish. 

Crabs and lobsters are caught in large numbers, and oyster raising has become an important industry in Japan. 

Forests and Animal Life. The mountainsides are clothed with forests of several curious woods, the ginkgo, a Japanese elm, a cedar, a camphor tree, sturdy teak, and frail bamboo, though there is little good timber for building purposes. 

Mulberry trees, with their sweet berries, are often found growing wild. In these forests live monkeys, clownish little black bears, shaggy brown bears, and burrowing badgers. In every frog-peopled pool, herons and other fish-eating birds pursue their prey. 

The cormorant is a bird almost as large as a goose, and the Japanese train it to catch fish for its owner. A ring is put about the bird's neck, and it cannot swallow the fish till the ring is removed at feeding time. Therefore it brings each fish hopefully to its master.

MINERALS 

45 Minerals. A large amount of copper is mined in Japan, which is one of the important copper-producing countries. 

There are also valuable deposits of petroleum and pottery clay. Japan is one of the three chief coal-mining countries of Asia. She also imports coal from China. 

Questions 1. How far is Japan from our West coast? How far is Japan from China? How far from the Philippines? (Estimate the distance by your map scale.) 

2. Compare the climate of northern and southern Japan. 

3. What advantages result from Japan's irregular coastline, with eastern boundaries in shallow seas? 

4. What is the surface of Japan proper, and of other parts of the Japanese Empire? 

5. What are Japan's natural resources?

There are good articles in Asia, by Carpenter, and How the 

World Is Housed, by the same author.

Optional Reading 

If you have the time to spare, read Tales Told in Korea, by Metzger, and The Story of Japan, by Van Bergen.

CHAPTER IV A PROGRESSIVE PEOPLE EFFECTS OF THE GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS 

Nowhere have the geographical controls influenced the character of the people more strongly than in Japan. The rugged surface, much of it unsuited for farming, has compelled the Japanese to cultivate their fields intensively, and has helped to make the people thrifty and hardworking. 

The scarcity of lumber has led to the erection of flimsy houses which, although they provide privacy and shelter from the rain, give almost no protection from summer heat or winter cold. Thus, the Japanese have developed patience and strength to withstand hardships. Their little land compels them to live close together. Thus they have been taught consideration for one another, and are willing to sacrifice

A PROGRESSIVE PEOPLE 

47 their personal interests to those of their relatives or to the welfare of Japan. No people in the world are more polite or more patriotic than the Japanese. Their picturesque country has developed in them good taste and artistic ability. 

Crops. More than half the Japanese live on farms, though hardly more than a seventh of the land is adapted to agriculture. The farms are extremely small, usually only two or three acres apiece; but often several crops are raised each year.

Farmers usually give a part of their time to fishing. Rice, vegetables, and tea are the main food crops. 

Rice. Rice, the food on which fully half of the human race depends, is the foremost food crop in Asia. Rice is a grass. 

Its white seed is a grain or cereal. No other plant provides food for so many people. Rice flourishes best on land that can be flooded. In Japan, it is grown chiefly on the lowland meadows that fringe the streams and irrigation canals. 

Courtesy Japan Tourist Bureau 

Japanese women threshing rice. 

Most of the rice eaten or exported is grown in Asia and bordering islands. Rice grows best on the fertile coastal plains where the fields can be flooded during most of the growing season, though it is also grown on mountain terraces.

JAPAN 

The seeds are first sown in beds of mud enriched with such fertilizer (manure) as the farm provides. As a rule, the plow is drawn by a buffalo. One acre will sprout enough plants for a ten-acre crop, while the remaining nine acres are being used for other purposes. When the seedling plants are about a foot high, they are transplanted to the fields, and grown under several inches of water. Each level rice field has a little earthen wall to keep the water in. 

, 200,000 acres

Equator  

200 400 600 2. of Cancer 

A. - Rice-producing regions of Asia.

Though much hand labor is employed, modern methods of irrigating the paddy fields are used, as a rule. Often the people of a farm-village combine to install a pumping-plant. 

The toylike farms are terraced, little irrigation ditches are dug, and fenced off with growing herbage. When the plants have ceased to grow, the fields are drained. 

When the grain has ripened, it is harvested by hand with short-handled sickles and carried in baskets or on the owner's back to the threshing-floor. The women winnow the rice by tossing it into the air till the husks are removed, or it may be trodden by human feet. The kernels may also be separated from the stems by a stone roller that is dragged across

CROPS 

49 the threshing floor. The rice straw is fed to the buffalo or it may be used as fuel, or thatch for the roof of a hut. 

It is even used in making raincoats, hats, sandals, and paper. If anything is left, it is saved for fertilizer. Look at the map and note the rice-growing areas of Japan. 

Other Crops. The tea bushes are grown on hillsides, chiefly in southern Japan. The leaves are picked by hand, and when harvest time comes, entire families are thus kept busy. Most Japanese tea is cured for home use.

Sugar, cotton, and tobacco, and in cooler areas, wheat, potatoes, and soy beans are also raised. Wheat cannot be grown cheaply, because the farms are too small. 

Although Japan has difficulty in providing food for her population, she raises such enormous crops of beans that large quantities are dried for export. 

There is little livestock in Japan, save in Hokkaido.

In too many parts of Japan, bamboo occupies land that might otherwise provide grass for forage. Newman

Picking tea leaves on a hillside in Japan. Asia is the great tea-drinking and tea-exporting continent.

JAPAN 

Bamboo. Bamboo has many uses. 

It is a graceful grass with large, hollow-jointed stalks that grow to a height of twenty to over a hundred feet. It grows abundantly throughout Asia, as far north as Japan. It is one of the most useful plants in the world, although in America it is seldom used except for fishing rods. The entire stems are used in 

Asia for rafts, masts, and water pipes, as well as for building houses and making furniture. The joints are made into water buckets, bottles, vases, and cooking utensils. Strips of bamboo are woven into baskets, packing cases, and mats. 

Bamboo is also used for umbrella handles, canes, and fishing rods. The inner parts of the stem are made into paper; the young shoots, which are rather like asparagus, are used in Oriental dishes; and even the seed or grain is eaten. 

Fishing. A great many people engage in this occupation; for with the scarcity of livestock, there is little meat in Japan. The government subsidizes a large number of fishing-vessels (aids them with public money), besides supporting hatcheries in order to replenish the salmon, which have too often been caught without regard to the future. 

To preserve some of this fish, salt is evaporated from sea water. Herrings, sardines, and other fish are exported to 

China, while crabs are canned for export to the United 

States. Fish oil is also made, and what is left when the oil is extracted is used as fertilizer. 

Oyster culturists find conditions especially favorable around the southern end of Honshu, where the water is clear and not too cold. Here bushy weeds are made to grow under water for the spat (young oysters) to cling to. 

The patient Japanese discovered the method of producing real oriental pearls by putting a foreign body into the shell of the pearl oyster. The producing of cultured pearls is a profitable industry in Japan.

Goldfish for ornamental pools are raised, and many are exported to the United States.

SILK 

Manufactures. There is plenty of coal, especially in 

Hokkaido and Kiushu, and the mountain streams furnish water power for developing electricity. The Japanese are exceedingly dexterous. The people are frugal, living upon a little tea, rice, and fish. They work for long hours at the lowest possible wages. The combination of cheap hydroelectric power for running machinery, coupled with cheap skilled labor for work that must be done by hand, largely accounts for Japan's prominence as a manufacturing nation. 

100,000 lbs.

200 400 600 M. Equator of Cancer

Distribution of silk-producing areas of Asia. 

Silk. The preparation of raw silk is an enormous industry. Its tremendous development is an indirect result of the suitability of the climate to the mulberry tree. The raising of silkworms on mulberry leaves is conducted as a home industry, though the fabric is spun and woven in mills. 

Here again, cheap labor is in demand, and large numbers of young women find occupation in the production of silks, satins, and tapestries of exquisite softness and lovely hue.

In addition to what is woven for home consumption, much is exported, for over half the world's raw silk is shipped from Japan.

JAPAN 

The silkworm is not a worm, but the caterpillar of a moth. 

The moth lays several hundred minute eggs, from which the tiny caterpillars are hatched in incubators, by artificial heat. The caterpillars are fed mulberry leaves. About 

Ewing Galloway 

A school playground at Kyoto. Small girls dress much like their mothers. 

They even wear their hair up. four times the greedy caterpillars outgrow their skins and shed them. When full-grown, each caterpillar spins a cocoon, wrapping its body in a silk case, which it fastens to a bare branch arranged for the purpose. The cocoons are about an inch long, and are either white or yellow. They are gathered after a few days, and are heated to kill the silkworms, then soaked in hot water, to loosen the gum which holds the cocoons together. The filament in a cocoon is about 1000 yards long and very thin. It takes several twisted together to make one thread. This process is called reeling. The product, raw silk, is stronger, but also more expensive than any other fiber used in textile manufacture.

POTTERY 

53 Lacquering. Lacquering and artistic woodworking are crafts in which the Japanese are especially skillful. A tree native to Japan yields a sap of which lacquer, a fine varnish, is made, and the smoothly lacquered boxes and other artistic bits of woodwork are usually painted and decorated. 

Pottery. Pottery clay, a fine grade, is found in Japan, and pottery has been made since at least the thirteenth century near Nagoya. There practically every citizen is employed in pottery making, glazing, and decorating. 

Satsuma ware is even better known than Nagoya ware. 

Pottery is, indeed, one of the chief products of Japan. 

Other Manufactures. The Japanese are noted for their artistic taste, especially in the decoration of pottery, in the em- 

A Japanese room. 

As people sit and sleep on straw mats about an inch thick, everything is spotless, and everyone removes his street shoes at the front door. broidering of screens and garments, in the carving of wood and ivory, and in the manufacture of bronze and silverware. 

Silks are often decorated with pictures in Japanese embroidery. With exquisite simplicity of line and coloring, cranes and irises, azaleas, chrysanthemums, Mount Fuji-

JAPAN 

yamas appear in turn on embroidered silks, velvets, and brocades. In fact, no modern nation excels Japan in the beauty of her decorative art. 

Raw cotton, imported from India, is woven into cotton. cloth, and made into cheap garments for ordinary wear. 

Straw is manufactured into hats for export. 

Ewing Galloway

The wooden houses typical of Japan, outside the large cities. 

There is also a paper mulberry tree from which the 

Japanese make paper for window panes, handkerchiefs, and even certain kinds of clothing. 

In the manufacture of watches and in paper making Japan is one of the leading countries. The so-called rice paper is not made from any part of the rice plant, but from the pith of small trees that grow in Taiwan. Paper is, however, made from rice straw. Thousands of breweries in Japan are engaged in the manufacture of sake, the national drink, a sort of beer made from rice.

People. The Japanese are, as a rule, short in stature; but they are agile and possessed of enormous endurance. 

Their system of self-defense, jujutsu, is a famous one.

PEOPLE 

55 The professional wrestlers are huge men who possess great strength, and Japanese acrobats are noted for their skill and daring. A frugal, hard-working people, the Japanese are extremely courteous and adaptable, light-hearted, and polite. 

Like all Oriental people, they also seem to us very secretive. 

For generations they have been subject to stern military discipline. Save at court and in military circles, where people have become completely westernized, they eat with chopsticks. Men, women, and children wear kimonos long enough to wrap warmly around their feet in winter when seated on the floors of the little bamboo houses with paper Parts of Japan get occasional winter snows. windows. They remove their outdoor footwear before entering a building, sleep on mats, and dine on stands only a few inches high.

Dense population. In an area no larger than the state of Texas, Japan has more than 80,000,000 people. That is more than half as many as there are in the United States, as

JAPAN 

you can see by consulting the population figures in the Appendix at the back of the book. Add to the fact of the large number of people who make the islands of Japan their home, the fact that part of Japan consists of snowcapped mountains, part of rocky, uninhabited islands, and it is easy to see that the population must be dense in the warm lowlands of the inhabited islands. 

Colonizing. Japan finds it hard to raise enough food for her increasing population. Moreover, the opportunities for advancement in Japan are limited, because most of the people are poor; hence thousands of Japanese emigrate to other countries. Many have emigrated to the main body of the 

United States, as well as to the Hawaiian Islands. Because so many Japanese emigrants came into competition with American farmers and other workmen in California, a Federal Law excluding them was passed in 1924. 

Unlike England, Japan has no large family of colonies scattered all over the globe, where her surplus population may settle and still remain citizens of the empire. Hence her rise to a place among the leading nations of the world is the more remarkable. But Japan has made extreme efforts to establish colonies. After having aided Chosen (Korea) to freedom from Chinese control, Japan fought off Russian efforts to acquire that fertile land, then added it to her own dominion. She also gained possession of the island of

Taiwan (Formosa). Half of Sakhalin belongs to Japan, but it is too cold to attract many Japanese settlers. 

Chosen. This fertile mountainous peninsula is the home of a people who are largely tillers of the soil. Chosen is valuable to Japan as a source of food supplies. Modern methods are being introduced, and Japanese immigrants are making homes for themselves there.

Taiwan. This large island once belonged to China, and is still partly peopled by Chinese, though Japan has been establishing schools and railroads. The bananas and raw

COLONIZING 

57 sugar of this tropic island are needed in Japan, and Taiwan's camphor industry is managed under Japanese government 

Camphor is obtained from a sort of laurel tree that thrives especially in Taiwan. The lumber is used for making clothes chests. Chips are steamed to extract the white gum or camphor, which is used as a medicine. Most of the camphor is, however, bought by manufacturers of celluloid. 

In pursuit of her policy of expansion, Japan has invaded. the mainland of China, and as a result has come into conflict with the armies of China. Japan claims part of Manchuria, though China considers it her own. The League of Nations at Geneva has attempted to secure the withdrawal of the 

Japanese forces from this territory.

History. Japan was once a hermit nation. No foreigner was permitted to set foot upon her shores; but 250 years of peace so strengthened her that she was able to forge ahead. to her present degree of progress and prosperity, and rank as one of the world powers. Her first contacts with white people were with Dutch traders who came to the port of Nagasaki in the sixteenth century. Later came Spanish and Portuguese traders and explorers, and American whalers.

In 1854 Commodore Perry made a treaty by which Japan agreed to trade with the United States. Other countries followed this example. The Japanese quickly adapted themselves to the changes thrust upon them.

Old customs were abolished by law; and a nation of 

Samurai (military men) armed with swords, whose leading general, the shogun, had greater power than had the Emperor (Mikado), became a nation of merchants and manufacturers. The Japanese simplified their language, and modernized their fields and factories, schools, and business houses. They invited scientific men at Washington, D. C., to come to Japan. They secured advisers and technical men from abroad. They adopted American

COMMERCE 

59 educational methods, together with such western achievements as are typified by railroads, radios, and electric lights. 

Commerce. It is only natural that the two leading nations bordering on the Pacific Ocean should carry on a great deal of commerce with each other. We have, in fact, more trade with Japan, in both exports and imports, than with any other country in Asia. For instance, we ship raw cotton to Japan, and Japan returns it to us woven into cotton goods, and decorated with Japanese designs. 

Raw silk, tea, and manufactures of distinctive Japanese products, silk, cotton, copper, and rice to be used as food or made into starch, are exported to Europe and America. 

The United States is Japan's best customer for these products.

Raw cotton, steel, and machinery are imported, also wheat to be ground into flour, which comes chiefly from 

Russia, Argentina, and the United States.

The standard of Japanese money is the yen, which is worth fifty cents.

Japan has a large merchant marine, with steamship lines that use the Panama Canal to reach the east coasts of the United States, Europe, South America, and Australia.

Japan has a total of over 7000 miles of railroads. The principal railroad is a trunk line from the northern part of Honshu to the southern part of Kyushu, which are joined by a tunnel that lies for a mile under the sea as it crosses the strait between Honshu and Kyushu. Electric railroads serve the larger cities. Japan has submarine cable connections with all parts of the world. The telegraph, telephone, and radio are widely used; the post office system includes a postal savings bank. Airplanes are extensively used, especially by the army.

Government. Japan is a limited monarchy. The Emperor, or Mikado, has more power than most European monarchs. The laws are made by the Diet, composed of two

JAPAN 

Courtesy of Japan Tourist Bureau 

Nihonbashi Street, Tokyo, which has many modern aspects. bodies, the House of Lords and the House of Representatives. 

The people take great interest in political questions and campaigns. 

Education and Religion. Many Japanese students attend college in the United States. One sees them from New York to California. Japan has several Imperial Universities, and there is universal education for girls as well as boys. 

Owing in part to Russian literature, many Japanese are agnostics, professing a belief in no religion whatever. Most of the people, however, are Buddhists, and the traveler sees images of the Buddha everywhere in Japan. A mammoth bronze image called Great Buddha sits contemplative at 

Kamakura. Some Japanese are Shintoists. Throughout 

Japan there are thousands of humble temples and shrines at which the people worship their ancestors, the heroes of Japanese history, and countless gods, including such natural objects as the sun, mountains, and winds. This religion is called Shinto (the holy way). A few have accepted the teachings of Christian missionaries, and there are Christian

CITIES 

61 hospitals everywhere. Freedom of worship is permitted throughout the Empire.

Cities. Two of the largest cities of Asia, Osaka and Tokyo, are in Japan; each has about two million inhabitants. That is very large indeed for Asia. With twice as many people as France, and somewhat larger in area, Japan like France has nine cities with a population of about 200,000 or more. 

In architecture and other features except size, most Japanese cities are less impressive than those of Europe and America, for they are mainly collections of huts and small shops. 

POP Wide World Photos 

Yokohama, on Tokyo Bay, one of Japan's many good harbors.

TOKYO, the capital city, lies on the plain behind Tokyo Bay, on the island of Honshu. It is an interesting combination of the ancient and the modern. It has the palace of the 

Mikado and many government offices, as well as the Imperial University, the leading educational institution in Japan. It has tall buildings, constructed like American skyscrapers. 

In the wide new streets laid out since the earthquake of 1923, jinrikishas and bicycles divide the traffic with American automobiles.

The jinrikisha is a two-wheeled carriage drawn by a coolie. 

The coolie, as the native laborer is called, will draw a passen-

JAPAN 

ger in a jinrikisha at a rate as fast as that of a horse, for several hours at a time.

Engines, blankets, glass, hats, and a great many works of art in bronze and ivory are made in Tokyo. Modern sugar refineries, cotton and woolen mills, and office buildings look across the city at an older quarter with streets like muddy lanes, fronted by little shops built of bamboo. Women in sandals and kimonos, with their babies strapped to their backs, go shopping in a department store equipped with escalators. Perhaps their daughters play tennis in American dress and wear bobbed hair. 

YOKOHAMA, Tokyo's port city, is less than twenty miles south, and connected with Tokyo by electric trains. 

Yokohama has a fine, deep harbor inclosed by artificial breakwaters on Tokyo Bay, and is the leading seaport of the Japanese Empire. At her wharves there are concrete warehouses, built since 1923. Vessels aromatic with squared logs of mammoth Douglas firs from Vancouver Island drop anchor side by side with ships whose holds disgorge rice and eggs from China, beef and wheat from Canada, Australia, or the United States, and cotton from India, China, or our own south. Japan herself exports enormous amounts of raw silk to France and the United States.

NAGOYA is a great center for embroidery and silk manufacturing and makes pottery and toys.

OSAKA, the chief cotton milling center, is a great port and railroad terminus, and the chief manufacturing city in Japan. The establishments include shipyards, iron works, sugar refineries, and breweries. It also manufactures locomotives, agricultural machinery, and other iron and steel. goods.

KYOTO is an ancient city, with the pagodas and rock gardens beloved of Japanese artists. On account of its beautiful architecture, its temples and palaces, its artistic china, embroidery, fans, and toys, lacquer ware and inlaid

PUPIL ACTIVITIES 

63 metal work, as well as the charm of the unspoiled Japanese customs that prevail there, it is most interesting to visitors. 

KOBE, on the south coast of Honshu, is another factory city and seaport. Kobe has railroads and electric cars, and its waterfront resounds to the clang of its busy shipbuilding yards.
