CHAPTER V CHINA HOME OF AN ANCIENT PEOPLE GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS 

On the mainland of Asia, opposite Japan, lies China, a giant among nations. China has been weakened by its lack of political unity. 

Location. The Chinese Republic, with the dominions and dependencies which it claims, occupies a vast area in eastern Asia, extending from Asiatic Russia to the peninsula of Indo- 

China, and from the Yellow Sea to the Himalaya Mountains. Thus it occupies about one fourth of Asia. 

Of this territory, China proper, in the southeast, is a compact area about a third that of the Chinese Republic, and here almost the entire population is to be found. 

The territory claimed by China includes Mongolia, Tibet, Sinkiang (Chinese Turkistan), and Manchuria. Of these, most of Mongolia has, however, recently declared its independence, while Manchuria has been the object of aggression on the part of Russia and Japan. As a result of recent warfare, part of Manchuria (Manchukuo) has, indeed, disclaimed Chinese control. Furthermore, Sinkiang, in western China, and Tibet, in the southwest, are remote and difficult of access. Consequently their allegiance to the central government of the Chinese Republic is largely nominal. 

A brief study of the globe will show China lying straight across the Pacific, from the United States, though China reaches a little farther south than does Florida. Not far off the southern coast of China lie the Philippine Islands. 65

CHINA Surface. The great interior of the Chinese Republic is composed of desert highlands. Along the coast there are wide lowlands. These enter inland as a network of fertile river valleys which reach clear to the Himalayas in the south and on the northwest to other ranges. Southwestern 

China is largely plateau cut by steep canyons. 

Coastal Lowlands and River Valleys. Much of China proper occupies the coastal lowlands and the valleys of two great rivers, the Yangtze and the Hwang. The fertility of these river valleys, with their network of branch streams, is what has enabled the dense population to secure its living from the soil.

Interior Desert Highlands. On all sides save the east, there are mountain barriers around China. Tibet rises between China and India in a high mountain mass and desert plateau 12,000 to 17,000 feet high. This desert is, on the whole, forbidding alike to settlers and invading armies, while the Himalayas are, for the most part, impassable. 

Sinkiang, in western China, in the basin of the Tarim River, is also a desert highland.

Within the borders of China there are mountains stupendous in height and all but unknown to any save the Chinese. Such is the black and wind-swept Minya Konka 

Range on the border between China and Tibet. The main peak rises to 25,600 feet. Yet opposite the Minya Konka

Glacier, a Buddhist monastery clings to the slope. Watch towers are still used in this part of the world to keep a lookout for approaching enemies.

Mongolia, on the north, bordering the region of Siberia, rises, in the Altai Mountains, in a plateau from 5000 to 10,000 feet high. This is the highland containing the dreadful desert known as The Gobi. There is some fertile soil, however, in the mountain valleys, up to 8000 feet. The 

Gobi, too, has helped to keep invaders out of China, and to keep the Chinese at home.

CLIMATE 

67 Manchuria, extending on the north to the swamps of the Amur River on the Siberian border, is largely cut off from the sea by Siberia and Japanese Chosen (Korea). Why is this part of the Chinese Republic most likely to be attacked by Japan? 

Climate. Most of China lies within the north temperate zone and has a continental climate. Winters are extremely cold, especially in the north, and the summers are excessively hot. These extremes of temperature are modified near the seacoast. Southern China has a climate somewhat similar to that of Cuba, while that of Manchuria more nearly resembles that of southern Canada. Central China, which has a growing season of at least eight months, can produce a variety of crops.

Most of the rainfall comes in summer; and, in southern 

China, near the coast, there is more than twice as much moisture as in New York. In northern China, on the other hand, there is little more than half as much rainfall as in 

New York. 

The climate is characterized by monsoons, or alternating winds, some of which bring moisture from off the ocean in summer, while winds from the dry interior of the continent blow cold in winter. In midsummer the heat waves of high noon may shimmer blindingly; then suddenly dark clouds roll across the sky, lightning flashes, and the rain comes in torrents. 

Resources. Soil. The yellow soil of northern China is unusually fertile. For hundreds and thousands of years the soil of China's river plains and central lowlands and of her fertile mountain valleys has been cultivated intensively. 

A fertile soil is to be ranked high on her list of resources. 

The mountains of southeastern China have fertile slopes cultivated to an altitude of 2000 feet, while the valleys of the northern mountains to a height of 8000 feet are often planted. This use of steep slopes is made possible by ter-

CHINA 

races of level earth held in place by walls shelf above shelf. 

Thus the soil is kept from washing away. 

It is a soil in which a variety of plants thrive. One sees the flame and rose of the poppy and the rhododendron, both wild and cultivated. Southern China is the original home of the orange and the peach. The lotus has roots and pods that the Chinese relish. Bamboo grows in dense stands, and affords materials from which to make paper, houses, furniture, and boats. The Chinaman uses the tender bamboo shoots as a vegetable.

Forests. China is such an old country that the original forests have disappeared. It is likely that areas now productive of nothing but tough grass were once forested. In 

China some slopes are well wooded with a new growth of trees. Northern China, where the forests have been cut down, is, however, subject to drought.

Minerals. There are rich mineral deposits in certain areas of China, though these have not been developed to any great extent. China has the greatest coal fields in the world, but for lack of transportation some of the best deposits are not worked. Not far from Mukden, in Manchuria, lies a mine ten miles long, with enormously thick seams of bituminous coal. It is said that 7000 tons of fuel are taken daily from this valuable mine. China also has quantities of iron ore, some of it conveniently near the coal fields; but there is little mining save in localities where there is water transportation. There is also an abundance of limestone. The eastern highlands have valuable deposits of pottery clay or kaolin, which probably takes its name from the Chinese word 

Kao Ling, meaning " High Ridge," where this clay is found. 

Salt is one of the most important minerals. Tungsten, the mineral used in making the wires in an electric light bulb, is found chiefly in China, and there is tin and antimony in the mountains. Copper and quicksilver are also mined.

WATERWAYS 

69 Waterways. The rivers and canals are the main highways in China proper. Indeed, throughout the Chinese 

Republic, there are few roads, and these are utterly impassable part of the year, even to coolies and packhorses. Coolies hauling a boat against the current of the upper Yangtze River. 

The rivers and canals of China are not only a means of transportation, but they furnish water for irrigation, as well. 

They must therefore be named as a resource of China. 

They are an important geographical control. 

The Yangtze and Hwang Rivers. Rising in the snow capped mountains of Tibet, these rivers flow across the plains of China to the Pacific Ocean. Follow their courses on the map.

Both streams flood the fertile farm lands at certain seasons, and when the flooding is moderate, it is good for the rice fields. On the river deltas and elsewhere, canals have been dug to drain the crop lands. There are, thoughout 

China, thousands of miles of canals leading in many directions, and the canal system serves, at times, as an outlet for the flood waters of the great rivers.

CHINA 

The Yangtze, China's " great river," is over 3000 miles in length, and in summer ocean-going vessels can ascend it for 680 miles, as far as the city of Hankow. River steamers can paddle even farther inland along this great commercial waterway, and native boats can reach a point 1750 miles from the river's mouth. The Yangtze is crowded with small craft and junks (larger vessels with masts).

The Hwang River is 2400 miles long, but the bars of mud and the summer floods hinder navigation. The rushing current sometimes cuts through the banks, sweeps away villages, drowns thousands of people, covers crops with mud, and sometimes cuts a new channel for itself. From the devastation caused by these annual floods the Hwang is called "The Sorrow of China." The Hwang brings so much fertile yellow soil (loess) from the plateaus that its waters are yellow, and it is sometimes called the Yellow River. The loess colors even the Yellow Sea, into which the river flows.

The Tarim River flows for about a thousand miles through the desert, to lose itself at last in Lob Nor, a district of salt lakes and marshes.

Famines and Floods. The Yangtze and especially the 

Hwang rivers overflow their banks after heavy rains. If the rains are late in coming, and the flooding does not occur in time to soak the ground before the planting of the rice seed, a poor crop results, and so many are the mouths that must be fed that thousands of Chinese go hungry. On the other hand, if the rivers overflow while the plants are getting their growth, the rush of waters may uproot them or the deposit of yellow mud bury them hopelessly. Then, again, thousands are faced by famine and starvation. Too often there are floods so deep and swift that they destroy the levees built to hold them to their courses. Then people by the thousands are drowned, crops are washed away, and famine overtakes those who survive.

CHAPTER VI CHINA'S MILLIONS EFFECTS OF GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS 

We have been considering such geographical controls as China's location, surface, climate, soil, and other resources. 

What is the first result of these controls: by what means are most of her people earning their livings? 

Dense Population. China proper, with an area not much over half that of the United States, is the home of over three times as many people. Its population is estimated at something under 500,000,000, as you will find in the Appendix. That amounts, roughly, to a quarter of the population of the world. Moreover, most of the people

AGRICULTURE 

73 are crowded into the river valleys, where there is fertile agricultural soil. Comparatively few people make their homes in the surrounding dry, mountainous wastes. Farming is China's most important means to a livelihood, though there are also varied manufactures of the sort that require cheap labor.

Agriculture. Though wheat is grown in the north, subtropical crops are favored by the abundant rain in the growing season, coupled with the mildness of southeastern 

China. Tea, rice, hemp, and mulberries for silkworms are important products of Chinese farms and orchards. Cotton, corn, peanuts, soy beans, and tobacco are also grown.

While crude methods of farming, such as fertilizing with garbage, are employed, these methods have served for thousands of years to keep the soil fertile, though it has been cultivated intensively all those centuries. A few well-to-do 

Chinese farmers use American agricultural machinery. 

Many farms consist of but two acres, and there are seldom more than ten acres to a family. These acres are therefore made to yield what the Chinese family requires; but a degree of thrift is practiced that seems incredible to an 

American. No small patch of soil is overlooked. Along the Yangtze Basin, in southern China, where there are warm winters, several crops are grown each year, one ripening while another planted between the rows is sprouting. 

Thus cabbages and wheat may be grown together. A small garden patch is made to yield a succession of garden crops. 

Rice. Paddy (rice) fields occupy one eighth of all the crop lands of China proper. The average year's rice harvest amounts to 1,000,000,000 bushels. The Chinese methods of cultivating rice are generally more primitive than those of the Japanese. Sometimes matting sails are erected which catch the wind, and as they turn, pump water to irrigate the young plants. Since earliest times the Chinese have made irrigation canals, protected by dikes and levees, to

CHINA 

Society for Visual Education, Inc. 

Chinese rice fields planted with foot paths between. bring water to their fields. Many Chinese farmers still thresh rice, as did their forefathers a thousand years ago, by beating it against a bamboo screen. No country raises more rice than China; yet she does not have enough for her hungry millions, and must import some rice as well. In some parts of the country there is a surplus of rice, which is exported. 

It is cheaper to import extra rice from Indo-China or elsewhere than to transport it from distant parts of China to those where it is needed. 

Wheat. Wheat is grown on the northern plains. Here, too, primitive methods are the rule. Sometimes neighbors help one another; but usually the farmer and the members of his own family work the fields, even to the grandmother, who watches for thieves, and the smallest girls, who are required to catch the grasshoppers that might injure the crop. Except in Manchuria there are few farms large enough to hire coolies.

Wheat is still ground in hand-mills, though in the south there is often water power sufficient to turn the mill wheels, and in the north grain may be ground between mill-stones turned by donkeys. Flour is also ground in boat-mills

AGRICULTURE 

99 75 anchored wherever the current of the river is sufficiently swift to turn a water-wheel. The "chow mein we see in America is fried noodles made of wheat flour. Rice grains are cooked whole, but wheat is usually ground into flour. There are few modern flour mills in China and those only in large cities. Hence flour is imported from the 

United States and other countries. 

10,000 acres Equator

200 400 600 2 of Cancer/ 2:9 

Distribution of tea-growing areas of Asia. 

Tea. Tea is one of the products for which English and 

American sailing vessels first voyaged to China. It is the staple drink of the Chinese. It is, in fact, usually used to flavor all the water that is drunk, as such water has to be boiled to purify it. Tea is grown in abundance in the highlands of southeastern China.

Tea is made from the dried leaves of an evergreen bush a few feet tall. In most countries tea bushes are grown on plantations. India, China, Ceylon, Japan, and Formosa all produce tea, and in all these sections of Asia the coolies work for a few cents a day.

Only the youngest leaves at the end of each shoot are picked. The leaves are spread out under sheds to wither.

CHINA 

Packing tea in China. 

Then, in most countries, they are rolled through a machine. 

In China, however, this work, as well as the cultivation, is still done by hand. After being rolled the leaves are treated, to give them a green or a black color, whichever is desired. 

Then they are dried thoroughly by heat, a process that is called firing. The leaves are broken apart and sorted by machinery, according to size. The buds form the best, and the large lower leaves the cheapest grade of tea. The dust, branches, and coarse lower leaves are pressed into bricks, to be sold in Tibet and other parts of the interior of Asia.

Once it has been picked, dried, and bound into packages, some of this tea is transported overland; and there are men who can pack as much as eighty pounds on their backs over the high mountain ranges to Tibet. Quantities of tea are also transported by camel caravan across the desert to Russia.

Enormous quantities of tea are consumed by the natives of Japan and China. The English are among the great tea drinkers in the world. Pekoe tea comes from Ceylon; oolong, from Formosa; and English breakfast and hyson tea

AGRICULTURE 

77 

from China. Under the National Pure Food act of 1906, the importation into our country of adulterated or artificially colored tea has been stopped.

Other Crops. Millet is an important grain crop in northern China. Cotton is grown extensively. Soy beans are planted for the edible oil extracted from them. A white vegetable curd made of the soy beans is a favorite food of the coolies. A plant that the Chinese prize highly as a medicine is ginseng. 

Chinese picking mulberry leaves for the silkworms. 

Mulberry trees. Mulberries grow readily in China proper, and every spring cards of silkworm eggs are secured and

CHINA 

the worms reared till their cocoons are spun. The natives also gather the cocoons of silkworms in the northern 

Hemp. Hemp is cultivated for making ropes and bags.

Extending the Croplands. Vast is the population that must get a living from the land in China. Often a man who has not enough land for his needs will build a raft on the river, erect a hut in which to live, and cover what might be likened to his porches with earth deep enough to grow some food crop while the raft drifts with the current. Some of the shallow lakes along the Yangtze River display artificial floating islands planted to verdure. 

Irrigation is, however, the chief way by which land otherwise fertile is made to produce crops. Mountainsides are terraced for farming purposes, and irrigated. Water for irrigation is usually carried from the streams below by a series of water-wheels equipped with pails or willow baskets. As the wheel nearest the river turns, it carries the water to a tiny canal on a higher level. A second wheel lifts this water to a still higher level, and so on, till the highest terrace has been reached. The water is then made to flow back over the planted slopes. Since human labor is abundant and machinery scarce, man power is often used in relays to keep the wheels rotating, though the Asiatic water buffalo is employed for the same purpose.

Domestic Animals. China is one of the few places in the world where milk is not drunk. As the soil is considered too valuable for pasturage, cows, horses, and sheep are very nearly unknown in China. The water buffalo is sometimes used for plowing the rice fields and as a draft animal. Otherwise, the livestock consists chiefly of ducks and chickens and an occasional pig. No bird has a more gorgeous plumage than the Chinese pheasant. Domestic ducks are numerous. 

Some, like the mandarin duck and Peking duck, have been introduced into the United States and elsewhere. Eggs

MANUFACTURING 

79 dried in quantities sufficient for export are a product of Chinese farms. The Chinese, like the Japanese, use the cormorant to catch fish for its owner. Goldfish were first bred by the Chinese.

Manufacturing. Cheap labor, much of which is carried on in the home, is a determining factor in China's output of manufactures. The Chinese use little machinery, though it has been introduced in the factories of a few large cities. 

The Hair-Net Industry. The making of hair nets gives thousands of girls and women a means of livelihood. The industry has spread, till literally millions of nets are made for the United States and Europe. These nets are of 

Chinese human hair.

When the Manchus captured China in the seventeenth century, the men wore their hair in long braids and forced the Chinese to do the same. Though pigtails have since come into disfavor, many Chinese cling to the custom of their ancestors, and the traveling barbers still find work at the country fairs. A pound of coarse Chinese hair, bleached and dyed, will make two thousand nets. A single net may contain a thousand knots, but a Chinese girl can make an average of ten hair nets a day, and so earn enough for her rice and tea. Most of the work is done in small country villages. The nets are sent to the cities for export. 

Silk. Silk was first woven in China, and for centuries the method was a guarded secret. In time, though, it became known in Japan, India, Italy, and France. Today China. produces about one fourth of the world's supply of raw silk. 

That from the neighborhood of Canton and that from the 

Shantung Peninsula suggest trade names with which we are familiar. That from Manchuria, where the silkworms are fed on oak leaves, is called wild silk, and is used in making pongee. Silk making has long been an important industry in China, and the textile is still woven on wooden looms in the homes of the workers.

CHINA Other Manufactures. Cordage is manufactured of hemp. Cotton milling is a recent innovation, as is the manufacture of iron and steel. Straw matting, also beautiful rugs and carpets, are woven. The presence of immense beds of kaolin (porcelain clay) provides the means for china making. Chinese pottery is noted for its artistic decorations. Large quantities of clay products are made. Pagodas and other buildings in China are constructed of tile. 

P.K. 張​京 

Newman Traveling fourth class, China. 

The making of laces, embroideries, rugs, tapestries, and matches are other industries that depend upon cheap labor. 

Some of the finest embroidery is done by children, for only their tiny fingers can make stitches sufficiently small. The carving of ivory and sandalwood, the decorating of lacquerware, and the making of gold and silver filigree work are occupations which take time and talent. 

Commerce. Most of the trade is controlled by England, Japan, and the United States. The Japanese, because they are of the same race, and are so near, have made tremendous gains in all branches of business in China. In spite of resentment at the aggressions of Japan, which occasionally shows itself in boycotts against Japanese goods, Japanese merchants control a large share of Chinese commerce.

THE OPEN DOOR 

81 Cotton goods are the chief import. Most of them come from England. Other imports are rice, metals, salted and canned fish. China is usually the best customer among foreign countries for our wheat crop. A great deal of kerosene is imported into China from the United States. Silk, both raw and manufactured, beans, bean oil, and bean cake, tin, and tea are the chief exports. 

The rivers and canals are the main avenues of communication throughout China. The chief railroads are north and south lines that connect some of the large cities with the Siberian railroad. They cross the great east-flowing rivers. 

The Open Door. Because of her large population, nations which manufacture wares in great quantities or produce huge crops desire China as a market. The attempts of Japan to take Chinese territory are opposed by other commercial countries, partly because they fear loss of trade. For various reasons China has granted special commercial favors to certain countries. The United States, however, advocates equal rights for all nations in trading with China and other Asiatic countries. This policy is called "The Open Door."

Chinese progress? 

2. What kind of manufacturing is done in China?

CHAPTER VII AN AWAKENING GIANT EFFECTS OF GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS An Ancient Civilization. China is the oldest civilized country in the world. The magnetic compass and gunpowder were in use there long before those inventions became known in Europe. The Chinese were the first people to make porcelain, as well as the first to manufacture paper and movable blocks for printing. The invention of writing marked a long step forward from the ways of barbarism to

AN ANCIENT CIVILIZATION 

83 those of civilization, for with the possibility of reading the records of other ages, it was no longer necessary to trust to memory and word of mouth to hand down the records of the past. The Chinese language is a survival from very ancient times, and is an extremely difficult one to learn. Only a few Chinese can read and write. Instead of learning an alphabet of a few dozen letters, a Chinese student must learn at least three thousand symbols, each of which represents a one-syllable word.

There are records to show that, at the time of the eclipse of 776 B.C., Chinese laborers were cultivating the soil for great land-holders, each of whom controlled enough territory for a state.

During the centuries that followed, China had little contact with the outside world; but in the thirteenth century A.D. the Mongols, led by Genghis Khan, began to invade the country, and under the grandson of Genghis, Kublai Khan, they became the ruling people in China, which was at that time known as Cathay.

During a rebellion in the seventeenth century, aid was asked of the Manchus, and these tall northerners seized the throne for themselves. The Manchu dynasty continued to govern China till the Chinese Republic was formed in 1912. As the change of government was a sudden one, so many of the Chinese people were unprepared for it that there has since been much civil strife. Sun Yat Sen has been called the father of the Chinese Republic. 

Government. China is a young republic, and the national government is not strong. The governors of some provinces maintain separate armies. The peaceable disposition of the people, the divided government, and the weak army put the trade and territory of China at the mercy of other nations. The chief nations of the world, led by the United 

States, have now agreed not to seek special privileges in China.

Society for Visual Education, Inc. 

Audience hall of the summer palace of the Chinese Republic. 

People. The Chinese are courteous, lovers of peace, and as merchants have a world-wide reputation for honesty. 

Most Chinese, however, are farmers. Patient and hardworking, they live principally on rice and soy beans. Rice and tea are supplemented, on the Chinese dinner table, by vegetables and fish and, on feast days, pork.

Education. The Chinese have, for centuries, valued education, and are now adopting modern educational standards. In China, only the well educated can be advanced to high places in the government. Confucius is the most famous character of Chinese history. His sayings are studied in all the schools. 

Emigration. Few educated Chinese go abroad. Most of those who emigrate are coolies. Thousands of Chinese coolies are employed upon the plantations in the Philippines and the Hawaiian Islands, and thousands more in the mines of South Africa. Throughout the United States the Chinese are known as skilled laundrymen, and in the western states many Chinese are employed as house servants. Still others are market gardeners and fishermen. Mongolia and Manchuria have been largely colonized by Chinese,

MODERN CHINESE LIFE 

85 Modern Chinese Life. A young man marries long before he is able to support a family. He takes his bride to his parents' roof, where she renders obedience to her motherin-law, even though the family may be so large that there will not be food enough to go around.

The problem of the average Chinese has ever been, first, that of getting enough to eat. Fortunately, there are fish of all kinds, and even fingerlings the size of sardines are eaten. So, too, are wild mushrooms, certain kinds of seaweed, and the meat of camels too old for the caravans.

Chinese farmers live in walled villages, both for safety from bandits and for sociability; and the typical farmhouse of sun-dried brick has an earthen floor. The dwellings, even of the rich, are practically unheated, but in winter people wear clothing warmly padded with cotton wadding. Wheelbarrows employed on a country roadway near Nanking. 

So frugal are the Chinese that it takes two thousand of their smallest coins to equal an American dollar. 

The wheelbarrow is a vehicle much used in China, and the human burden-bearer employs the carrying-pole, from which two balanced baskets may be suspended. Wellto-do city people travel the rough and narrow roads in sedan-chairs carried by coolies, and carts of produce are often drawn by men instead of beasts.

A Chinese temple, showing the decorative roof often seen. 

Religion. The tourist finds temples everywhere in China. 

There are more Buddhists (believers in Buddha) than in any other country. Most Chinese also worship their ancestors and reverence old people. Chinese boys and girls are noted for the respect they show their parents. 

The Great Wall. If someone on a neighboring planet had a telescope sufficiently powerful to view China, he would see the Great Wall wandering up and down mountainsides and across the plains like a mammoth worm. If this Wall could be brought bodily to the United States, it would reach from Philadelphia to Topeka.

Several hundred years before the time of Christ, the Chinese built a rude earthen rampart that served, at the time, to keep the barbarian Mongols of the northern deserts from overrunning the fertile plains of China. Centuries later, in fact about a hundred years before Columbus, when pikes, bows, and arrows were the weapons of warfare, the present Wall was built of huge rocks to a height of about twenty-five feet, with a road on top. 

This engineering feat, an eighth Wonder of the World, with its 20,000 watch towers and the great gates that closed

THE GREAT WALL 

87 the mountain passes, required the labor of hundreds of thousands of men for years. That Wall still stretches for nearly 1500 miles from the shores of the Pacific into the Province of Kansu, forming a barrier that shuts off Manchuria and Inner Mongolia from China proper. But the 

Great Wall no longer affords security to the Chinese. With bombing planes and heavy artillery the Japanese army from Manchukuo crossed the Great Wall, entered China, and routed its Chinese defenders. Caravans from China to 

Mongolia pass freely save at night-through a gate in the Great Wall at Nankow Pass, and the Peking-Kalgan 

Railway follows this same fifteen-mile gorge.

Foreign Intervention. At the time Japan secured the island of Formosa and the Liaotung Peninsula, the foreign powers intervened between the opponents and forced Japan to give up part of this territory. Again, when trouble with 

Japan arose, at the close of the World War, over the Shan- Newman

A section of the Great Wall of China, twenty-five feet high.

tung peninsula, foreign powers chiefly the United States intervened, and Shantung was restored to China. But 

Japan has persisted in attacks upon China, driven by her need for territory, and encouraged by the lack of national union in China. China and Japan entered the League of Nations, but Japan's continuance in the League is uncertain. 

Cities. China has no cities as large as Chicago. The cities of China are centers of trade rather than of manufacturing. Some of them are treaty ports, where foreigners are allowed to live and engage in business, but many Chinese cities exclude foreigners. 

NANKING, 230 miles inland, where the railroad crosses the Yangtze River, is the capital of China, and the residence of the President of the Chinese Republic. There is a 

Nanking Government Flying School at Hangchow.

PEIPING, formerly known as Peking, was until 1928 the capital. The city has neither commerce nor manufactures; the inhabitants are chiefly government officials and priests. The Oriental beauty of the palaces, pagodas, and temples of Peiping is noted, as is the loveliness of the groves and gardens that surround them. The streets, though broad, are unpaved and littered with refuse. Walled off from the rest of the city is an inclosure called the Forbidden City, where stands the palace of the former Emperors. 

Peiping is a center of learning rather than of industry. 

TIENTSIN, a railroad center and the northern terminus of the Grand Canal, is the port of Peiping and of northern China. It is connected by a branch line with the Siberian railroad, and with the ports of middle China by the Grand Canal. Cotton goods are manufactured, flour is milled, and there is a large trade in rice, beans, salt, and coal. The river front is controlled by the British, Japanese, and other foreign nations, and has many modern improvements. 

SHANGHAI, not far from Nanking, near the mouth of the 

Yangtze River, is the chief seaport and largest city of China.

CITIES 

89 It has a modern "foreign" section, the streets of which look amazingly like those of American cities. There are ten-story buildings, and increasing numbers of automobiles. 

There are jinrikishas drawn by trotting coolies. Shanghai has mills for spinning silk and cotton, polishing rice, and making paper and matches. Here American and European exporters receive the raw silk of the interior for shipment abroad. The exports include rice and raw cotton. 

CANTON, on the Si River, is the great port of southern China, though its harbor is not sufficiently deep for ocean vessels. Here thousands of families live in junks and sampans, like the small houseboat on page 78, moored in the river. Raw silk and cotton are milled in big factories as well as in individual homes, and fireworks are manufactured.

HONG KONG is a British possession eighty miles from Canton, on a hilly island. It is the center of British trade in China and one of the principal ports in the world. About half the population lives in VICTORIA, a city about the size of Seattle. Hong Kong is important chiefly because it reships the exports and imports of the inland cities. 

FOOCHOW is the port for the large tea-growing district between the Yangtze and Si rivers.

HANKOW, 600 miles from the ocean, on the Yangtze, is a manufacturing city, accessible by rail and river boat. 

Mongolia. The high desert plateau of Mongolia consists of bare mountain ranges and The Gobi, called by the Chinese the "Sea of Sand." The region is swept by terrific sandstorms. None the less, it is crossed by a great caravan route from China to Siberia via Urga. Find it on the transportation map.

The nomad herdsmen of Mongolia migrate every spring to fresh grazing grounds with their sheep, cattle, goats, camels, and ponies, and in autumn return to more sheltered regions with their skin tents.

30 A modern iron factory at Hankow, China, on the Yangtze River. 

URGA, the capital, is a town on the outposts of Asiatic civilization. 

Sinkiang. Sinkiang is as little known today as any corner of the earth, though camel caravans from Peiping to India have, for centuries, followed a route across the sands of The 

Gobi, and through Sinkiang. 

The great trading caravans usually march from late afternoon till midnight, that the camels may graze by day, when their owners can keep an eye on them. From midnight till dawn the pack animals are made to kneel near the tents of the travelers. It takes camel freight about three months if it escapes brigands -to reach a Sinkiang border town that is 1600 miles from the railroad. way, it will have crossed passes dreaded for their freezing winds, where camels have been known to freeze fast to the ice as they slept. Where streams exist, they have to be crossed by fording or swimming; and if the traveler is overtaken by a sand-storm, it is "all in the day."

Here, in a desert plateau, the few people live in the oases watered from the little streams that come from the Tian

MONGOLIA AND SINKIANG 

91 Shan and other mountains. It is a land of blistering sunshine, salt swamps, and the dust of fine yellow loess. A town that has lain buried for 2000 years beneath the sand dunes has recently been discovered.

YARKAND is the largest oasis and chief city of Chinese Turkistan, which is part of Sinkiang. 

URUMTSI is the capital of Sinkiang. 

Tibet. This remote land is well named "the Roof of the World." There is no spot on the globe higher than the highest peaks of the Himalayas, which tower like walls about the 16,000-foot tableland of Tibet. The mountains rob the winds of their moisture, though they blow fiercely across the sand and salt of the plain. The few people who manage to live in this chill desert raise sheep and yaks, a kind of cattle. A little barley is grown in the lower slopes, which are irrigated from the mountains. The inhabitants are followers of Lamaism, a religion marked by a belief in Yaks, valued by dwellers of the desert highlands of Asia for their milk, their meat, and the long hair which can be woven into clothing.

evil spirits who must be placated. Their priests are called lamas. China finds Tibet hard to control. 

LHASA, the capital, is a torch-lit stone city dominated. by lamaseries (Lama monasteries). Lhasa is the end of a caravan route from China and India. Two women of Tibet, who live 11,000 feet above sea level, and have to dress warmly even in summer. 

A 28,000-foot peak of another towering range of Tibet,

Amnyi Machen (the mountains of mystery), rivals Mt. 

Everest. Near the headwaters of the Yellow River, 10,000 feet above the sea, a nomad people live in tents of yak hair, and wear clothing padded for warmth. They drive their sheep and ponies from one grassland to another. Το cross the streams, they use rafts of inflated goat skins. In this nearly unexplored country, there are hermit monks who live in cells in sandstone cliffs that remind one of the cliff dwellings in our own West.

MANCHURIA 

93 

Manchuria. This land of the warlike Manchus lies north of the Great Wall of China, and has been likened to our American West. Once all forests and grasslands, the haunt of tigers and other fur bearers, it now has wheat fields, soy bean farms, coal and iron mines, and cities in which modern business houses, as well as ancient temples, are seen. 

With the coming of the Trans-Siberian Railroad, and especially since the World War, Chinese farmers by the millions, together with Japanese and Russians, migrated to this fertile northern land. 

Today railways cross south Manchuria, as you can see by your transportation map. One may ride behind a locomotive built in America, though the Chinese laborers along the way may use that vehicle of ancient times, the wheelbarrow with sails.

MUKDEN, the ancestral seat of the Manchu dynasty, is the trade center of Manchuria. It has colleges established under Japanese as well as Chinese and Christian missionary auspices. Mukden is connected by rail with 

Siberia, Peiping, and Japanese ports.

DAIREN, the seaport of Manchuria, is an eastern terminus of the Trans-Siberian Railway.

Dairen is under Japanese control.

HARBIN, sprung up on the plains of Manchuria, is a little Moscow of the Far East, though under Chinese control, and equipped with American flour-milling machinery. Here, too, Japanese firms have established various businesses.

PART III NORTHERN AND SOUTHERN RIVALS 

For centuries one nation of Europe after another has sought to acquire territory in Asia, as they have on other continents. In this, Russia and Great Britain have been the most successful.

The boundary of European Russia along the Ural Mountains coincides with the boundary between Asia and Europe. 

After acquiring territory in Europe as far west as the Baltic Sea, Russia began to expand eastward into Asia. 

The Ural Mountains offer no barrier to travel. Moreover, Russia was settled by the same tribes which occupy northern Asia. Hence Russia has been able to maintain her sway over northern Asia all the way to the Pacific Ocean.

The British possessions in Southern Asia include India and a number of smaller dependencies of an Empire which encircles the globe. Although the mainland of Europe lies between Great Britain and her Asiatic possessions, the powerful British navy enables that nation to guard the sea routes to southern Asia and keep them open for her merchant ships. This is true also of the routes between the mother country and other parts of the British Empire. 

Both Russia and England have sought to extend their power to other parts of Asia. Thus the safety of the lesser countries of Asia has, in a measure, been preserved because of the very rivalry between these two European nations. 

This is especially true of those countries which lie between northern and southern Asia. 95

Newman This cross was erected by Wrangell, the Russian explorer, in 1821, near East

CHAPTER VIII A NORTHERN WILDERNESS GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS 

The central plain of Europe, with its tundras bordering the Arctic Ocean, its fertile grasslands, its black soil belt, its forests watered by great rivers, merges with a similar region of even greater area in Asia. This northern lowland of Asia is called the Siberian plain. It is separated from the central plain of Europe by the low Ural Mountains; but south of the Urals, as far as the lowlands around the Caspian Sea, the surfaces of the two continents are alike. 

Moreover, these adjoining areas - Russia in Europe and Russia in Asia are parts of one country, the Union of Soviet 

Socialist Republics. 

Location. Russia in Asia is an area perhaps twice that of the United States. It is divided from European Russia by the Ural Mountains, the Ural River, and the Caspian Sea. On the north, it reaches to the Arctic Ocean, and on the east to Bering Sea and the Sea of Okhotsk. On the southeast, this vast territory meets the Amur River, the 96

LOCATION AND SURFACE 

97 mountains, and the deserts that bar off the Chinese Empire. 

On the south, it reaches to the mountain ramparts beyond which lies the British possession of India, as well as Afghanistan, and Persia. Before the time of steamships Asiatic

Russia was a land route to the Indies and China. 

Divisions. While Asiatic Russia includes Transcaucasia, south of the Caucasus Mountains, Turkmenistan, Uzbekistan, Tajikistan, and other divisions in the region of Russian Turkistan and the steppes, the best known region is that of Siberia, extending northward from the steppes and the northern mountains of Asia to the Arctic Ocean and east from the Urals to the Pacific. This region comprises several political divisions, all of which, including other divisions in Asiatic Russia, are controlled in national affairs by the government at Moscow.

Surface. Asiatic Russia is a vast plain drained by four great river systems, those of the Ob, the Yenisei, the Lena, and the Amur. 

Much of Siberia lies in the basin of the Yenisei. Siberia is chiefly a great northern plain, part open plain, part tundra, and part forest, though it rises to highlands on the southeast. 

Tundras. Along the low Arctic coast are treeless plains resembling those along the north coast of Europe and North 

America. They are called tundras. Large parts of the tundras are marshes which are frozen solid in winter. Even during the short summer though the thermometer may rise to 65 degrees, they thaw out only to the depth of a few feet. That is deep enough, however, for masses of quickgrowing plants to spring up and bloom, and for swarms of mosquitoes to breed in the stagnant pools. 

This tundra is productive of little save mosses and such dwarf berries as can spring up during the brief summer. 

Reindeer browse upon the mosses, wild fowl come to nest in the marshes, and feed their young on the abundance of succulent herbage.

A NORTHERN WILDERNESS 

Natives riding across the tundras in summer. 

Taiga. The tundra gives way, on the south, to a belt of dense fragrant evergreen forest more than one hundred miles wide, called the taiga. This northern forest occupies most of Siberia, and no one knows how much tall timber it contains, nor how many wild sables, foxes, wolves, and lynxes live within its shelter. A treasure trove of future lumber and pulpwood, it has almost no means of transporting these materials. Lakes, marshes, and mountain chains intersperse the forests; and toward their southern rim, the pines, firs, larches, and silver birches give way to oaks and other hardwoods, which alternate with farmlands. 

South of the taiga in the middle of Siberia, lies a belt of marvelously fertile black soil. It is here that most Siberian farmers make their homes, where they raise wheat and dairy products.

Steppes. In the same latitude as the black earth region, and extending still farther to the southeast lie high grass-

RESOURCES 

99 lands or steppes, in part a waste of drifting sand and yellow dust. The steppes begin near the Pacific coast and extend westward into Europe. For the most part the steppes are clothed with grass. There the Cossacks, Tatars, Kalmucks, and other tribes pasture their goats, fat-tailed sheep, camels, and small, sturdy horses.

The Stanovoi Mountains in the southeast descend to the broad Amur River Valley. Numerous salt lakes gleam on the plains and plateaus.

Climate. Northern Asia is a land of long winters, howling winds, and drifting snow, followed by the wet springs, hot summers, and golden autumns that characterize the continental climate. In that, Siberia resembles Canada. 

The Arctic Ocean is too icy most of the year to permit navigation. The Kara Sea is open for barely two months each year. In winter, blizzards sweep over the northern lowland, and even as far south as the steppes animals perish by the thousands in these violent snowstorms. The temperature of northern Siberia has been known to fall to 97 degrees below zero. Under these conditions, milk is frozen into cakes, and sold as we sell ice. Yet so dry is 

Cossack village on the Kirghiz Steppe.

A NORTHERN WILDERNESS 

the interior that one feels the cold less than might be supposed.

The rainfall is light, but the thawing of the subsoil keeps the plant roots moist.

The grasslands between the Urals and the Altai Mountains were originally clothed with wild flowers and grasses, and along the streams the wild cherry perfumed the air each spring. This area is now devoted chiefly to stock raising, though there is some farming. 

Resources. Timber. There are millions of acres of timber; but most of it stands unharvested. Some of it is cut by extremely wasteful methods. Quantities are used for fuel. More would be cut for export if it were not for the difficulty of transporting it to a shipping point. Much of the lumber is conveyed to Vladivostok. 

Minerals. The mountains of Siberia are rich in minerals, vast gold mines and a wealth of copper. The Ural 

Mountains contain much of the world's platinum, also manganese, tungsten, lead, zinc, silver, and asbestos; and

Siberia has some of the largest coal resources in the world. 

There are also rich deposits of iron ore. There is sulphur and sodium, and there are deposits of petroleum on islands of the Pacific just off the Siberian mainland. Graphite or plumbago, used in making lead pencils, crucibles, and other wares is mined near Lake Baikal.

An American syndicate has the privilege of mining Siberia's gold for a limited number of years, but the cost of freighting ore is enormous.

Fur Bearers. Sables, ermines, and kolinskies, as well as foxes, squirrels, hares, and wolves are numerous in the taiga. 

The ermines, whose coats turn white in winter, all but the black tip of the tail, and the sables supply the most valuable pelts.

PEOPLE 

101 Samoyeds outside their summer hut, at East Cape, Asiatic Russia. Newman

CHAPTER IX  EFFECTS OF GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS 

We have been considering such geographical controls as the location, surface, and climate of Northern Asia, coupled with its resources of timber, minerals, and fur-bearing animals. What effects of these controls may we expect to find in the lives of the people of Asiatic Russia? 

People. Most of Asiatic Russia is unexplored wilderness. 

The rest is sparsely populated. Most of the people are 

Slavs akin to the inhabitants of European Russia. There are also nomad tribes most of whom are of the Mongolian race or at least partly so.

Short, yellow-skinned Samoyeds keep reindeer and hunt between the taiga and the tundra. The Yakuts dwell along

Cossacks (from the Russian Kazaks), dwellers of the steppes. These people lived in scattered villages and held their land in common, as is the practice throughout the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics. 

the Lena River, where they own herds of reindeer which supply them with milk, meat, a means of transportation, and skins for their huts and clothing. Ostyaks live along the Yenisei River in birchbark shelters, hunting and fishing. 

On the grasslands, other nomadic peoples, including the Gypsies, follow their flocks and herds. In the Altai Mountains and elsewhere along the Chinese frontier live primitive Tatars, many tribes of whom keep sheep and goats, or rear cattle and horses. There are Mongols and Buriats in 

Mongol-Buriat, and Siberiaks, who are half Mongolian and half Caucasian. The Buriats are great fur hunters. The inhabitants of the steppe are chiefly Kazaks, fine horsemen and weavers of handsome rugs. Their common drink is kumiss, a preparation of milk. The Kirghiz Steppe of arid western Asia is peopled by tribes resembling the Turks. The tribes of the desert east of the Caspian Sea make Bukhara rugs and irrigate patches of cropland from the wells of the

OCCUPATIONS 

103 oases. On the steppes of Turkistan in the southwest, huts of adobe have been built around the oases, and fruit trees are grown under irrigation. The Ural Mountains are the home of a people half Caucasian and half Mongolian, who live in felt tents, bake in outdoor ovens, and drive their animals wherever there is grass.

Some of the white Russians now in Siberia, or their ancestors, came as political convicts, chiefly from the upper classes of European Russia; for Russia exiled large numbers An irrigated vegetable garden in southern Siberia. to the hardships of convict settlements. Political refugees also fled to Siberia.

Occupations. In Siberia and other parts of the northern lowland, the inhabitants raise reindeer, hunt and fish in the far north; hunt and trap in the forests; grow wheat in the black soil belt; conduct dairies, and care for grazing animals on the steppes.

In the north, herds of reindeer are easy to raise, since the hardy animals require no winter shelter, and will even paw away the snow to find and eat the mosses and lichens. 

Fishing for sturgeon in the Caspian Sea is profitable.

It is from the sturgeon that caviar, so relished by many

people, is obtained. The roe or eggs of the fish are treated with salt. There are deep-sea fisheries in Bering Sea, off the Kamchatka Peninsula.

In the forests, Siberia's valuable fur bearers are hunted and trapped by the natives and others to supply a large fur market. Furs and gold are transported by way of the Lena River and the highway between Yakutsk and 

Irkutsk. 

The hunting of deer, rabbits, and game birds provides fresh meat for those who dwell in the taiga. 

In the black soil belt, which resembles that of European Russia, there are millions of acres of good agricultural land, part of which is used for wheat growing. Where the land is divided among the peasants, they are trying to learn how to use modern farm machinery.

Collective Farms. Sometimes all the land around a farm village is worked as one cultivated farm. Model farms are operated by the Government, and there are great estates, on which bumper crops are often produced. Besides wheat, soil and climate favor the raising of white potatoes, beets, beans, oats, rye, flax, and a variety of berries.

On the steppes, where the soil is less fertile, dairying is more profitable than wheat growing, and butter is made for export by a number of coöperative societies. 

The grasslands of steppes and mountainsides afford grazing to flocks and herds of sheep, goats, yaks, camels, and horses. Fat-tailed sheep are bred for mutton, and merinos for their fine wool. Hay is harvested to feed these animals through the winter. 

Highways of Travel. Though people use horses, the roads are poor, and in spring and autumn, impassable with mud, and there are few bridges. It is easier to travel when winter snows lie hard and glistening beneath the runners of a sleigh. There are rivers navigable for thousands of miles. Most of these flow into the Arctic Ocean.

HIGHWAYS OF TRAVEL 

105 

The Yenisei River, which flows across northern Asia from the Altai Mountains to the Arctic Ocean. 

Rivers, Lakes, and Seas. The three great rivers of the northern lowland of Asia, the Ob, the Yenisei, and the

Lena, rise on the continental divide between Lake Baikal and Lake Balkhash.

The Ob River, 3000 miles long, is formed by rivers which rise in the Altai Mountains. Canals link its tributaries, and freight is carried overland from its western branches to the eastern tributaries of the Volga River in Europe. 

The Yenisei River, 3000 miles in length, also flows into the Arctic Ocean. It is thirty miles wide in places, and part is navigable when free from ice. The Ob and the 

Yenisei are connected by canals. The Lena River, 2800 miles long, likewise discharges into the Arctic. 

The Amur River, 2700 miles long, rises on the mountain rim of The Gobi, and makes its way to the Sea of Okhotsk. 

Sea-going ships can ascend the Amur to Khabarovsk, and smaller vessels go many miles farther west.

Save where they are frozen, these rivers have been extremely valuable as a means for the cheap transportation

of lumber, furs, and other products of Asiatic Russia. They would be even more useful if they were more closely connected by canals. The Yenisei, with its tributaries, is the main highway for steamboats north of the railroad. But at times, especially in spring, the rivers are blocked with ice. 

Northern Asia, like the other northern continents, North

America and Europe, has innumerable lakes, ponds, swamps, and marshes. Many of them are the remnants of vast inland seas.

Lake Baikal is the largest body of fresh water in Eurasia. 

It is rimmed by mountains and is very deep. In summer commerce is carried on by steamboats; but in winter the ice is so thick that freight is sent across by horse and sledge. 

Lake Balkhash is fourth in size among the lakes of Asia.

It has no outlet and is shallow, but evaporation keeps it from overflowing.

The Caspian Sea and Lake Aral, with the streams that flow into them, are in the western part of the extensive inland drainage basin of Asia. Neither of these bodies of water has an outlet.

The Caspian Sea lies in a vast hollow, and its surface is below sea level. The Volga and Ural rivers flow into it, and there is considerable commerce across the Caspian. 

Though the northern ports are frozen, the Kara Sea in the Arctic, north of the Ural Mountains, provides a route that can be used in August and September. Radio is employed for communications dealing with commerce by this route, and hydroplanes are used to spy out the position of the ice for vessels making trips by the Kara Sea route.

Caravan routes. In the arid portions of Asiatic Russia, horse or camel caravans, with saddle animals, are the customary means of travel and transportation. From caravan routes across the desert, between rivers, comes China tea for

Europe; and if you watch the papers, you may see news of air freight lines in Siberia. Your air map shows that thou-

THE TRANS-SIBERIAN RAILROAD 

107 sands of miles of air travel are already possible in passenger planes, and your map of the principal railroad, steamship, and caravan routes shows how the caravans travel from

Okhotsk to Yakutsk and Irkutsk, and from Peiping to Irkutsk, Omsk, and down through Sinkiang into India. 

Scene along the Trans-Siberian Railroad, which crosses northern Asia.

The Trans-Siberian Railroad is about 5000 miles in length. That is the largest single railroad line in the world.

It is not as comfortable or in as good condition as it might be, but it is still a marvelous thing for the vastnesses of northern Asia. The steel rails cross plains, forests, and swamps, connecting Moscow and Leningrad with Vladivostok and Dairen, on the Pacific coast, by about eleven days of travel. Though this great railroad is connected with all the river routes of Siberia, the area traversed is so great, from north to south, that there is great need of additional railway transportation from the mines, fisheries, and lumber camps, if Asiatic Russia is to reach its fullest development. 

Government. Siberia and the other parts of the northern lowland belong to the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics. 

Asiatic Russia has a high degree of local independence, but national affairs are administered from Moscow, the capital, in European Russia. The Asiatic territory is of untold

value to the Soviet Republic, not alone in its wealth of natural resources, but in the possession of Vladivostok, a seaport on the Pacific, and also their access by rail to the Japanese port of Dairen. The value to Russia of this outlet to the Pacific Ocean is tremendous. Russian peasants, The harbor of Vladivostok, the chief Pacific port of the Union of Soviet 

Socialist Republics. 

finally, have come as immigrants to the lumber camps and fertile farmlands of Siberia. 

Cities. There are a few towns and cities in Siberia, located chiefly along the railroad, over which three trains a week pass to Europe. There are also occasional villages of log houses, each clustered about a bulbous domed church. Where there are not towns, traders hold fairs attended by the country people for miles around. Both town and country districts are, for the most part, more comfortless, more poverty-stricken, than anything we, in America, know anything about. 

VLADIVOSTOK, the chief Russian port on the Pacific Ocean, is farther south than Seattle. It has a fine harbor called the Bay of the Golden Horn, but on account of the severe climate it is icebound for three months in winter, except as it is kept open by ice-breakers. The surrounding coun-

Siberian Railroad connects Vladivostok with Moscow and 

Leningrad. Timber is the leading export.

IRKUTSK, near Lake Baikal, on a branch of the Yenisei 

River, is a center of the fur trade. It is on the Trans- 

Siberian Railroad and the caravan route to China. Irkutsk is connected by air line with an important gold field. It has a library, a theater, a university, and paved streets.

Novo-SIBIRSK, on the Ob River and the Trans-Siberian Railroad, is a center for distributing agricultural machinery. 

It sends butter and frozen meat to the west, has flour and lumber mills, iron smelters, breweries, and other industries. 

It also ships work horses and work cattle bred in western Asia to the farmers and lumbermen who need them. 

OMSK, where several trade routes intersect, is subjected to severe blizzards in winter and hot sandstorms in summer. 

It is the chief city of the steppes. It has sawmills, tanneries, and railroad repair shops, carriage and tobacco factories.

TOMSK, east of Omsk, on a branch line of the railroad, lies in the midst of rich farms and gold mines, on the camel caravan route from the Kirghiz Steppe. It has lumber mills, match factories, carriage factories, porcelain works, flour mills, and factories for pressing oil from pine cone seeds. 

Its cottage industries include the making of sheepskin coats and boots, and metal milk containers.

Turkistan is a region comprising several divisions of Russia in Asia, which lie mostly on the steppes between Siberia on the north, and Persia and Afghanistan on the south. Here are the valleys of the Amu and Syr rivers. 

Turkish Mohammedans formerly ruled the land and their descendants form the wealthy and educated class, most of whom live in the cities; but many Cossacks have immigrated into Turkistan from the steppes. The Trans-Caspian

Railroad joins TASHKENT, SAMARKAND, BUKHARA, and other cities, to European Russia. Another railroad forms a connection between the Trans-Caspian and the Trans-Siberian systems.

Irrigation is in general use, and cotton growing is extensively developed. Other products are silk and wool; and many fine rugs are woven.

CHAPTER X INDIA AND NEIGHBORING STATES GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS 

We have seen that undeveloped and sparsely inhabited northern Asia owes its importance, not to its great size, but to the powerful European government to which that region belongs. So it is in southern Asia. There India and the neighboring parts of the British Empire owe their importance, not merely to vast populations and rich resources, but also to the ambition and power of Great Britain, Russia's rival for influence in Asia. 

Location. India occupies the middle peninsula of southern Asia, between the Bay of Bengal and the Arabian 

Sea. On the north the Himalaya Mountains curve in a towering rampart nearly 2000 miles in length that walls. 

India off from Tibet and Sinkiang. The boundaries, however, reach west of the Indus River to include Baluchistan, and east of the Bay of Bengal to take in 

Burma, on the neighboring peninsula. Off the southern tip of the Indian peninsula, the island of Ceylon is connected with the mainland by a chain of small islands. 

Thus India is isolated by her position between tropic seas and mountain walls. These walls tower from two to five miles in height. Mt. Everest, the world's highest peak, thrusts its icy crown nearly five miles and a half above the sea, and there are several other peaks nearly as high. The map will show how a southward spur of the mountains even separates Burma from Siam, while the mountains of Baluchistan, and of Afghanistan just north of it, divide western India from Persia and Asiatic Russia. 113

INDIA AND NEIGHBORING STATES 

The west coast of India on the Arabian Sea. The harbor of Bombay. 

About half of India, the southern part of the peninsula, lies south of the Tropic of Cancer. Find the southernmost parallel of latitude to which the tip of the peninsula extends. 

Surface. Fertile river valleys, a rugged plateau, dense forests, snow-clad peaks, and a great desert area, these make up most of the surface of India. 

The coastline of India is exceptionally regular, affording few harbors in proportion to its length, save those at the deltas of the great rivers.

With the physical map before you, see how the southern part, peninsular India, called the Deccan, rises from narrow coastal lowlands to an interior plateau. This plateau, which is dotted with extinct volcanoes, rises on the west to mountains some of which are 8000 feet in height, called the Western Ghats (Steps). The plateau slopes gradually to the Eastern Ghats, which seldom rise above 3000 feet. 

The northern edge of the Deccan is bordered by another highland rim, the Vindhya Hills, between which and the Himalayas lies a broad lowland that reaches from the Bay of Bengal to the Arabian Sea. Through this lowland wind three great rivers, the Indus, the Ganges, and the Brahmaputra. Find their wide, fertile deltas on the map, and trace the streams to their sources. Two of them rise north of the 

Himalayas, and cut through the mountains, each in a canyon

SURFACE 

115 wild and deep. Two of them combine to make one great delta. Find which two.

Rivers. The Brahmaputra River, 1800 miles long, rises in a great glacier on the north slope of the Himalayas. It skirts the base of the range, breaks through the mountains, and flows southward into the Bay of Bengal. The lower course is broad and majestic. There are no bridges to interfere with river traffic, or with the floods that water the plantations of rice, jute, and mustard.

The Ganges River issues from a cave in a glacier on the southern slope of the Himalayas and flows into the Bay of Bengal, 1500 miles away, through the same delta as the Brahmaputra. The chief mouth of the Ganges is called the Hooghly. Ocean steamships ascend the Hooghly as far as Calcutta. Although irrigation canals have been built to draw off the high water, the summer floods do much damage.

The Indus River, like the Brahmaputra, has its source in glaciers on the north slope of the Himalayas. It crosses the mountains in a remarkable gap, amid superb snowcapped peaks, but its lower course lies through tropical plains to the Arabian Sea. The Indus discharges through a delta, and furnishes water for irrigation, but there is little commerce except in native boats.

The plains are kept fertile by the annual flooding of the rivers, which bring rich silt from the mountains. In the course of time, so much soil has been washed down from the Himalayas that the soil is almost incredibly deep. 

Burma, to the east, also has a fertile river plain and delta, that of the Irrawaddy River, from which rise wild, jungle-clad hills and mountains.

The Irrawaddy River is 1300 miles long, and flows into the Gulf of Martaban. It is a broad stream, navigable for 1000 miles, and ocean steamships can enter two of the nine channels which comprise its delta.

INDIA AND NEIGHBORING STATES 

Mountain barriers. Below the rocks and glaciers of the high peaks of the Himalayas are grassy slopes where goats and sheep find forage. One such place, famed for its loveliness, is the Vale of Kashmir in northern India. Below the alpine meadows of the Himalayas are dense hardwood forests tangled with vines and underbrush. 

In only a few places can the mountains be crossed. The route best known is narrow Khyber Pass, between India and Afghanistan, which your map will show not far distant from the Vale of Kashmir. Through this pass wind an ancient caravan road, a foot trail, and an automobile road. 

The pass is guarded day and night.

Climate. Between the plains and the tops of the Himalayas can be found, in succession, the climate and characteristic products of every zone between the Equator and the poles. The peninsular part of India lies entirely in the Torrid Zone. Except on the coastal lowlands and the low alluvial plains, however, the temperature is somewhat moderated by the elevation of the mountains and plateaus. 

Hence, in parts of India, it is cool enough for growing wheat. 

There are but two seasons, the rainy one beginning the last of May, and the dry season which begins in November. The monsoon winds of summer come blowing moisture off the Indian Ocean, and part of the country has an abundance of rains. (Read page 28 again.) During that season, the lowlands are unhealthful, and Europeans throng to the Vale of 

Kashmir and other health resorts on the slopes of the Himalayas. People also find the higher parts of the Deccan, though in the Torrid Zone, enjoyable.

These monsoon rains, borne by winds from the southeast, are heaviest where they must rise over the mountains, especially in Ceylon and Burma, and on the southern slopes of the Himalayas. Very little moisture is precipitated by the winds that reach the low, hot plain of the Indus Valley in the west. That is an irrigated desert area. The Hima-

RESOURCES 

117 layas cut off the winds from the north, but the northern slopes of the mountains are left almost entirely dry by the winds off the great desert plateaus of central Asia. Thus the northeast winds of winter, blowing from the arid interior of Asia, are dry winds, but an occasional cyclonic storm brings rain.

Varied Resources. India has a wealth of forests, rich soil, minerals, and the extensive irrigation projects by which certain areas are kept watered when the rains fail. 

100,000 acres

200 400 600 

Equator of Cancer

Distribution of wheat-growing areas of Asia. 

Minerals. Coal is the leading mineral product. There are rich petroleum wells, and salt is obtained. Rubies are mined, and jade, a beautiful stone, sought by Chinese carvers, is mined in Burma. Graphite is mined in 

Ceylon. 

Fertile soil. The great flood plains and deltas of the Indus, the Ganges, and the Brahmaputra rivers and of Burma's 

Irrawaddy River are remarkably fertile. When the mountain snows melt, the rivers are flooded and, in places, deposit rich mud where they overflow their banks, thus fertilizing the crops. There is also fine agricultural soil on the

INDIA AND NEIGHBORING STATES 

Deccan plateau, as well as between the Ghats and the east and west coasts of peninsular India. 

Irrigation. Extensive irrigation projects exist in many parts of India, for the northern plains, especially, are subject to drought. At such times the crops fail, there is famine, and danger that people may starve to death by the thousands. This used to be more true than it is since the British have built railroads that carry produce into the famine-stricken areas. A camel operating an irrigation pump in western India.

66 Irrigation is possible (1) wherever streams from the mountains bring water that may be stored in tanks," reservoirs or ponds made by damming streams, and (2) wherever there is underground water that may be pumped from wells. In certain areas dry land crops are watered from mountain streams, enabling numbers of people to earn a living from the land.

Irrigation has been practiced since earliest times in this part of the world. Where the rivers fail to supply enough water, wells are sunk to underground waters fed by the melting snows of the mountains. The water in these wells is pumped into canals and ditches supplied

FORESTS 

119 with water gates that may be closed when the water is not needed in the fields. In dry years, millions of acres may be watered from these wells. The Ganges River supplies an irrigation canal over a thousand miles long. 

The British government has done a great deal for India in building huge irrigation tanks to store the flood waters, and canals to distribute this water to the millions of little farms that need it. There are thousands of tanks in India. 

Even parts of the arid Baluchistan Plateau are irrigated for the growing of fruit. In India proper, the warmth of the lowlands makes it possible to grow crops under irrigation during even the dry winters. 

The rice fields are also irrigated, even where there is abundant rain. Do you know why?

Thus there is irrigation employed at times of drought, also the irrigation practised in permanently dry areas, and the irrigation employed in the rice fields. Altogether, India irrigates an area even larger than the irrigated area in China.

Forests. Fully one quarter of India is heavily forested. 

In the dense forests of the lower slopes of the Himalayas, there are tropical hardwoods of great value. The same is true of parts of the Deccan, where rainfall is abundant. 

The Indian forestry service guards the timber areas against fire, and reforests cut-over areas by the planting of thousands of trees every year to replace those cut down. 

Teakwood is one of the most valuable products of these forests. A durable wood that will not warp nor corrode iron, as does oak, it is much used in shipbuilding, as well as in laying floors and making furniture. 

When teakwood logs are taken out of the hill forests of Burma, they are floated down the rivers to the sawmills, and there handled by trained elephants, which can lift these heavy logs on their tusks, steadying them with their trunks.

INDIA AND NEIGHBORING STATES 

Ebony is also lumbered. It is usually black, and takes a high polish. It is especially valued for piano keys. 

There are also thick stands of bamboo, a wood that combines lightness with sufficient strength for certain purposes. 

Among the best known trees of southern Asia are the banyan tree and the deodar, a kind of cedar. The banyan is a sort of fig tree. It does not bear edible fruit, but is 

10,000 tons

200 400 600 2. 

Equator 

Distribution of rubber-producing areas of Asia. planted for shade. The tree grows to a height of 100 feet. 

Besides its underground roots it has many air roots that grow down from the branches. The air roots become additional trunks to prop the ever-spreading branches, which eventually cover a space 200 to 300 feet in diameter. 

The fibers of the bark are made into rope; the large leaves are used for plates; the sap is made into birdlime, and the gum is used in making lacquer, a fine varnish.

Rubber is another valuable tree. It is found chiefly in great plantations on the island of Ceylon, to which the British introduced seedlings from Brazil a few generations ago, and in hot, wet Burma, where it has been planted in clearings of the jungle. When the trees are about five years of age, they are tapped for the liquid rubber (latex) of their sap, which is dried and pressed into rolls for shipment. 

Animal Life. India's swampy forests are the home of the intelligent Indian elephant, numbers of which are tamed and made to work, and the buffalo, which is related

ANIMAL LIFE 

121 to our domestic ox. The trees are the home of leopards and monkeys. Hordes of monkeys chatter in the palm groves, gather about the temples, and even carry on their pranks in the city streets. The tropic Deccan has cobras and many varieties of poisonous snakes. As the native superstition forbids killing, thousands of barefoot people die each year of snake bite. Fortunately, there is native to India a ferocious little yellow-gray animal called the mongoose that is talented in killing snakes, as well as rats. It is a wonderful sight to see the lithe creature, scarcely more than a foot in length, facing a poisonous serpent, and finally darting in to seize it by the back of the neck and kill it. The grasslands were once the hunting ground of the great Bengal tiger, striped that it might hide A cobra, one of the poisonous snakes commonly seen in India. It is known by the curious spectacle-like marking on the back of its neck, and its power to expand the neck, when angry, till the skin forms what is termed a hood. amid the yellow sunlight and black shadows; but these animals have been hunted into more and more remote hiding places as the grasslands have been converted to agriculture. Cheetahs, sometimes called "hunting leopards, are tamed in some parts of India, and taught to hunt for their masters. 

---

INDIA AND NEIGHBORING STATES 

A Bengal tiger that has caught a sheep.

Tame Elephants. When wild elephants are to be cap tured, a stout stockade called a keddah is first built. Men mounted on tame elephants, accompanied by beaters, then go in search of a wild herd, the beaters surrounding it and helping to drive it toward the keddah. Then what a dust arises! The wild elephants roar in protest and alarm, baby elephants squeal, keeping directly beneath their mothers, as if her four pillarlike legs could fence them within the bounds of safety. As the funnellike entrance to the keddah is neared, the beaters shout, beat tom-toms, and blow horns on all sides but that one, till the wild herd is afraid to venture in any direction save the one enclosed by the upstanding logs of the enclosure. Once within, they are left to learn that the keddah will not give way for them. 

Some time later, when they are comparatively calm, and rather hungry, the men on tame elephants enter and select those they wish to keep and train. These are usually fairly young specimens. Lassooing these with ropes, they allow the rest of the herd to go back to the jungle. Then the mounted men take a wild elephant between each pair of tame ones, reward it with food and water for doing their bidding, and in a surprisingly short time have a new

TAME ELEPHANTS 

123 trained animal to show for their pains. Their elephant charges are well fed and cared for. They especially love to go swimming.
