The Taj Mahal. 

It is built of white marble, and its interior set with jewels and made more decorative with screens carved like lace. 

CHAPTER XI LIFE OF A BRONZED WHITE RACE EFFECTS OF GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS 

Agriculture. India has, for thousands of years, raised its own food; and agriculture of a primitive sort is still the chief industry. The majority of the natives of India are farmers. Nine families out of every ten live in rural villages and cultivate each its own small patch of soil. 

Many a farm has no more than five acres, but will produce more than one crop a year.

PRODUCTS 

125 Grains. Rice is the staple food crop, where there is the heat and moisture necessary. The river deltas, flood plains, and coastal lowlands are ideal for rice. 

Millet is the grain on which people live where it is too dry for the production of rice, as it is in the western Deccan. 

Certain fruits, particularly the mango, grow in the same areas. 

Wheat does well where it is cool in winter, as on the high and irrigated land of the Deccan, and in the northwestern provinces of India. It is shipped from Karachi, a port on the Arabian Sea not far from the delta of the 

Indus. 

Fibers. Cotton, which requires heat, but little moisture, is the great export crop of the Deccan. It thrives best of all on the plateau behind Bombay, the great port on the west coast of the peninsula. For generations India has sent her raw cotton to Great Britain, where part of it is milled and returned to India as cotton cloth for native wear. India is, however, coming to have cotton mills of her own. Cottonseed oil forms an important by-product. 

Cotton also grows well in upper Burma. 

Jute is a plant native to India that grows to about the height of Indian corn. The fiber obtained from the stalk is used to make burlap wrappings for bales of cotton, and to make burlap bags for produce. It is extensively used in making carpets and rugs. The wet lowland of the 

Ganges delta is where jute thrives best. 

Linseed Oil. Flax is grown in India for the oil that is pressed from the seeds, as well as for the fiber from which linen is woven. The oil is called linseed oil, and is used in making linoleum, paints and varnishes, printer's ink, and certain medicines.

Rubber. Several kinds of rubber-producing trees are grown on plantations of the wet lowlands of Ceylon. British growers introduced the first rubber plants from Brazil.

Tea. Tea plantations have been established by the 

British on the hillsides of Ceylon, the tropic island just off the southern tip of India. Ceylon tea leaves are usually cured at factories built on the plantations. Tea is also grown on the warm lower slopes of the Brahmaputra valley. Picking tea on the lower slopes of the Himalaya Mountains. British 

India leads the world in the export of tea, and Ceylon ranks second. 

Domestic Animals. Most of our domestic animals came originally from Asia.

Cattle are reared as draft animals to draw the plows and the two-wheeled produce wagons of India. Zebus are the common domestic cattle of India. They have prominent humps. One or two bullocks or zebus, or water buffalo on the rice lands, are usually found on each little farm. 

Religious scruples forbid most of the people to use beef or

MANUFACTURING 

127 even milk as food. Yet India has a good deal more than twice as many cattle as we have in the United States, and exports their hides. 

Tame elephants are used when princes ride. They are also trained to work in the lumber camps and lumber mills. 

Large numbers of goats and sheep are kept in the mountain meadows, chiefly for wool and hides. Camels are reared as pack and saddle animals in the arid parts of India. 

Planting rice in Ceylon with water buffaloes.

Manufacturing. Though India has millions of skilled hand workers, the British have also established numbers of factories equipped with modern machinery. The coal mines in the Deccan are therefore of great value in providing the power needed by factories and railroads.

Courtesy India Railways Bureau 

Making clay toys, one of the hand manufactures of India. 

Cotton clothing is worn by both men and women in India's warm climate. The cotton cloth sent from England is therefore supplemented by cloth milled at Delhi and the cities of the Deccan, near where cotton is grown. 

Calico takes its name from the city of Calicut, and there is another cotton material named for Madras. Jute is manufactured into bagging and other products in Calcutta. Can you think why? Silks are woven on hand looms. Carpets and rugs are made in handsome designs. 

Some of the carpet yarn is made of jute, some of wool. 

Shawls exhibit all the artistry of the native hand looms. 

The soft wool of the goat of Kashmir is the material of the exquisitely soft and colorful Cashmere shawls prized by our grandmothers and still worn in India. 

The workmen of India show much artistic skill in making brass and copper utensils, in designing jewelry of gold and silver wire, and in the exquisite carving of wood and ivory.

COMMERCE AND TRANSPORTATION 

129 

Commerce. England controls the trade with India. The chief exports are cotton and jute, both raw and manufactured, rice, tea, hides and skins, seeds, wheat, coffee, and lac. The imports are chiefly cotton goods, machinery, hardware and cutlery, and sugar.

Transportation. For long centuries India's only connection with the outside world was by the caravan routes across the high passes of the Himalayas, and that, only at times of year when these were not choked by snow. Then came the sailing vessels, which eventually gave way to steamships plying between Europe and the Far East. 

Today the difficult caravan routes across the high passes of the Himalayas and The Pamirs are still the only roads to the interior of the continent. But there is an air line between London and India; and there are local airways between the cities of India. There is telegraphic communication with Europe by way of a submarine cable beneath the Red Sea. Your transportation map shows railway and steamship lines between the chief cities of India. 

Not least important, there are long, navigable rivers, some of which are connected by canals. 

Population. India represents one of the great civiliza tions of early times, a civilization based on agriculture. 

There are, today, more people in India than in any other country of the world, with the exception of China. In round numbers, there are about 300,000,000 souls. (The 

Appendix gives the exact figures.) The majority live close together along the great river plains, so that the density of population is very great in these areas. Few people, of course, live on the bleak plateaus of the upper slopes of the high Himalayas.

Most of the people of India are of an Aryan (white) race that have, through the centuries, become deeply tanned by the sun. (The name Aryan is worth looking up in Webster's Dictionary.) More than half the people are

Keystone View Co. 

A temple at Maduva, India, the towers of which are covered with carved images. 

Hindus (people of the Indus River), from which the name of the country itself is derived. There are also a number of Mohammedans in north central India whose warlike ancestors invaded India from Arabia. In remote parts of the Deccan there are dark-skinned Dravidians whose ancestors lived in southern India before the Aryans came to the peninsula. There are also a few hundred thousand English people in India, government officials, or soldiers of the 

British army.

Let us see what has happened to a white people whose ancestors for thousands of years have dwelt in a climate. for the most part too hot for strenuous effort. With the exception of the few whose ancestors had the luck or the ability to rise to place and power, the people are poor and illiterate. It has taken their entire energy to wrest a living from even fertile soil. A few of the race are, however,

RELIGION 

131 highly educated. The racial stock is one of good mental capacity. Moreover, the British are constantly establishing new common schools.

There is no national tongue in India. Instead, there are between 150 and 200 dialects. Of these, Hindustani is the best known, while Bengali probably ranks second.

Religion. The Hindus worship Brahma and many lesser gods. Their scriptures are called the Vedas. There are also 

Parsis of Bombay. The Parsis came originally from Persia, bringing with them a belief that the sun is typical of the Creator. worship fire as one of the emblems of the sun. They millions of Buddhists and Mohammedans in India. The bible of the followers of Mohammed is called the Koran. 

The Caste System. According to the Hindu religion, people are divided according to race and occupation into classes or castes. The highest caste is composed of priests

and their descendants, called Brahmans. The caste system divides the people of India into five main groups, priests, warriors, merchants and agriculturists, servants, and “untouchables." The last named are chiefly the dark-skinned 

Dravidians whom the white race found in ancient India. 

No intermarriage is permitted between these castes. Each caste has its strict rules as to food and many other personal matters, and those who break these rules become outcastes.

The five main castes contain many subdivisions. To give but one illustration out of hundreds, there is, in central India, a group called by a name that means sportsmen. 

These are divided into those who use firearms, those who use snares, those who tame the hunting leopards of India, and those who employ bullocks to stalk game. The British in India have tried to establish the principle of justice. to all, regardless of caste.

Government. India is a possession of Great Britain, and the King of England is also Emperor of India. About a third of India is divided into Native States, each of which has its native rajah or local ruler. The rest of India is divided into provinces. The representative of the British 

Government who rules India in national affairs is called a Viceroy in the Native States, and a governor-general in the provinces. Some of the native leaders have been demanding independence, but the mother country does not believe that India, as a whole, is ready for it. This is a matter which has caused much disturbance in India, and of which you may, at times, see news in the papers. 

Native Life. India is known to have had a highly developed civilization long before Europe was settled. It is possible that Aryan peoples first made their way into India as much as four thousand years ago, conquering the native tribes but refusing to permit intermarriage with them.

NATIVE LIFE 

133 In the days of Columbus, the great explorers tried to find a new route to India, partly for her jewels, but chiefly because her spices were in demand to preserve meats and season unattractive food.

The British East India Com- 

One of the handsomely carved temples of India. In this one, the home of the monkey god Hanuman, a drove of monkeys is fed. pany, coming originally for purposes of trade, became a power in India about 1757. This was followed by the conquest of India by the British. The British Government has since been active in establishing schools, railways, and irrigation works.

Hindu snake charmers. Whatever their secret, the weird music of these showmen appears to charm poisonous reptiles into swaying rhythmically. 

The British are trying to stop the practice of child marriage in India. A girl's betrothal is sometimes arranged by her family before she is born. Another native custom is that upper class women live in seclusion, seldom showing themselves on the streets. The native dress is one well adapted to a hot climate. For the poor, it consists chiefly of loose folds of cotton cloth. The native princes, however, attire themselves for state occasions in rich fabrics and gorgeous jewels, and many of the Hindu temples are richly carved and decorated.

Hindu showmen perform many curious feats, some of them based on hypnotism and mind reading. 

The native foods of India are usually spicy. A sauce much relished in India and Southeastern Asia is one called curry. It is hot with pepper and other spices, and flavored with garlic. Curry is especially good with such a dish as rice.

CITIES 

135 

Cities. Considering the vast population of India, the cities are few in number. Bombay, on the west coast, and 

Calcutta, on the east, are the leading ports.

BOMBAY, on the west coast, is India's largest city. It is built on an island joined to the mainland by a causeway. 

The city has a beautiful land-locked harbor five miles wide and over twice as long. Bombay is India's great cotton port and the chief center of the textile industry. Dyeing, tanning, and brass and silver work are among its other industries. One finds modern buildings as well as the little booths of the older streets.

Among the wealthy and educated residents of Bombay are many Parsis, or fire worshippers. The Towers of Silence are a gruesome sight. Here the Parsis, in accordance with one of their religious beliefs, expose the dead to the vultures. 

Bombay, India.

CALCUTTA is India's second largest city. Located be tween eighty and ninety miles inland, on the Hooghly River, a branch of the Ganges, which has been deepened for ocean steamships, it stands in the midst of the crowded

(C) 

Chowringhee Road, Calcutta, where one finds clubs and hotels. rice fields of the delta of the Ganges and Brahmaputra rivers. Calcutta is the terminus of numbers of canal and river boat lines and railroads. This enables it to tranship goods en route from Europe and Africa for Australia and the Pacific coast of Asia.

The principal jute mills of India stand on the outskirts of Calcutta, and jute is one of the leading exports. 

A tablet in a city park marks the site of the Black Hole, a small room into which, during a native revolt in 1756, 146 English prisoners were locked till 123 died of suffocation.

MADRAS, the third largest city of India, is a port on the east coast protected by sea walls built out into the Bay of Bengal. Much cotton is woven here and cotton, hides, and other products of India are exported.

CITIES 

137 RANGOON, near the mouth of the Irrawaddy River, is the capital and chief port of Burma. It is connected with a petroleum pipe line from the interior, as well as rail and river transport. There are lumber yards, paddy mills for husking rice, and petroleum refineries, and the docks are piled with teakwood and bags of rice for export. On a hilltop in Rangoon stands a pagoda covered with pure gold. 

It enshrines religious relics. 

DELHI, the capital of India, is a prosperous railroad and trade center built on the midst of the Indus-Ganges river plain of northern India. It has many lovely palaces, and shops containing delicate hand-carved ivories and colorful embroideries. Near Delhi stands a beautiful tower 238 feet high, built of white marble and red sandstone. 

Keystone View Co. 

The Council House at Delhi, with English and native troops on parade. 

The Indian legislature has a Council of State and an Assembly. 

SIMLA, the summer capital, stands on a cool spur of the 

Himalayas at 7000 feet.

AGRA, not far from Delhi, is a city of palaces. Here the beautiful Taj Mahal, a white marble mausoleum in which lies buried the wife of a Mohammedan ruler, was built in the seventeenth century. This lovely structure, set in a garden of surpassing beauty, is famous for its beauty. 

BENARES, on the Ganges River, is one of the most ancient cities in the world. It is considered a holy city by the Hindus, and the Ganges is believed to be a sacred stream.

$11 The ghats at Benares, where pilgrims seek to wash away their sins. 

Pilgrims from all over India gather at Benares for the religious festivals, and bathe in the Ganges as a religious rite. 

The city contains many hundreds of mosques and temples. 

Benares is the center of the brass and copper industry. 

KARACHI, a seaport on the Arabian Sea, is near the mouth of the Indus River. Its chief exports are cotton, wheat, hides, and wool.

INDIA'S NEIGHBORS 

Afghanistan. Just beyond Khyber Pass lies Afghanistan, a small monarchy controlled to a degree by Great Britain on the east, on the north by Russia. It is a buffer which protects India from attack by way of Turkistan. The

CEYLON 

139 monarch of Afghanistan, called the ameer, has absolute power in local matters.

The home of several Mohammedan tribes, the Afghans raise fruit and rear sheep amid the semi-arid mountains of the Hindu Kush. Here caravans from 

Persia laden with Oriental rugs wind along the steep trails to India, to return with products like tea and cotton cloth. 

Baluchistan. The British protectorate of Baluchistan, politically a part of the Indian Empire, is south of Afghanistan. It is ruled by tribal chiefs and policed by British troops. A land of arid plateaus and mountains, it is inhabited chiefly by a few nomad peoples who follow their camels, sheep, and goats from one bit of grassland to another. Baluchistan also protects India by acting as buffer. 

Nepal and Bhutan. The two small kingdoms of Nepal and Bhutan, on the southern slopes of the Himalayas, are also under British control. They lie entrenched amid deep canyons and untraveled slopes between India and Tibet. 

CEYLON 

Ceylon rises from hot coastal lowlands to a mountainous interior. The delightful tropical island is a bit of India broken off from the peninsula. Politically, however, Ceylon is a separate colony of the British Empire. It produces tea, rubber, graphite, and sapphires. 

The waters of Ceylon, the Persian Gulf, and the Sulu Sea produce the pearl oyster, which is much larger than the common oyster. Pearls are formed because a tiny worm or some other foreign body finds its way into the shell. To protect itself the oyster imprisons the intruder in a covering of pearl. 

Some pearl divers wear diving suits. The native diver, however, does not; he is able to hold his breath in water an incredible length of time. He dives down to the sea bottom,

The harbor at Colombo, Ceylon. 

 fills the basket at his waist, and swims up to the boat.

Pearl divers are in constant danger from sharks and cuttlefish. When the oysters are landed, they are left in the sun until they open. The natives then search carefully for the precious gems. The workmen are constantly under guard to prevent stealing. At the end of the season the pearls are sold at auction.

COLOMBO, a port city of southeastern Ceylon, exports the products of the tea and rubber plantations, and imports rice from Burma and elsewhere. Colombo is a port of call on the route between Europe and the Far East via the Suez 

Canal.

PART IV MINOR COUNTRIES OF OF ASIA 

Two thirds of Asia is owned by four nations; eastern 

Asia by China and Japan, northern Asia by the Union of

Soviet Socialist Republics, and southern Asia by the British Empire. The rest of Asia, amounting to about a third of its area, may be divided into two sections, which occupy the southeastern and southwestern parts of the continent. 

These areas are divided into numerous countries, colonies, and mandates. Most of them are small and unimportant and are controlled by European countries. 

India, the middle peninsula of the three which project southward from Asia, is far more important politically than the other two because it is part of the powerful British 

Empire. The peninsula of Indo-China lies east of India, beyond the Bay of Bengal, and with the adjacent archipelagos makes up Southeastern Asia. This section shuts off the Indian Ocean from the Pacific. West of India and the Arabian Sea lies Southwestern Asia. It consists of the countries which occupy and adjoin the peninsula of Arabia and the peninsula of Anatolia. Southwestern Asia lies between the Arabian and Red seas to the south and west and the Mediterranean, Black, and Caspian seas to the north. 

Southeastern and Southwestern Asia differ greatly in surface, climate, products, and people. Southeastern Asia has a heavy rainfall, which produces dense vegetation; and, as this region extends farther south, many of its products are those of the tropics. Its people are Indo-Chinese and 

Malay. In contrast, Southwestern Asia has a light rainfall, and much of its surface is desert. Most of this region is in the Temperate Zone, with characteristic vegetation. 

Large areas are devoted to grazing. The people, Arabs, Turks, Persians, and others differ widely from those to the eastward. 142

DIVISIONS 

143 One of the thousands of floating houses on the Menam River in Siam.

CHAPTER XII A GREEN JUNGLE GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS 

Divisions. The southeastern peninsula of Asia, lying between the Bay of Bengal and the China Sea, is called 

Indo-China. Properly speaking, it includes Burma; but we have already considered Burma as a part of the Indian Empire. The remainder of the peninsula consists of Siam on the west, and French Indo-China on the east. The strip of land between Siam and Burma and the islands known as the East Indies is the Malay Peninsula. The southern part includes the Federated Malay States and the Straits Settlements. Singapore is the city at its southern tip, on the

A GREEN JUNGLE 

Malacca Strait. This is an important location, for through this strait pass most of the ships that have come through the Suez Canal, as well as those from India, bound for China, Japan, and the Philippines. The East Indies help to sepa rate the Indian Ocean from the Pacific.

The region we study as Southeastern Asia includes Siam, French Indo-China, the Federated Malay States, and the Straits Settlements, all on the mainland of Asia, and the islands of the East Indies. 

The chief's house of a village of Papua built over the water. 

The East Indies are the world's largest group of islands. 

Their area far exceeds that of the part of Southeastern Asia which lies on the mainland. A great portion of Southeastern Asia consists of the seas and straits which separate the islands. The islands of the Malay Archipelago curve like a huge crescent around the Malay Peninsula. 

The larger islands of the group are Sumatra, Java, Borneo, Celebes, and Papua (New Guinea). Find these places on your map, as irregular in outline as so many ink blots. 

Sumatra, lying directly across the Equator on the other side of the world from us, is nearly as large as all the New Eng-

EAST INDIES 

145 land and Middle Atlantic states together. Borneo may be compared in size to Texas, while Java has about the same area as the New England states. 

Sumatra, Java, Celebes, part of Borneo, and a number of the smaller islands are together called the Dutch East Indies. They are the possessions of the Netherlands. 

There are also countless smaller islands, such as Bali, a night's cruise east of Java. The Philippine Islands are also a part of this group of islands, but have been treated in another volume in connection with the United States, to which they belong politically.

Distances on the mighty continent of Asia are greater than you might suppose. With your map scale, estimate the number of miles between Bangkok, Siam, and Singapore. 

Location. Southeastern Asia lies entirely in the Torrid

Zone. All of the mainland section, and about half of the islands are north of the Equator. 

Wallace's Line. The edge of the continental shelf which marks the boundary between the two parts of the East Indies, and separates Asia from Australia, is called "Wallace's Line." The western islands rest upon the continental shelf, and hence the seas and straits that surround them are shallow. These islands properly belong to Asia, although separated from it by water. The soil resembles that of the mainland, and the flora or plant life includes the same trees and other forms of vegetation as Asia. 

The animal life of western Malaysia includes such Asiatic animals as the tiger, elephant, rhinoceros, and ape.

The eastern islands, on the other hand, rise from the bottom of the deep sea. There the plants and animals are like those of Australia.

There are giant lizards in the volcanic lesser Sunda Islands in Malaysia, great lizards up to 200 pounds in weight, and formidably armored with scales underlain by plates of bone.

A GREEN JUNGLE 

Surface. Indo-China has a rocky backbone of mountains which are steep on their eastern side above the coastal lowlands, but on the west, descend to a chalky plateau and sandstone hills. Here, near the Yangtze River, which flows down the northern slopes, the Mekong River begins its southward journey of nearly 2000 miles. This river, roaring through rapids to its low coastal delta, waters a land as fertile as any known to man. The delta forms part of 

Cambodia and most of Cochin-China, which you can find on your map. Another great delta is that of the Songka 

River, which extends over most of lower Tonkin. One of the horned houses of Sumatra, built to shed heavy rains. 

The East Indies were all formed by the volcanic action. of past ages. Java alone has nearly half a hundred volcanoes, the highest of which towers over 12,000 feet above the sea. Among the volcanoes still active in the East 

Indian Archipelago are Mount Bromo, on Java, where

CLIMATE 

147 sulphurous fumes and vapors roar almost steadily, and Semeroe Mount, which still smokes and flames. 

Climate. Lying between the Indian Ocean and the South 

China Sea, no part of the southeastern peninsula is far from some large body of water. Instead of trade winds, Indo- 

China has monsoons, as do other regions of Asia near the Equator. There is a hot, wet season and a hot, dry season, though the mountains because of their high altitudes are cooler than elsewhere. The monsoon from the southwest blows from April to October, and brings rains to the northern part of Indo-China while the southern part has its dry season. The monsoon from the northeast blows from October to April, and the seasons are then reversed. In the wet season, there are daily rains, and sometimes typhoons, while the temperature is above 80 degrees, even at night. 

During the dry season the nights, at least, are cooler. 

The mountainous Malay Peninsula is a hot rain forest tangled with vines and undergrowth and inhabited by wild beasts. Here and there clearings have been made for the native rice fields or a rubber plantation. 

The East Indies, lying along the Equator, have a tropical climate, hot and rainy. As one result, there are luxuriant forests. Coconut palms and rubber trees grow wild. It is no unusual thing to have it rain for forty days on end, in the East Indies. But, much of the summer, the rain comes only in the afternoons and the dawns are calm with opal-tinted sunrise, the forenoons bright with sunshine. 

There are violent thunderstorms.

Resources. Dense forests of valuable hardwoods, important deposits of tin and petroleum, and an extremely fertile soil are the leading resources of Indo-China and the East Indies. The region has, however, with the exception of Java, only a small population to farm the soil. 

There are large tin mines on several of the islands, and on parts of the Malay Peninsula. Most of the world's tin

CELEBES 

A comparison of tin production in Southeastern Asia with that of the rest of the world.

Forests. The tropical forests of this region have ideal conditions for growth, of which the most important is abundant rainfall. Teak, rubber, and logwood grow wild. Sandalwood, with its pungent scent, and camphor wood are also cut for export, and kapok, the cottony product of another tree, lighter than cork, is collected to stuff life preservers and to make soft mattresses, pillows, and upholstered furniture. 

The "cotton" of the kapok tree grows in pods, and in Sumatra monkeys are trained to pick it for their masters. 

The cinchona trees of Java provide most of the world's quinine. Certain palms yield oil, and there are pepper and mulberry trees. Silkworms are found in a wild state on the mulberries of Tonkin.

ANIMALS 

149 Logwood is one of the chief dye woods, and a great deal of indigo is harvested. The indigo plant is steeped in vats to obtain an extract, which is then beaten to bring it in contact with the air, which causes it to turn blue. The use of natural indigo is, however, giving way to the use of cheaper chemical (coal tar) substitutes. 

Bamboo is abundant and much used in building native houses. A tough grass more than head high-good for thatching roofs and palm trees are other products of the jungle. Rattan is a species of palm useful in the weaving of one kind of furniture. Palm-roofed cottages as tiny as play-houses nestle among the tree ferns and banana trees of the tropic shores, flowering lianas (vines) droop from the branches of the trees, and orchids grow wild in the lush jungles behind the villages. Nature provides an easy means to shelter in these tropic isles. 

Wild life. In the forests of Indo-China live herds of wild elephants, tigers, and the deer they prey upon, An elephant hunt in Siam. Wild elephants are easily tamed.

A GREEN JUNGLE 

rhinoceroses, and wild boars. The forests of Laos are the hiding places of wild buffalo, to say nothing of enormous snakes and gorgeous butterflies.

Among the East Indian birds is a species of swift, whose nests are gathered by the natives for export to China, where they are used to make the expensive birds'-nest soup. 

The orang-outang, a large ape, lives in the forests of Borneo and Sumatra. The orang-outang is one of the apes that resemble man.

One of the most destructive animals is the flying fox, an enormous bat, with wings that spread five feet. By day, the flying foxes hang head down from the branches of trees. When night comes they fly in flocks to the plantations and forests, to feed on fruit. The natives kill them for food, as well as to protect their crops. The coast waters of the Indian Ocean are the home of the dugong, an animal which resembles the sea cow or manatee. The Malays relish the flesh of the dugong. The bones of this animal take a polish like ivory, and from the hide leather is made.

CHAPTER XIII  EFFECTS OF GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS 

Fishing boats with white sails and double outriggers ply the blue waters between palm-wreathed islets, passing cliffs over which foam silver waterfalls, and white beaches curved like the new moon. Wood smoke rises from thatched villages. The fishermen are after golden carp, a favorite food of the little brown people of the Malay 

Peninsula and neighboring islands. They build their outriggers by extending spars from the sides of their canoes Entire fleets of these to help balance in rough waters. canoes, tied together by long lines, go out together, dangling white leaves in the water to scare the fish toward the nets waiting alongshore.

Agriculture. Rice is the staple food crop, though large quantities of sugar, coffee, spices, and tobacco are grown in 

Southeastern Asia for export. For these products, the rich

volcanic soil and the hot, wet climate of the region are excellently suited.

Rice. The great crop of southeast Asia is rice, and Siam and Indo-China are among the world's largest rice producers. 

Cochin-China alone exports over 1,000,000 tons in an average year. The natives of the Dutch East Indies, however, have to import part of their supply from China and India. Harvesting rice in Siam, where warm rains favor the cereal. 

Knowing that most rice is grown with its roots under water, where would you expect to find it? The fertile deltas, the level river plains, and coastal swamps are ideal for this crop. Rice is, indeed, the leading product throughout most of this region. Small farms are the rule, and it is the effort of each farmer to produce as large a quantity as possible from each acre. Nor does he waste even, the bran left from milling his rice; he saves it carefully for cattle feed. Women work side by side with men, and in many places the women operate the crude husking devices with their feet.

RICE 

153 Rice is also grown in small amounts by the hill people. 

This variety is known as upland rice and can be grown on land as dry as that used for wheat.

Rice, fish, often with a high seasoning, and the wild fruits of the region solve most of the food problems of this part of Asia. Where a farmer lives too far inland to secure sea food, he frequently raises carp in an artificial pond.

Sugar. Sugar cane is a plant requiring deep, fertile soil, preferably volcanic soil, such as that on Java; and Java ranks second only to Cuba as a producer of cane sugar. 

Here in the lowlands there is a continuous growing season free from frost, with the combination of hot sunshine and frequent heavy showers. There is also the abundance of 

Javanese girls picking the cherrylike berries of the coffee trees.

cheap labor necessary to raising this crop with profit. 

The cane is grown on large plantations, usually owned by Europeans or by the Dutch government, where heavy and costly machinery can be used; and these plantations have their own mills for crushing the brown juice from the stalks, straining and evaporating it. The cane is taken to the mills in wagons drawn by zebu cattle. Java has nearly 

200 sugar mills. Usually the sugar is exported before it is refined. The crushed cane that is left is used for fertilizer. Java exports much of its sugar crop to the Nether lands. 

Coffee. Coffee can be grown in but very few places, usually on the edge of the tropics, where there is no frost, but rather, a season of abundant rainfall, followed by a dry period in which the beans can be sun-dried. It also requires good drainage, such as that found on hillsides. It thrives best in red volcanic soil, which is rich in iron. These conditions exist in Java, which has produced coffee of world-wide reputation. Java coffee is shipped first to the 

Netherlands, and there our supply is transshipped. 

The Dutch East Indies, as a group, rank next to Brazil and Colombia in coffee production. On the mainland, part of Indo-China also cultivates coffee for export, besides which, most of the hill tribes grow coffee for use at their own tables. Where coffee is grown for export, there are large plantations. These are equipped with expensive machinery and great platforms for drying the coffee beans and grading them according to size. These plantations are so huge that the native workmen are usually housed in villages built within plantation boundaries. 

Tea. The natives also drink a great deal of tea, which grows wild in Indo-China; and there are tea plantations on the mountainsides of that peninsula. Women pickers carry the tea leaves to the packing houses in huge hampers strapped to their backs.

SPICES 

155 

Spices. Pepper, nutmeg, ginger, and cinnamon are crops that require hand labor and the sort of climate found in the Malay Peninsula and the East Indies. Singapore is the world's great spice port. Spices used to be more important in the days when Europeans lived largely on meat and had no way of preserving it save by a plentiful use of spices. Pepper is exported in the natural state (not 

Picking pepper berries from vines in the Malay Peninsula. ground). So also are nutmeg, which is the kernel of a fruit, cloves, which are the flower buds of a tree abundant on the Molukka islands, and cinnamon, which is the inner bark of a small tree that grows in Indo-China and elsewhere in 

Southeastern Asia. 

About half of the spice trade of Southeastern Asia is in pepper. The pepper which we know most commonly in the ground state is the small round berry of a shrub or climbing vine that is usually trained up a pole, like a bean vine. For black pepper the berry is gathered while still green, unripe, but it turns black when dried in the sun or before a wood fire.

For the less biting white pepper, the berries of the same plant are gathered when red and ripe. The so-called red pepper is, however, the product of a different plant. Pepper is especially abundant in Borneo. Java and Madura export well over half of all the pepper used in the United 

States. 

Tobacco. The tobacco plant grows well in the Dutch 

East Indies, and is grown on large estates owned chiefly by European and Chinese companies. One plantation in Sumatra is so immense that it has fully eighty big drying sheds. 

A tapioca factory at Singapore.

Other agricultural products of this region are copra (dried coconuts), palm oil, cassava (tapioca), and cacao.

There is some breeding of cattle, buffaloes, horses, and hogs.

Forestry. The cultivation of rubber plantations and the lumbering of teak and of valuable cabinet and dye woods. provide a second means of livelihood for the natives of 

Southeastern Asia. 

Rubber. The product from this part of the world that is probably most important to us in the United States is

RUBBER 

157 rubber, for it is from the Malay Peninsula and certain of the East Indies that we get most of our supply. 

The rubber tree demands a hot, wet climate and rich soil, and its sap is best secured where there is a supply of cheap labor, in areas not too far from seaports. The tree 

Palms of the East Indies; a traveler's palm and coconut palms. grows wild in the jungle, but a finer kind is cultivated in great plantations, especially in British Malaya, and to some extent in Java and Sumatra. As in Brazil, a deep V is cut, each morning, in the bark of the rubber tree, that the sap may flow into the tin cup fastened directly beneath the incision. 

In the open spaces between young rubber trees, coffee is often grown.

Teak. We have learned of the teak forests of Burma. 

The tree grows both wild and in plantations in the land to the east of Burma, and in western Java. It is highly valued. 

Green teakwood is so heavy that it will not float; but a unique method of drying it out (so that it is lighter) has been discovered. About three years before a tree is to be felled, a ring is cut clear around the trunk through the sapwood. This kills the tree.

If the forest thus dried out escapes the forest fires of the hot, dry spring, the logs are felled, and dragged by trained elephants to the nearest creek. The first rains of summer transform every stream into a raging river that floats the logs thunderously down out of the hills. Once in the big rivers, the logs are made up into rafts, which drift along to the mills at tidewater, or are pushed along by swimming elephants directed by men in dugout canoes.

Teak is used as a cabinet wood in the Orient, as are ebony, ironwood, and sandalwood.

A Snake Farm. The jungles are so full of poisonous snakes that the Siamese Red Cross Society makes serums to cure snake bite. For this purpose, it has established at 

Bangkok, Siam, one of the world's few snake farms. Here cobras and vipers are reared in enclosures surrounded by concrete walls, where the creatures sleep within small concrete caves. Once a week their venom (poison) is taken from them and used on horses, whose healthy blood develops an antitoxin or serum that combats the poison. This serum usually cures the natives who have been bitten by snakes, if they secure it within an hour of the bite. 66 

People. The brown race, chiefly Malay, is the one native to most of this part of the world, though Chinese coolies are often brought to the big tobacco plantations to do manual work, and there are large colonies of Dutch, French, and English white people. The natives of Indo-China are either 

Mongolians or a mixture of many tribes,

MALAYS 

159 The wild tribes of Borneo are not Malays, nor are the 

Papuans of New Guinea. 

Malays. The Malays are short, slight people, with round heads, broad faces, flat nostrils, and eyes set rather slantwise. Their skin is brown, their hair black and straight. 

The Malays are thoroughly at home on the water. 

Malay sailors (Lascars) are daring. Their chief weapon is the creese, a short sword with a wavy blade. The seas used to be infested with barbaric Malay pirates, but piracy has been effectively discouraged by the English and other white peoples.

The Malays love bright colors, and both men and women wear sarongs, or pieces of cloth wound about the waist like skirts, the men's being not quite knee length. Loose trousers for the men and caps or head-kerchiefs are other characteristic items of attire. The women who can afford to do so have the most gorgeous jewelry and headdresses, but bare feet are the rule.

The Malays love gambling and avoid work. But then, they find it easy to supply themselves with rice and fish, the small amount they need, and to build roofs to shed the rain. Their siesta, or afternoon nap, is, of course, a necessity of the climate. If they wish to live along the rivers where there is danger of floods, they build their huts on piles. In 

Siam, especially around Bangkok, many people live on houseboats. The Malays are Mohammedans. 

In the highlands of Sumatra, the natives have small farms, collect rubber, and dry copra. They sometimes make boat-shaped houses. Market day is the great event of the week. Then women in holiday attire carry their produce, to town, live chickens in cages or even cabbages balanced on their heads. Along the coast, many go to market in canoes.

The women of a village often get together to clean their rice in community sheds. They grind it in hand mortars

Javanese women doing batik work on cloth. The word batik means painting with wax.

The wax is applied to the parts of the cloth designed to resist the dye bath. Differences in design and color are used in different parts of Java. to remove the husks. When flocks of hungry birds descend upon the rice fields, the highland farmers of Sumatra climb poles and shake rattan devices, the noise of which frightens the birds away.

One of the interesting arts of the region is the colorfully designed cloth made by the Battak women of Sumatra and the natives of Java. What we call batik work is familiar in the United States. Sumatrans are clever wood carvers and often decorate their steep-roofed houses with grotesque painted carvings. 

Wild Tribes. In Borneo, New Guinea, and the Solomon

Isles, the natives are savages. Natives who live in the wilds of Borneo build large log houses, and the entire tribe dwells in one building, perhaps a hundred feet long, with a hallway down the middle, each family having its own small apartment. The savages of New Guinea and the Solomon 

Isles dwell high in trees, drawing their ladders up at night lest cannibals and enemy head-hunters get them.

EAST INDIES 

161 The natives of the dense rain forests of New Guinea wander from the mosquito-infested swamps to the steep mountainsides. They fish, hunt wild pigs, with bows and arrows, use coconut shells for bowls, and grow sweet potatoes. Much of New Guinea and Borneo has never been explored by white men.

Prehistoric Ruins in Indo-China. Cambodia, in Indo- 

China, has many monuments of ancient times, the most famous of which are the ruins of Angkor Thom. Here, before historic time, stood a splendid city with many carved temples. One religious shrine, Angkor Vat, till recently hidden and deserted amid jungle growth, was built in a pyramid that rises by steps, but its presence there remains a mystery.

Government. These picturesque natives are not independent. Southeastern Asia, with the exception of Siam, consists of colonies, each ruled by a European nation or by our own country, as will be seen by the text that follows. 

THE EAST INDIES The East Indies include the Sunda Islands, Borneo, the Philippine Islands, and the Molukka or Spice Islands. These islands, with the Malay Peninsula, are often called Malaysia. 

We have already named the Philippine Islands as belonging, at present, to the United States, though they may become independent in the near future.

A part of Borneo, which is the largest island in Malaysia, belongs to Great Britain. Half of the small island of 

Timor belongs to Portugal. All the rest of the islands form a colony of the Netherlands. We have already mentioned them as the Dutch East Indies. In population they are seven times as large as the mother country; in area they are nearly sixty times as large. For centuries the East Indies have poured untold wealth into the lap of Holland, so that to them is largely due her importance among the nations.

Java. By far the most important of the islands owned by 

Three quarters of all the people the Netherlands is Java. 

in Malaysia live in Java. In area it is about equal to New 

York state, but it has almost four times as many inhabitants. 

Java is well supplied with railroads, telephone and telegraph lines.

Life in Java. The Javanese (natives of Java) culti vate farms that average no more than three acres apiece, on which grow their rice, corn, vegetables, and bananas.

Java and Java's neighbor countries, China and India.

The culture of Java came originally from India, and there are Hindu temples, a well-developed irrigation system, puppet plays and shadow shows based on historical legends. 

The people have dance dramas, which picture demons, and demigods perform to the music of drums and xylophones. 

BATAVIA, Java, the capital of the East Indies, is a busy port. Next to Singapore, on the mainland, and Manila, in the Philippines, Batavia is the chief city of Malaysia.

STRAITS SETTLEMENTS 

163 The irrigated rice terraces of the hillsides of Java. 

The Dutch originally built the city on swampy land, but disease-bearing mosquitoes made it most unhealthful. Today everyone who can lives in a neighboring suburb, and business structures are following. Side by side with modern motor vehicles, one sees two-wheeled carts where people sit back to back with the driver. American motion pictures, enjoyed by the natives, as well as by the small Dutch colony, are shown in Batavia.

STRAITS SETTLEMENTS 

The Straits Settlements, the Federated Malay States, and five small states not included in the federation, are British possessions at the southern end of the Malay Peninsula. They lie next to the British possessions of southern Asia.

SINGAPORE is the chief city and leading port of southeastern Asia. The flags of all nations fly in the open roadstead of Singapore Harbor, which is therefore called "the

Singapore, a port controlled by Great Britain, because of its importance on the steamship routes of British trade. crossroads of the East." Its sun-bleached wharves are so busy with most of the world's rubber, pepper, tin, all the products of the tropic islands, that a causeway has been built to the railway of the mainland. Singapore has what is probably the world's largest tin smelter, also tin mills. 

There are also canneries where pineapples are preserved. 

FRENCH INDO-CHINA 

The possessions of the French in Asia are larger than France itself. The French colonies and protectorates occupy the eastern part of the great southeastern peninsula, and are bordered on the west by Siam and Burma. 

Rice, the chief crop, is the only considerable export, while

SIAM the principal imports are hardware and cotton goods. Most of the trade is with France.

HANOI, in Tonkin, is the capital of French Indo-China. 

It is surprisingly modern for an Oriental city, has wide streets, well drained, paved, and lighted, several native colleges, and the university of Indo-China. Rice is the chief export. 

SIAM Siam is a kingdom which England and France have agreed shall be independent. It lies between French Indo-China and Burma. The Mekong River forms part of the boundary between Siam and French Indo-China. Siam is a tropical country, whose forests are full of big game, and the so-called white elephant is regarded as sacred. The people are farmers.

BANGKOK, Siam, has canals busy with gondolas. The 

Siamese princes have been educated in Europe, and some have traveled in the United States. As one consequence, the Throne Hall at Bangkok is a handsome structure, resem- 

In Bangkok, Siam, on the peninsula of Indo-China.

bling buildings of the Occident. The native huts are built on high supports as a protection against floods. 

Bangkok has a large rice and lumber trade.

CHAPTER XIV  GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS 

The southwestern part of Asia is like a bridge connecting Europe, Africa, and Asia. It has been the scene of many invasions from one of these continents to another. It was crossed by the most ancient caravan routes, and is now traversed by important railroads. 

Location. Southwestern Asia extends westward between 

Europe and Africa, lying altogether farther west than the most eastern part of Europe. It is much farther north than 

Southeastern Asia. Southwestern Asia is almost surrounded by water, the Caspian and the Black seas, the Aegean and the 

Mediterranean, the Red Sea, the Indian Ocean, and the Persian Gulf. The Black and Aegean seas are joined by the

L.L POATES CO., N.Y. 

60 strait of the Bosporus, the Sea of Marmara, and the strait of the Dardanelles. These waters form part of the boundary between Asia and Europe, and are part of the ring of waters which encircle Southwestern Asia. The Isthmus of Suez, which joins Asia and Africa, has been cut by the Suez Canal. 

Surface. Southwestern Asia consists mainly of plateaus, which are part of the line of highlands that rise across Asia

CLIMATE 

169 from Bering Strait to the Mediterranean Sea. As you can see by your relief map, it includes part of the Plateau of Iran, west of Afghanistan and India and south of the Elburz Mountains; the vast Plateau of Arabia, and the small Plateau of Anatolia, which occupies most of the peninsula of 

Asia Minor.

The physical map of Asia shows the elevation of different areas of Southwestern Asia. The Plateau of Arabia lies between 3000 and 4000 feet, the Plateau of Iran at about 5000 feet, and the Plateau of Anatolia ranges from 3000 to 

5000 feet. Mt. Ararat lifts a snowy crown boldly above the Plateau of Anatolia. Here, according to the Bible, Noah's Ark grounded after the deluge.

The lowlands of Southwestern Asia consist mainly of narrow coastal plains and the large river plain between the plateaus of Iran and Arabia. There are also some areas below sea level.

Climate. Your maps of Asia show Southwestern Asia lying chiefly in the Temperate Zone, though the southern half of the Arabian Peninsula is in the Torrid Zone. 

In general, it may be said that the rainfall of Southwestern Asia is scanty, as you can see by your rainfall map. 

It is barely sufficient, in some areas, for grasslands. Other large areas receive so little rain that they are deserts. In 

Persia and Anatolia, fortunately, the melting snows of the mountains provide water for irrigation. Southwestern Asia as a whole is in the belt of northeast trade winds. By the time the trades reach these plateaus, they have lost the moisture they brought from the Pacific Ocean. Hence Iran and Arabia are mostly desert, though in winter northern Arabia and the adjoining regions receive a little rain from the prevailing westerlies. 

Deserts and Grasslands. Southwestern Asia may be divided into two sections, the semi-arid north, consisting mainly of grasslands, and the dry south, which is mainly

Southern coast of Arabia at Aden with sea in background. desert. The monsoon blows across the southern deserts, however, and brings some rainfall. There are exceptions to the general dryness of both sections. Hence there are favored places in the mountains where the vegetation is luxuriant, and the same condition is found on the narrow plains which skirt the water boundaries in the north. 

The narrow coastal strip along Syria and Palestine has a Mediterranean climate. Then along parts of the southern coast of the Arabian Peninsula, the monsoon rains are sufficient to make it a region where some agriculture is possible. It is in this part of Arabia that one finds the frankincense groves, the fame of which was known in ancient times. This frankincense is sent to India for use in the temples, and so adds to the prosperity of Arabia. 

Even on the deserts the climate is cooler than it would otherwise be because of the altitude of the plateaus. 

Drainage. One would not expect to find many large rivers where the climate is so dry. Anatolia has more rivers than the other plateaus, but they are short and unimportant. Some streams flow to the sea through gorges

DRAINAGE 

171 which they have cut in the slopes of the plateau. Since these streams carry away needed water from the plateau, and dry up when the rains are over, they are of little value to agriculture. Valleys through which such streams make their way are called wadies.

There is one important valley in Southwestern Asia, that of Mesopotamia, in Iraq. The word Mesopotamia means "between the rivers." A typical wadi, with a garden in the background. 

The Tigris, 1150 miles long, and the Euphrates, 1800 miles long, are twin rivers that rișe in Asia Minor. Their combined valleys form a flood plain of wonderful fertility. 

That is Mesopotamia. Near the sea the Tigris and Euphrates unite in one stream, which discharges through a delta into the Persian Gulf.

Find the Caspian Sea, north of the Plateau of Iran, on your maps. With Lake Aral of the northern lowland, the

Caspian Sea belongs to the chief inland drainage system of Asia. It receives the waters of the Volga and the Ural rivers. Yet it has no outlet. It lies in a vast hollow, with its surface eighty-six feet below the ocean level. The sea, which is 760 miles long and from 100 to 300 miles wide, is important as a highway for the commerce of the neighboring countries. It is also important for the enormous hauls of sturgeon, herring, salmon, and lobster that are made.

Within the boundaries of smaller interior basins, there are other salty inland seas, such as the Dead Sea in Palestine, whose shores, 1300 feet below sea level, are the lowest land surface upon the earth. 

Resources. There is much grazing land in Southwestern 

Asia. That and petroleum are, indeed, the chief resources of the countries under consideration, though there are limited amounts of agricultural soil. 

Minerals. There are enormous deposits of petroleum in 

Southwestern Asia. Around Baku, on the eastern coast of the Caspian Sea, in Azerbaijan, one of the republics within Transcaucasia, there are immense oil fields belonging to Russia. Baku itself comes within the borders of Asia. 

There are important oil fields in western Persia, not far from those of northern Iraq. Although Iraq was a mandate of Great Britain, the League of Nations had decreed that the petroleum must be shared. There are, therefore, oil companies from the United States, the Netherlands, France, and Turkey developing this valuable resource. The British have been especially active in building pipe lines and refineries and developing the oil wells of both Iraq and Persia.

Of other minerals, the resources are largely undeveloped. 

There is marble, but it hardly pays to transport it by camel

FORESTS 

173 

caravan. The Dead Sea contains valuable chemical salts. 

The Persian Gulf contains rock salt. There are turquoise mines in Persia which are worked in a primitive manner. 

There is also some coal in Turkey and Syria, although very little is mined.

Power. Hydro-electric power, which will be a very valuable resource to the people of Palestine, is being developed around the Sea of Galilee. 

An oil well at Baku, near the Caspian Sea.

Forests. Southwestern Asia, as we have seen, is a treeless region because the rainfall in most parts is barely sufficient for the growth of grass and forage plants, and in the deserts is not enough even for that. However, some places, especially the mountain slopes which face the sea, receive enough rain to permit the growth of trees. Such are the Elburz 

Mountains of North Persia as well as ranges in the middle of the plateau of Iran which are reached by the monsoons.

Many slopes of Anatolia are heavily forested, and in Palestine, west of the Jordan, there are wooded hills on which grow oaks and cedars, including the cedars of Lebanon mentioned in the Old Testament. These should therefore be included as geographical controls of Southwestern Asia. 

Cedars of Lebanon, like those named in the Bible. 

Soil. The most fertile part of Southwestern Asia lies in the valleys of the Tigris and Euphrates rivers. It is believed that the Garden of Eden was located at their juncture. 

Other valleys and plains in this part of the continent, although not so large as Mesopotamia, have rich soil. Even

ANIMAL LIFE 

175 the desert soil is productive in oases places where there is water.

Irrigation. Because of the dry climate, it is necessary to water the farms by irrigation. Fortunately, there is water available for the purpose. Since ancient times, ditches and dikes have been used in irrigating the croplands during the growing season, and underground canals were built thousands of years ago. Nowhere else, except in 

China, do such ingenious engineering works supply the water denied by a scanty rainfall. 

The government of Iraq operates several canal systems which irrigate 1,000,000 acres. Tribal groups and individuals operate canals which irrigate 1,000,000 acres more. 

Animal Life. As in every region where there is little food and long distances must be covered, the wild animals are chiefly those capable of speed and those which can feed upon the scrubby pasturage of the stony or sandy soil. 

The camel. Native to the deserts of Asia, the Arabian camel, which has one hump, is the most common, and also the most valuable. It is never found wild nowadays, however. 

The camel can endure thirst and hunger, for it has a pouch to store the water drunk at the last oasis, and the hump stores nourishment in the form of fat. Camels can eat the dry, thorny plants of the desert, and their spreading hoofs do not readily sink in the loose sand. So tall are they that they must be made to kneel to receive their burdens. The dromedary is so fleet that it can carry a rider from fifty to a hundred miles a day, and can keep up that rate of travel for a week. At a slower pace, the common camel can carry a load weighing a quarter of a ton or more. The camel is often called "the ship of the desert." 

The camel is useful in many ways. Its milk is drunk by the desert people, its meat is eaten, its hide is made into leather, and its hair is woven into the finest and warmest kind of shawls, rugs, and cloth for tents and clothing.

A herd of young dromedaries. 

Not only in Arabia, but throughout southwestern Asia, the interior of China, and in some parts of Asiatic Russia, the camel is used as a beast of burden. In eastern Iran and in Central Asia, especially, a camel is used which has two humps. In winter its hair grows long and shaggy. 

Some herds of these camels still live in the wild state. 

Fine-haired animals. Some animals of Southwestern 

Asia have peculiarly soft, silky hair. Persian cats, angora cats, dogs, and the goats of Kashmir and adjoining parts of the Plateau of Iran are noted for the beauty of their coats. Most of the goats of this region, however, are of ordinary breeds, with coarse hair.

EXERCISES 

177 Horses. Fine horses are extensively bred, and some of the herds are very large. The thoroughbred horses of Arabia have the reputation of being the best in the world. Their beauty and intelligence have been celebrated for centuries. 

Fisheries. There are Caspian Sea fisheries owned jointly by Persia and Russia. Pearl fishing is conducted in certain areas of the Persian Gulf.

CHAPTER XV  GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS 

We have been considering such geographical controls as the location, surface, and climate of Southwestern Asia, and its resources of soil, water for irrigation, animal life, grazing lands, petroleum and other minerals. What would
