GRAZING 

179 you naturally expect the effects of these controls to be? 

How do the people of this region secure their livelihood? 

Herding is the chief means of livelihood in the grasslands of the drier area; but along the Mediterranean coast, in 

Mesopotamia and elsewhere, the people get their livings largely off the farmlands.

Grazing. Herding and grazing are, naturally enough, the leading occupations of the people who gain their livings from the grasslands. They rear sheep, goats, horses, and camels, and so produce quantities of wool, cashmere, mohair, and camel's hair, as well as hides.

Just as in primitive times, the herdsmen of the lands from Persia to Palestine allow their animals to feed on the open ranges. They lead their flocks and herds from one area of grassland to another, moving their tents and other belongings, as the need arises. Usually they migrate up the mountain sides as the grass turns green in spring, then return to the sheltered valleys for the winter. Thus a large number of the people of Southwestern Asia are nomads. 

In all of the grasslands of Southwestern Asia, flocks of sheep are the stand-by of the herdsmen. More sheep are reared for their wool than for their meat. There are also countless herds of goats. Although goat flesh and milk are used as food in the tents and other homes, common goats are kept principally for their hides, from which morocco leather is tanned. Angora goats are shorn like sheep. 

The long, silky fleece of the Angora goat is called mohair. 

Persian lamb, broadtail, and caracul, prized in the United

States for women's clothing, are the hides of Persian sheep and goats dyed black. Herds of horses, camels, and cattle are pastured throughout Southwestern Asia, but Syria makes a specialty of cattle, as Arabia does of fine horses and camels.

A breed of sheep commonly bred in Southwestern Asia is the fat-tailed sheep, the tail of which grows so huge and

heavy that the animal must find it a burden to drag about. 

The meat of this fat tail is highly esteemed in the countries around Anatolia.

Agriculture. Most of the farming of Southwestern Asia is still done in the primitive manner. For example, the crooked limb of a tree forms the rude plow, the fields are laboriously cultivated by hand, and where there is no buffalo, donkey, or camel to draw the plow, the farmer and his wife may do so.

In many sections the grain is ground in the type of handmill used centuries ago. In Iraq modern harvesting machines are used on a few estates. In Turkey, however, there is a good agricultural school, and a model farm, and the government is encouraging the farmers in many ways. The fertile plains are irrigated as they were in the days of their former greatness, so that they produce grains in abundance, and fine cotton. The British have not only encouraged cotton growing, but have provided a market for the crop.

From Persia to Palestine, agriculture means chiefly the growing of wheat and barley, olives, grapes, figs, dates, and other fruits. Both Palestine and Persia produce cotton and tobacco, and Turkey grows excellent tobacco. 

With a climate not unlike that of southern California, Southwestern Asia produces large crops of such fruits as prunes, apricots, and peaches. In fact the fine peaches and melons from which our own peach and melon seeds were originally secured, came from Persia. The date palm grows wild in the oases of the Arabian desert, or can be made to grow there by planting it in holes where the roots can be kept moist. The date is the staple fruit of the Arabs. 

Throughout the fertile oases there are extensive groves of date palms, and large quantities of dates are exported. The fine Smyrna figs one sees in American markets are grown in 

Turkey. Indeed, throughout Southwestern Asia, figs and dates are an important crop.

MANUFACTURING 

181 

Manufacturing. The manufacturing of fine Oriental rugs, dyed and designed by clever hand workers, and easily transported, is one result of the rearing of grazing animals. 

The wool is woven into cloth for tents and saddlebags. 

Persian rugs are regarded as the finest. There are fully thirty different kinds of rugs, for each district has its own 

Women weaving one of the handsome "Oriental " rugs of Persia. distinctive style of design. Shiraz rugs, Tabriz, and 

Kurdistan are named for the places where they are made. 

Persian rugs are made with one kind of knot, Turkish rugs with another. Indeed, all Oriental rugs are made with one of these two types of knots. The design nearly always has a border, and within it a field on which figures are arranged. 

The colors of the dyes are usually dark and rich. Indigo and madder provide lovely blues and reds. The white, brown, and blue are often undyed wools. Camel's hair provides the tan and brown. The figures may be symbolical or naturalistic. There may be representations of flowers and trees, hunting scenes, and animals. Small designs are often arranged in diamond-shaped medallions. The weaving is done chiefly in the homes, but a few are made in workshops.

Persia makes not only Oriental rugs, but tapestries, silk shawls, and attar of roses. There are petroleum refineries at the head of the Persian Gulf, and at Baku.

Camel's hair is woven into a durable cloth, and hides are tanned to make leather.

The heavy cloaks and tents of the nomad tribes of Asia are often made of felt, a fabric familiar to us in hats. To make felt, the wool of sheep and goats is not woven, but merely dampened and pressed till it becomes permanently matted. Some kinds of rugs are made of matted felt. 

Transportation. From time immemorial transportation in Southwestern Asia has been chiefly a matter of caravans, composed of 10 to 1000 camels. Merchants band together for fear of robbers. Though you will find a few railroads on your transportation map of Asia, you will see far longer caravan routes, which follow much the same trails as those used thousands of years ago. These now connect the desert oases and the cities of the interior with the ports served by steamship routes. With your transportation map before you, name the cities from Persia to Palestine that lie on caravan routes. Find those connected by rail. The important railroad from the Bosporus, opposite Istanbul, to Aleppo and Cairo, is being extended to Baghdad and the Persian Gulf. From Aleppo a comparatively long railway line runs to Damascus, and on to Medina.

The small, swift horses of Arabia and elsewhere in this region are used for saddle animals in the grasslands, and sure-footed donkeys in Asia Minor. Donkeys are, of course, not swift, but they are hardier than horses, and can find forage more easily. But camels are the beasts of burden, as well as the chief saddle animals of the deserts.

In Persia, where petroleum, dates, rugs, carpets, and skins are the chief exports, there are few good roads, and the railroads are very short. Goods are usually carried on donkeys or camels; and there are camel paths that

CULTURE AND RELIGION 

183 cross the mountains through the passes that lead to India and Afghanistan. The chief seaports are on the Persian 

Gulf. On account of the difficulty in transporting goods to these ports, most of the rugs and carpets are first sent to Turkey and reshipped. The telegraph systems leading to the Persian Gulf are important for maintaining communication between England and India. A few motor roads were built during the World War, and these are being extended. The number of motor cars used in this region increases each year. Regular airplane service is maintained.

Culture and Religion. The people of southwestern Asia are chiefly white. In religion, they are divided between 

Mohammedans, Christians, and Jews.

These three great religions, Judaism, Christianity, and Mohammedanism, had their origin in this part of Asia. 

They all teach that there is only one God. The believers of all three are now found in many parts of the world. 

The large majority of the people are Mohammedans, or, as they call themselves, Moslems or Mussulmans. The 

Moslems are followers of Mohammed, called the Prophet by Mohammedans, who lived in Arabia about 600 A.D. 

Mohammedan churches are called mosques, and priests called muezzins call the hours for prayer from towers called minarets. The Moslem flag has a new moon (the crescent) and a star. 

Languages. Most of the people, except in Turkey, speak the Arabic language. There are many Jewish merchants in the cities. In Turkey, Syria, and Asia Minor there are good-sized communities of Christians. Negroes, many of them slaves, are to be found in Persia and Arabia. 

Customs. Throughout this region, as well as in other parts of the Orient, private houses are usually two stories high and flat-roofed. They have no windows on the street, but have gardens in the rear. The portion set apart for

the women of Mohammedan households is called the harem, and, excepting members of the family, is visited only by women. The presence of Mohammedan mosques and minarets, the extensive use of brick and adobe, and the absence of wood impress the tourist who visits the cities. of southwestern Asia.

The turban. A special headdress, the turban, is worn by men in Mohammedan countries, though among officials and others in Turkey it has given place to the European hat. 

The turban is usually a sort of scarf that is wound about the head, though in places it is a cap with a tassel and no brim, called a fez. In Oriental countries, the style, color, and material of the turban denotes the rank or profession of the wearer. In India the native princes wear gorgeously colored turbans encrusted with jewels; but the priests wear simple white.

An Ancient People. The history of Asiatic peoples dates back thousands of years before the Christian era. At that time wandering tribes of the people we have come to call Indo-European (people of India and Europe) settled east of the Caspian Sea. Of these, certain of the Aryans chose the 

Iran Plateau, and those on the southern part of the plateau became the Persians. The nomads of the Arabian desert, known as Semites, became the Hebrew people, and after the wanderings described in the Old Testament of the Bible, reached Jerusalem. There were also Sumerians, fairskinned, long-headed people from the mountains northeast of the plain of Babylon, who finally adventured to Europe by sea and became the ancestors of the Greeks, the Romans, the Scots, and the Britons.

Ancient Cities. 

Some of the oldest ruins in the world are to be found in southwestern Asia. Near the coast of Asia

Minor is the site of Troy, the ancient city which was the seat of the great war described by the poet Homer in the 

Iliad, which you may study one of these days.

ANCIENT CITIES 

185 

A Persian mosque. 

The dome and towerlike minaret are characteristic of 

Mohammedan architecture. 

On the plain of Mesopotamia lie the ruins of two of the most wonderful cities of ancient times, famed for their wealth and beauty. They were Nineveh on the Tigris 

River, and Babylon on the Euphrates. Buried for centuries under mounds of earth, in the course of time they were searched out by learned explorers, and they are now being uncovered. Wonderful treasures of the past, statues, jewelry, and pottery, have been recovered. The writings found in the great library of Nineveh were on tablets of baked clay, and their inscriptions have been deciphered. 

The ruins of Babylon, about seventy miles south of Baghdad, are those of a city of about 3000 B.C. 

The mas sive walls, three hundred feet high, were so wide that a four-horse chariot could turn around on the top. The beautiful Hanging Gardens, built on seventy-five foot arches, were one of the Seven Wonders of the ancient world.

Countries. Turn back to your political map of Southwestern Asia, and find: (1) Persia, occupying the greater part of the Plateau of Iran, (2) Arabia, occupying the major part of the Plateau of Arabia, with (3) Iraq (Mesopotamia) in the valleys of the Tigris and Euphrates rivers, (4) tiny Palestine, the "Holy Land," lying sandwiched between less important countries along the Mediterranean coast, its neighbors, (5) Trans-Jordan, and (6) Syria, then (7) Asiatic Turkey, occupying the greater part of Asia Minor, and (8) Transcaucasia, between the Black and Caspian seas. 

One aspect of Persia: when winter whitens the land.

The countries of Southwestern Asia are not colonies of 

European powers, as are the countries of Southeastern Asia.

Nevertheless, the great powers of Europe control all of them to some extent. Turkey, the most independent nation in this part of Asia, is somewhat influenced by Russia, her powerful neighbor who controls the Transcaucasian republics. Persia is important as a buffer state lying between 

Russia and India. Then Arabia, because of her nearness to the British possessions in Africa, is largely under English influence. The other four countries of this region have

PERSIA 

187 actually been placed under European control through the mandate of the League of Nations. Iraq, Palestine, and 

Trans-Jordan are under British control, and Syria is under the protection of the French. 

PERSIA 

Persia is an ancient country. It is a feeble remnant of an extensive empire that at one time, long before the Christian era, was the mightiest realm upon the earth. 

But Persia has lost her pomp and power, together with much of her territory, and is now one of the least important nations of Asia. 

The country consists mainly of grassland, where great herds of camels and horses and even greater flocks of sheep and herds of goats find pasturage. A sandy desert 100 to nearly 800 miles wide spreads from north to south, like a broad band, across the middle of Persia; but the regions east and west of the desert are fertile. The Great Salt

Desert, for nearly 600 square miles, contains rock salt. 

Caravans choose a route between this and the Great Sand Desert. Efficient telegraph service is maintained across the Great Sand Desert. 

Irrigation is needed in most localities for the production of crops. The valleys are watered by the melting of mountain snows. Wheat, barley, rice, and millet are the chief grains. Fruits and vegetables are plentiful, and Persian gardens are noted for the profusion and beauty of their flowers. The roses are particularly celebrated. Persia also produces much silk.

Petroleum is Persia's most valuable export by far. It is shipped via the ports on the Persian Gulf.

TEHERAN, the capital of Persia, at the base of the Elburz Mountains, is an Oriental city of flat-roofed houses. It gets its water supply through underground canals supplied by mountain streams.

TABRIZ is a commercial center of Persia. The exports are raisins and other dried fruits, cotton, Oriental rugs, and petroleum. Tabriz has a noted mosque, and a tower built by the ancient Greeks. The city is connected with the northern border by a railroad.

SHIRAZ is a seat of Persian learning and culture. Here wine, tobacco, and carved wood are manufactured. Near

Shiraz are the ruins of Persepolis, an ancient capital of Persia. Marble columns and huge mounds of broken masonry and rubbish mark the sites of palaces and temples. Persepolis was captured by Alexander the Great.

BUSHIRE, on the Persian Gulf, is one of the chief seaports of Persia.

ARABIA 

Arabia is a vast plateau on a peninsula 1500 miles from north to south, with its greatest height near the Gulf of 

Aden. 

The tableland of Arabia is divided into the Syrian desert in the north, the great southern desert of Arabia, and between the two, a central region that is mountainous. 

Along the Red Sea there is a narrow strip of coastal plain about thirty miles in width, above which the cliffs of the plateau rise sharply, in some places, to a mile in height. At the northern end of the Red Sea is Mt. Sinai, on the Sinai

Peninsula, which is under the control of Egypt. 

It is the mountain region, the Nejd, that contains most of the fertile parts of Arabia. Here, during the rainy season, mountain torrents rush down the valleys, bringing welcome moisture, though they dry up when the rains are over. 

A valley through which such a stream makes its way at certain times of the year, leaving it at other times, is called a wadi. Many wadies are too dry for agriculture. Others are large and contain rich soil. Their slopes are covered with grass and groves of date palms.

ARABIA 189 In winter the westerlies bring a little rain to the Syrian desert. As a result, parts of the desert provide pasturage for a short time in the spring; but in summer, when the thermometer goes to 130 degrees at noon, all forage plants die down to the roots. The monsoon brings the rainy season 

Flocks and herds grazing near Mt. Sinai, just north of the Red Sea. in summer to the mountainous region along the Gulf of Oman, and makes it the most fertile part of Arabia. 

Water is so precious in a desert region that a good well is carefully walled in, and becomes known throughout the country. Some wells in Arabia have been landmarks for hundreds of years.

Arabs. The Arabs are a tall, handsome people, with great physical endurance. Although noted for their occasional cruelty, they are dignified and hospitable. An enemy is safe if he has broken bread with an Arab host. The Arabs are coffee, not tea, drinkers. For centuries there have been

190. 

learned men and writers among the Arabs. It was from 

Arabia that we got our Arabic numerals, 1, 2, 3, and so on.

A great many Arabs live in settlements near the coast of 

Arabia. 

The Bedouins, or desert Arabs, are small and swarthy. 

They are usually unable to read and write, but depend upon their remarkable memories for the history of their tribes. 

As the Bedouins claim the deserts of Arabia, caravans must pay tribute to the tribes whose territory they cross. Some of the Bedouins are villagers; others are nomads who wander from one place to another seeking pasturage for their sheep, goats, and cattle.

The oasis towns, where irrigation is possible, contain most of the population of Arabia. To these towns the Bedouins come, with wool and skins and goat's milk cheeses, in quest of dates and other products of the small areas of croplands. 

There are also Jewish merchants throughout Arabia. Africa, the home of the Negro race, is close by, and although slave trading is contrary to law, some slaves are smuggled into Arabia.

Products. Arabia's fleet horses are justly famous. Only the wealthy Arabs own the magnificent horses for which the country is noted. The average Arabian keeps a camel, a few horses, sheep, and goats. The Bedouins do most of the trading in horses. Hundreds of horses are exported every year, by way of the Persian Gulf.

Some Arabs have herds of humped cattle, but their chief wealth is in the countless flocks of sheep and goats, the milk of which is made into butter and cheese, and the wool clipped. 

The high-grade coffee that has been known for centuries as Mocha originally came from southwestern Arabia, though little is exported nowadays. Myrrh, the gum of an Arabian tree, is useful as a medicine. Gum arabic is obtained from the acacia, a shrub resembling the locust. It is used in making mucilage.

GOVERNMENT 

191 

Loose gowns

A street in Mecca, the home of the Mohammedan religion. and turbans protect the Arabs from the heat of the sun. 

The date palm looms above the landscape in the oases, and, indeed, wherever water can be obtained through rainfall or irrigation. Dates are an important export.

Government. The Arabs are organized into independent tribes. Hedjaz is the most important kingdom, because it contains the two holy cities of the Moslems, Mecca and Medina. Oman is another important kingdom. Aden is a 

British protectorate.

MECCA, the leading city of Arabia, is the center of caravan trails from Damascus and Medina, Egypt, and other 

Mohammedan countries, as well as a railroad terminus.

A line of telegraph has been built along the caravan route to Damascus. Mecca is the birthplace of Mohammed. 

Throughout the world Mohammedans face Mecca when they pray, and each tries to make a pilgrimage to the city.

MEDINA, where Mohammed is buried, is the terminus of a railroad from Damascus, in Syria. 

ADEN, with its seaport of the same name, is a British protectorate bordering on the Gulf of Aden. It is so strongly fortified that it is called the "rock nest" of Aden. Like 

Gibraltar, it is located on a narrow strait. Guarding the southern entrance to the Red Sea, it keeps open the route between England and India through the Suez Canal. From 

Aden, British ships secure coal and take aboard hides and skins after unloading cotton goods. As there is no other source of drinking water in this area, sea water is boiled for drinking purposes.

OMAN 

Oman is an independent Arab state of Southeastern Arabia. It is under British protection. Muscat is the capital. 

Gillians' Service 

A village and date palms of an oasis in the Arabian desert, not far from the Red Sea. Mud bricks or stones set in mortar were the building materials, and only walls without windows face the street. Yet Oriental rugs often cover the floors, on which people sleep without furniture.

IRAQ AND PALESTINE IRAQ 

193 

Iraq, also called Mesopotamia ("between the rivers"), comprises the fertile plains along the Tigris and Euphrates rivers, which unite and flow into the Persian Gulf. 

BAGHDAD, the capital of Iraq, on the Tigris River, is a terminal for both river boats and caravan routes, and has important rail connections as well. 

One of the narrow streets of ancient Baghdad, a city as mysterious as any in The Arabian Nights stories. 

This ancient city was the scene of the tales from the 

Arabian Nights. 

BASRA, although about seventy miles from the Persian Gulf, is a great modern seaport, which will serve as the

terminus for railroad traffic across Europe, Turkey, and Iraq when the railroad from Constantinople is finished. Dates are a leading export of Iraq.

PALESTINE 

Palestine occupies a strip of the Mediterranean coast south of Syria. It was the ancient home of the Hebrew race, and the birthplace of the Christian religion. It is often called the Holy Land. On your map of Palestine, find 

Jerusalem, the Holy City, then locate the areas described below.

Palestine consists chiefly of a coastal plain and inland valley. The valley of the Jordan River lies below sea level, though the stream is fed by springs in the Lebanon Mountains to the north, the home of the cedars of Lebanon men- 

International News Reel 

An olive oil seed press in Palestine.

PALESTINE 

195 tioned in the Bible. The Dead Sea, into which the Jordan flows, has no outlet. Its waters merely evaporate, and are far more salty than the ocean. The Sea of Galilee is also below sea level, though it is sufficiently fresh for certain fish to live in its waters.

Although Palestine was once the land of the Hebrews, most of the people living there today are Moslems and speak Arabic. There are, however, many Jews in Jerusalem and in colonies scattered over the country.

Palestine is governed by Great Britain, which permits the Jewish people to make it again a national home. English, The village well provides the water supply. Nazareth, Palestine. 

Arabic, and Hebrew are all official languages; and complete religious liberty is enjoyed by people of all religions. 

JERUSALEM (city of Peace), is the largest city in Palestine, and the seat of government. It is the Holy City of Jews, Christians, and Mohammedans. It has been captured and destroyed many times, but each time built again. It was finally held by the Moslems for 1000 years before the Christians got it back. Now the English government permits 

Christians, Jews, and Moslems all to live there. 

On the site of Solomon's Temple, built when the ancient

kingdom of the Jews was at its height, the Moslems have built the Mosque of Omar. Jerusalem is noted for its many places of historic and religious interest. 

JAFFA (ancient Joppa), on the coast, serves Jerusalem as a port.

TRANS-JORDAN 

East of the Jordan River is the Arab state of Trans- Jordan, which is under protection of the British. 

SYRIA 

Syria is in the Levant, as the eastern shores of the 

Mediterranean Sea are called. Because of the mountains and Jordan Valley of Palestine and the Syrian desert to the east, the main roads between Africa and western and central Asia have always passed through Syria. It has, therefore, a very mixed history. During the last three thousand years it has belonged to Egyptian, Persian, Roman, Ottoman, and other empires. Syria is now ruled by the French under a mandate of the League of Nations. 

In addition to the animal and vegetable products common to southwestern Asia, Syria produces licorice, which is used in candy, as a medicine, and as a flavoring for tobacco.

DAMASCUS is perhaps the oldest city in the world that is still inhabited. It is not only a holy city to the Moslems, but a thriving commercial city, the Arab trading center of a fertile region, and the starting point of caravans into the desert. Damascus steel is beautifully wrought of alternate layers of iron and steel, welded together to produce a lacelike pattern. Damask, a lustrous table linen, is another manufacture of Damascus. The city is very oriental, with its minarets and its bazaars, or covered streets lined with tiny open shops. There the tourist sees clever workmen making Oriental rugs and hammering brass and copper.

TURKEY 

197 

BEIRUT, on the Mediterranean Sea, is the chief port and seat of government, connected by rail with Damascus. 

ALEPPO, in northern Syria, is as large as Damascus and is of special importance as the place where two great railroad systems meet, forming a letter T. Here the railroad to the south, which joins the road being built north and south clear across Africa, connects with the great railroad running east and west which, when completed between Istanbul and Baghdad, will connect with the railroads of 

Europe. 

TURKEY 

Turkey includes chiefly the plateau of Anatolia. Its 

European possessions have dwindled away before the conquests of Christian nations, but it still desperately keeps a foothold on European soil. Turkey is now actually smaller than the state of Texas.

The plateau of Anatolia is bordered, on both the north and the south, by ranges of mountains near the coasts. The narrow coastal strips, north and south, receive sufficient rainfall for agriculture, and the western end of Anatolia, University of Istanbul 

This is a westernized co-educational institution, the center of the new intellectual life of Turkey.

Near East Foundation 

Harbor Island, near Smyrna, Turkey, where the blue of the sunny sky matches the warm blue of the sea. which is not shut off from the sea by a mountain rim, is well watered and fertile. The mountain slopes are heavily forested, especially on the sides toward the sea. The interior plateau, however, is arid to semi-arid. 

The most eastern part of Turkey, bordering on Persia, is called Kurdistan, the country of the Kurds. The Kurds, most of whom are nomads, have, however, some degree of self-government. Turkey has a constitutional government, somewhat similar to that of most of the republics of Europe. 

ANGORA (Ankara), the capital of Turkey, was founded before the Christian era. Angora is a picturesque town, but has become so modernized as to have electric lights. It is connected with the Mediterranean coast by rail, and exports rugs, figs, and other local products. 

SMYRNA, an ancient city with a harbor on the Aegean coast, is the capital and leading seaport of Asiatic Turkey. 

The exports include figs and raisins, oriental silks, rugs, carpets, and tobacco. Cups of acorns from Turkish oak trees, used in tanning leather, are also shipped from Smyrna in large quantities.

CAUCASUS REPUBLICS 

199 

TRANSCAUCASIA 

Transcaucasia lies between the Caspian and Black seas, and south of the Caucasus Mountains. Mt. Elbrus, 18,493 feet high, is the loftiest peak. Here are a few small republics under Russian control. 

Among these republics are Georgia and Armenia, which are south of the Caucasus Mountains. Azerbaijan extends on both sides of the mountains. There are railroads which connect these republics with the railroads of European 

Russia. 

Azerbaijan has remarkable natural wealth, especially in its petroleum fields. Silk and grain are produced, and cans for shipping oil are manufactured, as are carpets and leather goods.

BAKU, in Azerbaijan, owes its prosperity to the oil wells in the vicinity. Oil is brought to the refineries in Baku by pipe line. Baku has a good harbor, and is a port for freight from Persia and the east shore of the Caspian Sea.

Georgia is an agricultural country. Stockmen have large herds of camels, sheep, goats, and horses. Copper is mined. Silk and honey are produced.

TIFLIS, the capital of Georgia, has been for more than fifteen centuries the chief city between the Black and Caspian seas. Its hot springs attract tourists. BATUM is the principal port of Georgia. 

Armenia is smaller than either Azerbaijan or Georgia.

Like both of them, it is very mountainous. Armenia claims 

Mt. Ararat, which rises partly within its borders. 

The Armenians were converted to Christianity many centuries ago, and have been the object of frequent persecution by the Mohammedan Turks and the Kurds as well.

ERWAN (Erivan) is the capital of Armenia.

PART V AUSTRALIA AND NEW ZEALAND CHAPTER XVI  GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS 

Joined to the northwestern part of Asia is the continent of Europe. Close to southeastern Asia lies another continent, namely, Australia. Though not actually joined to Asia, Australia rises from the same continental shelf, separated from it by Wallace's Line. The East Indies are like giant stepping stones connecting the two continents. 

Location. Australia helps to separate the Pacific Ocean from the Indian Ocean. Your map of the Islands of the

England may go either by the Panama Canal or through the 

Mediterranean Sea and the Suez Canal.

LOCATION 

205 The Tropic of Capricorn crosses about midway of these two continents. Australia does not, however, reach so far north or south as does South America; but Sydney, on the east coast of Australia, is about on a parallel with Buenos 

Aires, on the east coast of South America.

The north coast of the island of Tasmania, which is one of the important states of Australia, bears nearly the same relationship to 40 degrees south latitude that New York 

City does to 40 degrees north latitude.

If, now, you trace the parallels of latitude across the 

Indian Ocean to Africa, what comparison can you make?

Tasmania and the southeastern tip of Australia lie farther south than the southern tip of Africa; yet there is a general correspondence between the location of Australia and that of the southern part of Africa. They lie at the ends of the horseshoe-shaped coastline which encloses the Indian Ocean. 

Australia is the smallest of the continents, smaller, even, than Europe. It is a little less than 3,000,000 square miles in area. How does that compare with the area of the United

States proper? Of Canada? Of Brazil? Australia is the largest island in the world.

Surface. Australia is lower in elevation than the other continents. As you can see by referring to the Relief Map of Australia, page 206, there is a great central plain bordered by a rim of mountains. The principal highlands are nearly parallel to the eastern coast, close to the Pacific Ocean. They extend from fifty to three hundred miles inland. Rising, on the whole, in rounded summits from 4000 to 5000 feet in height, these highlands include a few snow-capped peaks. 

The chief mountain ranges in the principal highlands are the Great Dividing Range in the north, the Blue Mountains, and the Australian Alps in the south. West of the Central Plain there is a secondary highland region with high ridges extending parallel to the western coast of Australia. In the center of the continent, a range of hills rises above the plain marked

on your physical map as the Great Victoria Desert and the Great Sandy Desert. The first settlements were made at 

Sydney and elsewhere along the east coast. The western half of the continent is a low plateau lying about 1000 feet above sea level, until near the western shore, where high ridges parallel the coast. There are also highlands along southern Australia. 

Coast. The shore line of Australia is, on the whole, more regular than that of any other continent, though there are a

Great Barrier Reef. Where there is ocean water of tropic warmth, as there is off the northeast coast of Australia, the tiny coral polyp and other forms of marine life. thrive. Corals cannot live more than about 150 feet below

Then, through countless centuries, the pounding waves pulverize the coral to fine sand, the sand becomes coral-limestone, and islands result. Most live corals are of rainbow 

Part of Australia's Great Barrier Reef, showing coral formations.

hues. When they die, their minute skeletons in countless numbers are deposited in various forms, such as those of trees, with trunks several feet in diameter, and branches that interlace with those of neighboring corals. As the coral colonies grow upward, they form coral reefs. 

Australia has the largest coral reef in the world in Great Barrier Reef, which you will find on your map. Here, at low water, one can see the tops of the coral jungle, made into a solid mass by the bits of broken coral, floating weeds, coconuts, and tree trunks that have piled up.on the living 

Great Barrier Reef would be a menace to the mariner who did not know of its presence; but its pink mass is also a breakwater which acts as a barrier to the force of the waves, and provides a safe inside passage twenty to seventy miles in width for shipping. This reef ranges from ten to ninety miles in breadth, and is fully 1200 miles in length.

Drainage. Australia has few rivers. The Relief Map shows the Murray River system in the eastern lowland. 

The Murray itself, rising in the eastern mountains, where it is fed by melting snows, is a stream more than 1000 miles in length. The main tributary of the Murray, the Darling

River, is likewise over 1000 miles long; and the two streams combined form a waterway of 2300 miles. 

There are no rivers for 1000 miles along the southern coast. The beds of ancient streams are dried and empty. 

The inland drainage basins of Australia cover half the continent. East of the central ridge of hills there are a number of streams which flow into lakes, some of which are below the level of the sea. One eighty miles long has no outlet, and is extremely salty. The basins west of the central ridge are almost waterless. Under the southern part of this region lies the largest artesian basin in the world. To tap it, artesian wells are driven, a few nearly a mile deep.

Climate. Australia is about as far south of the Equator as Mexico is north of it. The northern part of Australia is in the Torrid Zone and has a climate like that of southern Mexico and Central America. The southern part is in the 

South Temperate Zone, and has a climate like that of northern Mexico and the southern part of the United States. The mild climate is made more temperate by the surrounding ocean, so that northern Australia, though in the Torrid

Zone, has a subtropical climate. Northern Australia has rain from the trade winds; the southern part has the westerlies of the Southern Hemisphere, but over 40 per cent of the continent is made arid by the trade winds. 

The Australian summer lasts from December to March. 

Under the direct rays of the sun, the unshaded interior then becomes extremely hot, and a monsoon blows in from the ocean north of Australia, bearing rain. This falls chiefly upon the north and east coasts, flooding the rivers

Mean annual rainfall in Australia. and lakes. Indeed, the eastern coast has really heavy rainfall, and about 20 per cent of Australia is farming country. 

The interior is a stony and sandy desert, because there are no high mountains to condense the moisture that is left in the winds after they have crossed the coast ranges. 

In southwestern Australia the seasons resemble those of southern California. The winter is the rainy season; the summer is dry.

Compare the maps showing the rainfall, mountains, and temperature, winds, and ocean currents of Australia. 

With these climatic factors in mind, see if you can decide why there is heavy rainfall along the eastern coast, and why there is a great interior desert. Remember that Australia, lying south of the equator, has summer at Christmas time, and winter when we are celebrating the Fourth of July. 

Strange Plants and Animals. Australia has several animals found nowhere else. They possess a remarkable adaptation to the conditions existing in Australia. Of the strange creatures that appear to be survivals of ancient

STRANGE PLANTS AND ANIMALS 

211 types of animal life, one is the platypus or duck mole, a small aquatic mammal that looks a good deal like a bird, and lays eggs. You may have seen a museum specimen of this strange creature. Another primitive type of animal is the marsupial, of which the kangaroo is the most familiar member. There is also a smaller marsupial called the wallaby. Both the wallaby and the kangaroo carry their helpless young in the furry pockets on their under sides, as do our American opossums. They hop on powerful hind legs, and have tails sufficiently sturdy to rest part of their weight upon them when sitting up.

Other marsupials are the wombat, a thick furred little climbing bear that burrows like a badger, and a ratlike creature called the bandicoot.

Australia has only one native animal of the types seen on other continents, a wild dog called the dingo that has proven a menace to sheep and lambs. No animals of the cat tribe, no monkeys, and no animal that chews the cud is found wild in Australia. 

Of Australian birds, the strangest is the kookaburra, whose notes have nicknamed it the laughing jackass.

There is an emu or Australian ostrich, and a cassowary, both of which have become adapted to running, since they The tree ferns characteristic of the wet parts of Australia.

The kookaburra, an Australian bird that offers such merry notes that it is called the "laughing jackass." must travel long distances in treeless regions to find food. 

There is also a black swan, a lyre bird, so named from its tail plumages, and a bower bird that builds lovely bowerlike nests. There are parrakeets, parrots, and cockatoos, and in treeless regions there are mound-making turkeys. 

Many of the plants and trees found in the drier parts of Australia have thick, tough leaves that retain the moisture drawn from their roots, instead of presenting broad surfaces for evaporation, as do trees like our maples. There is a hard, prickly porcupine grass which could not be eaten even by a goat. The eucalyptus is perhaps the most distinctive tree in Australia. It sheds its bark in long strips, but does not drop its narrow leaves. The Australian "bush" or scrubby forest of the wild lands is made up largely of myrtles and stunted eucalyptus or gum trees, the aroma of which is pungent and delightful. On the well-watered plains of southeastern Australia, though, several native varieties of the eucalyptus grow to as great a

STRANGE PLANTS AND ANIMALS 

213 

height as the "Big Trees" of California. The eucalyptus has been introduced into many warm parts of the world, especially in unhealthful swampy places, as the roots drain the soil and render the locality less liable to mosquitoes and 

Our Cali- 

A forest of the eucalyptus trees in Australia. fornia eucalyptus was introduced from Australia. malaria. From the leaves an antiseptic oil is obtained, while the gum or resin of the eucalyptus is used for medicine.

There is also the jarrah tree, of whose wood strong paving blocks are made.

In northern and eastern Australia there are tropical forests containing tree ferns, orchids, and flowering acacias. 

Other Resources. Australia rivals south Africa and 

Alaska as a gold region. Silver, copper, tin, and coal are

mined also. The dry interior has vast ranges of grass and low bushes, which provide pasturage for sheep.

CHAPTER XVII LIFE BENEATH THE SOUTHERN CROSS EFFECTS OF GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS 

We have been considering the geographical controls of location, surface, climate, and other resources. We have found that while Australia has heavy rainfall along the eastern coast, there is a great interior desert. Further map study shows that the northern part of the continent is subtropical, but separated from the rest of the continent by the desert and the rim of mountains in the east. Where do we expect to find the best agricultural areas or the heaviest forests? 

Where would we find possible mineral wealth and where, semiarid grasslands? To what use may these grasslands be put? The answers to these problems follow. 

Sheep and Cattle Industry. When the first settlers came to Australia from England, to settle along the east coast, they

LIFE BENEATH THE SOUTHERN CROSS 

brought sheep and cattle with them; and today Australia is the world's leading country in the production of sheep's wool. Its yearly average is 900,000,000 pounds. trast that with the 320,000,000 pounds or so produced by Con- 

200 400 600 

100,000 sheep

Distribution of sheep, chiefly wool bearers, of Australia. the United States, and the 115,000,000 grown by Great Britain. Australia not only supplies quantities of the wool required for Great Britain's mills, but exports lamb and mutton, though not a quarter as much of the latter as does New Zealand. One reason why Australia specializes in wool rather than in mutton is that much of the sheep range lies remote from railroads and steamship lines. Wool can be hauled by freight wagon, whereas meat would require refrigeration. 

Sheep. As on our own western plains, there are vast areas in Australia too dry for crops, yet not too arid for nourishing grasses, nor too hot for wool-bearing animals. 

This is especially true of the great plains of the interior, and along the western coast. About half of the sheep

SHEEP AND CATTLE INDUSTRY 

217 of the continent, however, are herded on the western slopes of the highlands of New South Wales.

The mild climate of Australia permits the sheep to remain on the open range all winter. Some of the sheep stations, as the ranches are called, are enormous. They have to be fenced against the rabbit pest, besides which, growths of cactus have to be kept from spreading through the grasslands. The wolflike dingoes will kill sheep if 

Shearing Australian sheep with electric clippers. they get the chance, and in many places it is a problem to supply the sheep with drinking water in a dry season. 

At such times, also, the grass may dry up, and even the wells fail. These conditions combine to make sheep raising in Australia extremely hazardous, though many men have grown wealthy there in the sheep and cattle industry.

LIFE BENEATH THE SOUTHERN CROSS 

An Ayrshire dairy herd on the south coast of New South Wales. 

The sheep are of many varieties, including hardy Border Leicesters, Cheviots, and fine Merinos. They are sheared by crews of men who journey from one sheep station to another from July to November, removing the fleeces with electric clippers with almost incredible rapidity, so that one man can clip 100 sheep a day. The fleeces are then graded, and baled, and transported to the railroads, sometimes hundreds of miles over poor roads, in huge freight wagons to which several dozen bullocks are harnessed, four abreast.

The drivers who handle these extraordinary teams are powerful fellows called bullockies. Once at port, the baled fleeces fill many a warehouse till the ships arrive that are to take them to England.

Cattle. While cattle do not thrive on lands as dry as sheep range, they can be reared in climates too warm for sheep, and Australia has both beef and dairy cattle,

AGRICULTURE 

219 Queensland is the leading state for beef cattle, though New South Wales also has considerable numbers. The meat is frozen for export to England, and the hides are tanned. There are some large cattle ranches along the east coast of Australia, as in the northern plains region, and on the seaward slopes of the eastern highlands. Where railroads are lacking, the cattle have to be driven over so-called stock routes, along which drinking water may be obtained at tanks or artesian wells. 

Agriculture. Southeastern Australia is one of the great wheat regions of the world. This area, especially the western slope of the eastern highlands, Queensland, New South Wales, and Victoria, produces nearly as much wheat as Argentina, in South America, and, roughly, half as much as Canada. There are all the necessary conditions, fertile plains, moderate rainfall during the growing season, coupled A wheat field of southeastern Australia, where the grassy plains produce enormous crops. Though only a limited portion of the island continent has sufficient rainfall for this grain, Australia produces a huge wheat crop.

LIFE BENEATH THE SOUTHERN CROSS 

with a dry harvest season. Here, as in other wheat regions, a small number of men operate large machines. 

In Australia, nearly the same amount of wheat per acre is produced as in the United States. Wheat is therefore exported on a large scale. The average yield is well over 

100,000,000 bushels per year. Fortunately for the remote

All along the coastal plain of New South Wales, potatoes, oats, hay, and corn are grown in sufficient quantities to supply local needs, and there are truck farms near the large cities.

In the warm lowlands of Queensland, sugar cane, pineapples, and bananas are grown. On the hillsides, wine and raisin grapes, olives, oranges, peaches, and other fruits ripen in the hot sunshine. The lower part of the 

Murray River valley supplies quantities of fruit, especially

IRRIGATION 

221 in the form of preserves, to England. The island of Tasmania, where the winters are cooler, grows such fine apples that it is called the Orchard State. 

Mixed farming is possible in a wide belt along the eastern and southeastern coast of Australia, and in the extreme southwestern tip of the continent; but the rainfall usually

Distribution of the wheat-growing areas of Australia. amounts to less than twenty inches, and irrigation is necessary west of the eastern highlands, all the way from 

Queensland to Victoria. 

In the dry interior of the continent, and along much of the eastern coast, it is too dry for crops, nor is there any means of irrigating most of this region. 

Though there is heavy rainfall in the tropical lowland around the Gulf of Carpentaria, as well as an abundance of rivers, this region has been so remote from the settled lands along the east coast and elsewhere, and, withal, so inaccessible, that it is still almost uninhabited, save by the non-agricultural native race.

LIFE BENEATH THE SOUTHERN CROSS 

Irrigation. Though there are few rivers in Australia, irrigation is possible in certain areas because of underground waters which can be brought to the irrigation ditches by means of artesian wells. Queensland, New South Wales, and Victoria have hundreds of artesian wells. 

Two and a half tons of rabbits ready for shipment. 

New South Wales also has a huge irrigation project which was made possible by the damming of a branch of the Murray River system. This one project alone will extend the croplands by 1,500,000 acres. The dam holds back the river water at the time when it is highest after the rains.

Rabbit Pests. There were no rabbits in Australia till some Englishman brought a pair and turned them free.

There are now so many rabbits running wild that hungry hordes of them would devour crops and pasture if permitted to do so. Sheepmen and cattlemen have therefore put up thousands upon thousands of fine meshed wire fences to

MINING 

223 protect their ranges, and the quickest way to become unpopular in Australia is to go about the plains leaving other people's gates open. One of the most valuable men on a sheep ranch is the boundary rider, who goes about repairing breaks in the rabbit fences and getting rid of as many members of the rabbit pest as he can. One difficulty has been that if he were to put out poison for the rabbits, the sheep might get it. The fences add to the cost of sheep-raising, but 

Australians have found that they can export frozen rabbit meat, and make cloth and felt hats of rabbit fur, or export the rabbit skins for that purpose. 

Mining. Gold was discovered in Australia just two years after the boom days of '49 in California. That started a gold rush to the eastern highlands. Today 

Australia's output of gold is of considerably less value than the wool produced. Gold is now mined chiefly in western 

Gold and copper mines at Mt. Morgan, Australia.

Australia. Here the plateau is so dry that water has to be pumped to the mines from the west coast, several hundred miles distant. The railroad that crosses the continent from east to west carries the gold from Kalgoorlie, one of the most important mining centers of Australia, to the port of Perth.

LIFE BENEATH THE SOUTHERN CROSS 

Australia also has some silver, though it is of a great deal less value to her than her coal. New South Wales is the great coal-producing state, and the best deposits found have been located in the highlands back of Sydney. While 

Australia produces somewhat less coal than Canada, and only a small fraction of the amount produced in the mother country, it provides power for the few factories that handle 

Australia's raw materials. 

Lead and zinc are mined to some extent, especially in New South Wales. Of other minerals, there is a little copper and iron ore in Australia, and Tasmania has deposits of tin.

People. Australia has the smallest total population of any continent. The figures you should look up for yourselves by way of learning to use the Appendix. The population is unevenly distributed, as may be seen by the map. 

Most of the people live in the southeastern part. Here the temperate climate, combined with sufficient rainfall for agriculture, or at least a possibility of irrigation, and the

Loading wheat in New South Wales, Australia. presence of good harbors, have made it easier for white people to secure a livelihood. Along other parts of the coast, and in the grasslands, the population is exceedingly sparse. In the desert of Central Australia, there is an average of only one person to each 140 square miles of area. In Victoria, there are about twenty persons to the square mile. Most of the cities are in southeastern Australia. Nearly half the population of Australia lives in one of the six capital cities, Melbourne, Sydney, Brisbane, Adelaide, Perth, or Hobart.

Inspection of the population map will show that there is a vast desert waste in the interior where no one lives, though it is crossed by camel caravans and one great railway that joins South Australia to Pine Creek in North 

Australia. 

So eager is Australia for white immigrants that the Commonwealth has been paying at least part of the traveling expenses of desirable settlers, besides securing at least temporary employment for them on their arrival in the country.

LIFE BENEATH THE SOUTHERN CROSS 

Most Australians are descended from British colonists. 

Although isolated from the rest of the British Empire and far from the seat of government at London, the Australians are patriotic and loyal Englishmen. They are very fond of outdoor pastimes, from water sports to mountain climbing. 

There are also thousands of Chinese in Australia. These are not especially welcome, for they live cheaply, and will work for lower wages than white men, and Australia has many laws that favor the laboring man. 

There is also a small Mohammedan colony of Afghans in central Australia. They came originally to guide the camels that were used to transport railway ties and the food and water required by the workmen when the railway was built across the desert. 

Bushmen. Finally, there are still some 50,000 to 100,000 savages in Australia. When the first white people came, they found a native race of aborigines or original inhabitants whom they called blackfellows. These savages appear to be like no other native race on earth today. They are possessed of some Negro characteristics, and some like those of Caucasian peoples.

Those who live in the warmer parts of the continent go nearly naked, and build only such bare shelters as will shed the rains, while those in the cooler south have skin rugs for winter use. These aborigines engage in no activities save fighting, hunting, and fishing. They will eat even snakes and lizards. They use spears, stone hatchets, and boomerangs. The boomerang is a crescent-shaped piece of wood about a yard long which, when hurled in a certain way, will return to the thrower.

The blackfellows are now to be found chiefly on reservations or in the wildest, least-known corners of the continent. 

They appear to be dying out.

IS. inter-continental railroad crosses 1000 miles of arid waste, where there is no water. Follow its course on your map. 

The camel, introduced from Asia when the transcontinental railroad was being built, remains in the sun-baked silences of central Australia, and is today an important means of transport across the waterless desert. Large caravans are used to transport wool across the sands to the railroad where neither horse nor motor car could make its way.

Commerce. Trade is largely confined to England and other parts of the British Empire. The chief exports are wool and hides, meat and dairy products, wheat, and gold, while the chief imports are clothing, tea, and machinery. 

Australia is one of England's chief sources of meat. Large cargoes of frozen beef, mutton, and rabbits are shipped to the mother country. The surplus butter also is sent there. 

From Sydney and other Australian ports, there are direct steamship lines to New Zealand, and westward across the

LIFE BENEATH THE SOUTHERN CROSS 

Night on the harbor at Sydney, Australia, where electricity is abundant. 

Pacific Ocean, via Honolulu, Hawaii, to the west coast ports of North America. There are lines from the Australian ports northeastward, via Singapore, also southward to

India; and from Bombay these lines continue, via the Suez 

Canal, to ports of the British Isles. There are also steamship connections with other parts of the world. 

Government. The Commonwealth of Australia, including 

Tasmania, was, until 1926, a mere self-governing colony of Great Britain, but is now, like Canada, practically independent. A member of the British Commonwealth of Nations, it is really a federation of six states and one territory, and has a Constitution much like our own. The states have home rule. The chief official is the governorgeneral, who represents the King of England and is appointed by him.

The duties of the states are unlimited. The government operates the railroads, the telephone, and the telegraph, digs artesian wells, and builds pipe lines. It maintains cattle trails, erects fences, and carries on many forms of business that in other countries are left to private enterprise.

CITIES 

229 

The Commonwealth controls part of the neighboring island of Papua or New Guinea, the largest island in the world except Australia and Greenland. This island is, however, largely unexplored jungle inhabited by black tribes called Papuans. 

Cities. The six leading cities of Australia all export the products of the neighboring regions, whether wool, meat, butter, fruit, or gold.

SYDNEY, the capital of New South Wales, is the oldest city in Australia, and the largest today. It has a beautiful rock-bottomed harbor and a busy one, the modern warehouses are enormous, and the streets wide and clean.

MELBOURNE, the capital of Victoria, likewise has an excellent harbor. It manufactures farm machinery. At its Town Hall, free organ concerts are given.

HOBART, the capital of Tasmania, has a fine natural 

Melbourne, Australia, showing Princes Bridge over the Yarra River.

harbor on a bay sixty miles long, around the hilly shores of which the dwellings of the city are scattered. 

BRISBANE, the capital and port city of Queensland, drawing upon the grazing land of the region, has woolen mills and meat-packing plants.

ADELAIDE, the capital of South Australia, on a river flowing into a deep ocean bay, is also a port city. 

PERTH, the capital of Western Australia, is several miles inland, but has a port at Fremantle. Ships that come from England by the Suez Canal stop at Fremantle. 

CANBERRA, the capital of Australia, was planned and built in a model manner on an area selected for the Federal Territory, located midway between Sydney and Melbourne.

CHAPTER XVIII THE WINDY ISLANDS GEOGRAPHICAL CONTROLS AND THEIR EFFECTS Location and Physical Features. Find New Zealand, over 1000 miles southeast of Australia, on your map of the Islands of the Pacific Ocean. Between what parallels of latitude do the two chief islands of New Zealand lie, and how does their location correspond to that of Tasmania? What sort of climate would you therefore expect these islands to possess?

They have been called the Windy Islands because of the sea breezes, the prevailing westerlies, that blow constantly, moderating the climate and bringing abundant moisture. The temperature of New Zealand usually ranges from about 51 to 66 degrees. The city of Auckland has about the same amount of rainfall as New York City, while the mountain slopes receive far more precipitation. 

No part of these Pacific isles is farther than sixty miles from the sea.

New Zealand's great geographical handicap is her remote location, for she is several days' sail from Australia, and over 5000 miles from any other large body of land save that of the Antarctic region.

THE WINDY ISLANDS 

Courtesy Watson Line 

The harbor at Wellington, New Zealand. Located on the southern coast of North Island, the harbor is spacious and well equipped. It lies 6000 miles from San Francisco. 

Zealand occupy an area more than 1000 miles in length and up to 180 miles in width. North Island and South 

Island, which are connected by ferry steamers, are each, roughly, as large as New York State. They are separated by Cook Strait, named in honor of the famous navigator who, in his exploration of the Pacific Ocean, visited these shores, as well as those of Australia, beginning about 1769. 

Both islands are mountainous, and subject to earthquakes. They also have fertile valleys, plains, and plateaus. 

Though the rivers are short, some of them are deep enough for navigation. There are few wild animals, though deer and elk are found in the mountains, and rabbits have to be kept off the sheep ranges by wire fences. There are no snakes on the island, but a ferocious bird, the kea, attacks the sheep, clinging and biting into their backs, which results in the death of the sheep.

The islands of Samoa in the western part of this archipelago are under the control of New Zealand. Upon one, a monument marks the grave of Robert Louis Stevenson,

GOVERNMENT AND PEOPLE 

237 the author of Treasure Island. The other islands of this group are controlled by the United States. 

Government and People. New Zealand is a dominion of the British Empire, with almost entire self-government, though the governor-general is appointed by the King of 

England. The active head is a premier or prime minister. 

Most of the people are uncommonly self-reliant and progressive. They came as immigrants from the British 

Isles and from Australia, or are descended from such immigrants. But there are only about 1,500,000 people all told, including some 20,000 natives. 

The Maoris, or natives of New Zealand, are bold sailors and skillful farmers, who probably came from Samoa, Wide World Photo 

Maori women cooking in a hot spring of the thermal district of New Zealand. traveling the 1600 miles by canoe. They are a high type of brown people, Polynesians, and are advancing rapidly. 

They are received socially, and even elect a few native members to the New Zealand House of Representatives.

THE WINDY ISLANDS Natural Resources. New Zealand has fertile dairy and fruit farms and rich pasture land; its mountains are forested with pines and hardwoods; and its mines contain rich veins of gold, in addition to enough coal for local needs.

The fine flocks of sheep provide the most important source of wealth in wool, mutton, and skins that go to England. 

Oroc Photo 

A flock of New Zealand sheep destined for the dinner tables of 

England. This is at Hawke's Bay. 

Dairying is also important, and the islands export butter and cheese to the mother country.

The Kauri pine is New Zealand's most valuable tree, not alone because of its fine, straight timber, which is used in boat building, but because of its fossil resin, Kauri gum, which is used in making varnish and linoleum.

Sportsmen enjoy the deep-sea fishing off the coast of New Zealand, where there are countless sharks and swordfish, among other denizens of the deep.

The New Zealand "bush " ranges from palms to evergreens. Unfortunately, the early settlers destroyed large tracts of the country's giant evergreens. Important watersheds are now being reforested, but it takes several generations for the native trees to reach maturity.

SCENERY 

239 

Beautiful Scenery. Were New Zealand not so remote, it would be even more of a favorite with tourists than it now is, for its scenery is wild and lovely. Wooded hills rise abruptly from the blue-green fiords, sea birds call, and the surf booms musically against the cliffs. The clear mountain streams leap and foam along their torrential courses. Tree ferns, some of them sixty feet in height, cast their lazy shadows across the forest trails. 

Lake Wakatipu, one of the many clear blue lakes that lie cupped in the mountains of South Island, New Zealand. 

North Island has a so-called thermal district, a one-time Maori stronghold, where geysers whistle and hot springs steam. Here there are numerous volcanoes. One active volcano is Mt. Ngauruhoe, 7515 feet high. In the Bay of Plenty, off the neighboring coast, there is a bed of hot sulphur called White Island.

South Island is famed for the scenery of the snow-capped Alps along its western shore. The highest of these, Mt. 

Cook, towers skyward for over 12,000 feet (12,349)., Icy mountain lakes are fed by the Tasman glacier, which de-

THE WINDY ISLANDS 

scends nearly to the sea. The glacier was named for Abel 

Tasman, a Dutch sea captain, who discovered the islands in 1642. His name was also given to Tasmania.

A Land of Modern Ideas. New Zealand, remote from the rest of the civilized world, and possessed of a stimulating climate, has been in a peculiarly favorable position to try out new ideas. Of even greater importance in this connection is the fact that its people are all of a high type and, so far as white population is concerned, of the same race. These islands have therefore led the way in many types of legislation, some of which have since been adopted elsewhere, others of which might not work out so well in other countries.

New Zealand preceded the United States by many years in the granting of equal suffrage to women, and in the establishment of an income tax. For forty years New 

Zealand has been teaching agriculture to the unemployed. 

It has made loans to settlers; during the World War it received British schoolboys by groups and taught them to farm; and after the war it aided returned soldiers who wished to become farmers. The government taxes privately owned land, but not the improvements on it, and owns much land itself, which it permits people to use on long-term leases.

There is advanced labor legislation, such as the minimum wage, and compulsory arbitration of labor disputes, a term of which you will have a better understanding if you consult the dictionary. There are coöperative marketing associations for the benefit of those who produce meat and dairy products.

There is a good deal of social legislation, such as old age pensions for every person not an alien or an Asiatic who fulfills certain conditions. There are child welfare institutions, and a system of so-called family allowances for each child after the second, in cases where a family income

CITIES 

241 falls below a certain amount; for in a country larger than 

England, the total population of which amounts to only a fraction of that of London, a larger population is greatly desired. There is government life-insurance and fireinsurance, as well as insurance by private companies. 

NEW ZEALAND SCRANICE OLTO SOUTH BRITISH PAR TRUST EXECUTORS BOMEN CLARS 

The principal business street of Auckland, New Zealand.

Galloway 

As for public utilities, the government owns most of the railroads and some of the coal mines. It also controls a system by which the available water power can be made to supply the entire country with hydroelectric power. 

The free schools include a dozen for the Maoris. There are schools of mines, an excellent agricultural college, and the University of New Zealand with four colleges. 

AUCKLAND, with a population of just over 200,000, is the largest city of New Zealand. As the map shows, it has two fine harbors. There is ship-building and sugar refining, among other manufactures. 

WELLINGTON, the capital city, which is centrally located, as the map of New Zealand shows, is built around a deep natural harbor on the south coast of North Island.

THE WINDY ISLANDS Questions 1. What are New Zealand's nearest neighboring coun tries? 

2. What are the leading natural resources of New Zea land? 

3. If you were a settler in New Zealand, in what ways might you earn a living? 

4. What are some of the modern ideas that have been worked out in New Zealand?

Problems A. Estimate, on your map or globe, the approximate distance from Liverpool to the leading port of New Zealand by the route probably taken by colonists. 

B. Why do you think the English-speaking people of

New Zealand have been able to work out the modern ideas described? (Race, climate, resources, isolation.)

Watch the papers for news of New Zealand, and see if you can interpret such news in terms of geography.

REVIEW Review Questions on Asia 

243 

1. As you study your various maps of Asia, what great natural boundaries do you find dividing the various countries? 

2. What ancient artificial boundary still remains? 

What do you see in the news about it? 

3. Where do you find an outstanding lack of natural boundaries between Asiatic countries? 

4. What Asiatic countries have such fertile soil, coupled with such favorable climates and such favorable amounts of rainfall in the growing seasons that dense populations are able to earn their livings? 

5. In what parts of Asia are conditions so unfavorable to agriculture that but few people are able to live in these areas? 

6. What Asiatic country has too small an area to enable its people to grow much of their own food, and how do those people solve the problem of securing their needs from other countries? 

7. What Asiatic countries have climates too hot for much human exertion? 

8. What Asiatic countries are too cold, much of the year, for human comfort? 

9. What Asiatic country has a stimulating climate? 

10. What countries of Asia have navigable waterways? 

What other modes of transportation exist in Asia, and where? 

11. Where are Mongol peoples found, where are there white people in Asia, and where the brown race? 

12. How do these three kinds of people differ in racial temperament? 

13. What peoples of Asia have a well-developed civilization, and what Asiatic people are least civilized? To what extent are the reasons for these differences to be found in the climates and other geographical aspects of the countries in which these people live? 

14. What differences appear to be due to race (or racial heredity)? 

15. In what countries have there been outstanding individuals, and what did these individuals achieve?
